    Beginner 280 questions

    C# language fundamentals: types, strings, collections, LINQ, exceptions, tooling and the basics of the runtime.

    1. What is the difference between a value type and a reference type in C#?

    A value type stores its data directly where the variable lives, so assignment copies the whole value. A reference type stores a reference on the stack or inside another object, and the actual object lives on the heap, so assignment copies only the reference. Structs, enums and the built-in numeric types are value types, while classes, arrays, delegates and strings are reference types.

    int a = 10;
int b = a;   // copy
b = 20;      // a is still 10

var list1 = new List<int> { 1 };
var list2 = list1;   // same object
list2.Add(2);        // list1.Count == 2

    2. Which built-in C# types are value types and which are reference types?

    All numeric types (int, long, double, decimal, byte, etc.), bool, char, DateTime, TimeSpan, enums and structs are value types. string, object, arrays, classes, interfaces and delegates are reference types. Note that string looks like a value type because of its immutability, but it is a reference type.

    3. Why is string immutable in .NET and what does that imply?

    Once a string instance is created its contents can never change; every "modification" such as Replace or += creates a new string. This makes strings safe to share across threads and allows the runtime to cache hash codes and intern literals. The practical consequence is that repeated concatenation in a loop allocates many temporary objects.

    4. When should you use StringBuilder instead of string concatenation?

    Use StringBuilder when you build a string from many pieces, especially inside a loop or when the final size is unknown, because it mutates an internal buffer instead of allocating a new string each time. For a handful of concatenations the compiler-generated String.Concat is faster and clearer. Call ToString once at the end.

    var sb = new StringBuilder();
for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++)
{
    sb.Append(i).Append(',');
}
string result = sb.ToString();

    5. What is the difference between an array and a List<T>?

    An array has a fixed length decided at creation and cannot grow, while List<T> wraps an array and resizes itself by allocating a larger buffer when capacity is exceeded. Arrays are slightly faster and allocation-friendly for fixed-size data; lists are the default choice when the count changes. Both allow index access and are reference types.

    6. How do you declare and initialize a jagged array versus a multidimensional array?

    A multidimensional array uses a single block with comma-separated indices, while a jagged array is an array of arrays where each inner array can have a different length. Access syntax differs: matrix[1, 2] versus jagged[1][2]. Jagged arrays are usually more flexible and sometimes faster for uneven data.

    int[,] grid = new int[2, 3];
int[][] jagged = new int[2][];
jagged[0] = new[] { 1, 2 };
jagged[1] = new[] { 3, 4, 5 };

    7. What does the params keyword do in a method signature?

    params lets the caller pass a variable number of arguments of the specified type without explicitly creating an array. The compiler packs the arguments into an array, and the parameter must be the last one in the signature. It is convenient for logging or formatting helpers but allocates an array on each call.

    static int Sum(params int[] values)
{
    int total = 0;
    foreach (var v in values) total += v;
    return total;
}

int x = Sum(1, 2, 3);

    8. How do optional and named arguments work together?

    An optional parameter declares a default value and may be omitted by the caller; optional parameters must come after all required ones. Named arguments let you pass a value by parameter name in any order, which improves readability when several parameters share a type. You can combine both, for example skipping one optional argument while supplying a later one.

    void Log(string message, string level = "INFO", bool console = true) { }

Log("started");
Log("failed", console: false);

    9. What is the difference between ref, out and in parameters?

    ref requires the variable to be initialized before the call and allows the method to read and write it. out requires no prior initialization but the method must assign it before returning, which suits TryParse-style APIs. in passes by reference for performance but forbids modification, so it is used for large structs that should not be copied.

    static void Increment(ref int value) => value++;

static bool TryHalf(int input, out int half)
{
    half = input / 2;
    return input % 2 == 0;
}

    10. What is a nullable value type and how is it represented?

    Nullable<T>, written T?, wraps a value type so it can also represent the absence of a value. It is a struct with two members, HasValue and Value, and Value throws InvalidOperationException when HasValue is false. This is how a database NULL is mapped onto types like int or DateTime.

    int? count = null;
if (count.HasValue)
    Console.WriteLine(count.Value);

int safe = count ?? 0;   // null-coalescing

    11. How do you safely read the value of a nullable type?

    Check HasValue before touching Value, use GetValueOrDefault to fall back to default(T), or use the null-coalescing operator to supply an alternative. Pattern matching with "is int value" is also safe because it only succeeds when a value exists. Never access Value directly on a null nullable.

    12. What is the difference between an enum and an enum with the [Flags] attribute?

    A plain enum represents exactly one named constant, whereas a flags enum is designed to combine several values with bitwise OR. For flags you assign powers of two so each bit is unique, and you mark the type with [Flags] so ToString prints combined names. Membership is then tested with HasFlag or a bitwise AND.

    [Flags]
enum Permissions { None = 0, Read = 1, Write = 2, Delete = 4 }

var p = Permissions.Read | Permissions.Write;
bool canWrite = p.HasFlag(Permissions.Write);

    13. When would you choose a struct over a class?

    Choose a struct for small, immutable data that logically behaves like a single value, such as a coordinate or a money amount, because it avoids heap allocation and garbage collection. Keep it under roughly 16-24 bytes, since larger structs get copied expensively on every assignment or argument pass. If you need inheritance, reference semantics or identity, use a class.

    14. What are the main differences between structs and classes in C#?

    Structs are value types stored inline and copied on assignment, classes are reference types stored on the heap. Structs cannot inherit from another struct or class (they implicitly derive from ValueType) and cannot have a parameterless constructor with custom logic in older language versions. Structs also cannot be null unless wrapped in Nullable<T>.

    15. What does a static constructor do and when does it run?

    A static constructor initializes static fields and runs automatically at most once, before the first static member access or the first instance creation. It cannot be called directly and takes no parameters or access modifiers. If it throws, the type becomes unusable for the rest of the application domain.

    class Config
{
    public static readonly string Root;
    static Config() => Root = AppContext.BaseDirectory;
}

    16. What is constructor chaining with this() and base()?

    this(...) calls another constructor in the same class so shared initialization lives in one place, while base(...) forwards arguments to a base class constructor. Both must appear in the constructor initializer before the body runs. If you omit base(...), the compiler inserts a call to the parameterless base constructor.

    class Person
{
    public string Name { get; }
    public Person() : this("unknown") { }
    public Person(string name) => Name = name;
}

    17. What is the difference between a field and a property?

    A field is a raw storage location exposed directly, while a property is a member with get and/or set accessors that can add validation, lazy loading, logging or computed logic. Properties are the public API convention because you can change their implementation without breaking callers. Auto-properties let the compiler generate the backing field for you.

    private int _age;
public int Age
{
    get => _age;
    set => _age = value < 0 ? 0 : value;
}

    18. How do you create and use an indexer?

    An indexer is a property named this with parameters in brackets, letting instances be accessed with array-like syntax. It can have any parameter type and can be overloaded, and it may be read-only or read-write. Indexers are common on collection wrappers.

    class Bag
{
    private readonly List<string> _items = new();
    public string this[int i] => _items[i];
    public void Add(string s) => _items.Add(s);
}

    19. What do the access modifiers public, private, protected, internal and protected internal mean?

    public is visible everywhere, private only inside the declaring type, protected to derived types, and internal only within the same assembly. protected internal is the union of protected and internal, so either condition grants access. private protected, newer and rarer, requires both derived and same assembly.

    20. What is the difference between const, readonly and static readonly?

    const is a compile-time constant baked into every consuming assembly and limited to primitives, string and null. readonly is a runtime field that can be assigned only in the declaration or a constructor, so each instance may differ. static readonly combines both ideas: one value per type, assigned at runtime, which suits values from configuration or DateTime.Now.

    21. What is the difference between var, object and dynamic?

    var is compile-time type inference: the variable is strongly typed and the compiler knows its exact type. object is a static type that forces boxing of value types and requires casts to use members. dynamic defers binding to runtime, so mistakes become runtime exceptions rather than compile errors and IntelliSense is lost.

    var name = "Ann";        // string
object boxed = 42;       // needs unboxing
dynamic d = 42;
d = "now a string";      // allowed, checked at runtime

    22. What is the difference between implicit and explicit conversion?

    An implicit conversion happens automatically because it is always safe, such as int to long or a derived class to its base. An explicit conversion (a cast) is required when data could be lost or the conversion could fail, such as double to int. Custom types can define both with implicit and explicit operator methods.

    23. What is the difference between the is and as operators?

    is tests compatibility and returns a bool without throwing, and in modern C# it can also declare a pattern variable in the same expression. as performs a reference conversion and returns null instead of throwing when it fails, so it only works with reference or nullable types. Neither performs user-defined conversions.

    if (obj is string s && s.Length > 3)
    Console.WriteLine(s);

var str = obj as string;   // null if not a string

    24. What happens when you cast an incompatible reference type?

    An explicit cast between incompatible reference types throws InvalidCastException at runtime, whereas a failed as returns null. This is why testing with is before casting, or using a pattern, is safer than blind casts. Casting to a base or interface type never fails if the object really implements it.

    25. How does basic pattern matching simplify type checks?

    Pattern matching combines the test and the extraction in one expression, removing duplicated casts. A type pattern declares a variable only in scope when the match succeeds, and a switch expression can map several shapes to results concisely. Property, constant and relational patterns extend this further.

    string Describe(object o) => o switch
{
    int n when n < 0 => "negative",
    int n => $"int {n}",
    string s => $"string of length {s.Length}",
    null => "null",
    _ => "other"
};

    26. What is a tuple and how do you deconstruct one?

    A tuple groups a fixed number of values with lightweight syntax and no dedicated type. You deconstruct it by assigning its elements to separate variables in one statement, or by using the var (a, b) form. Tuples are useful for returning multiple values without defining a class.

    (int min, int max) Range(int[] data)
    => (data.Min(), data.Max());

var (low, high) = Range(new[] { 3, 9, 1 });
Console.WriteLine($"{low}-{high}");

    27. What is the difference between ValueTuple and the older Tuple class?

    ValueTuple is a mutable value type with named element support and no heap allocation, and it is what the (a, b) syntax compiles to. The older System.Tuple is a reference type with read-only properties named Item1, Item2 and so on. Prefer ValueTuple unless you specifically need a class.

    28. What is the difference between List<T>, Dictionary<TKey,TValue> and HashSet<T>?

    List<T> is an ordered indexable sequence allowing duplicates. Dictionary<TKey,TValue> maps unique keys to values with near-constant lookup by key, and HashSet<T> stores unique elements with fast membership tests. Choose based on whether you need ordering, key lookup, or uniqueness.

    29. What is the difference between Queue<T> and Stack<T>?

    Queue<T> is first-in-first-out: Enqueue adds at the back and Dequeue removes from the front. Stack<T> is last-in-first-out: Push adds on top and Pop removes the most recent item. Use Peek to inspect the next item without removing it.

    var q = new Queue<int>();
q.Enqueue(1); q.Enqueue(2);
int first = q.Dequeue();   // 1

var s = new Stack<int>();
s.Push(1); s.Push(2);
int top = s.Pop();         // 2

    30. What is the time complexity of a Dictionary lookup and why?

    Dictionary lookup is O(1) on average because it hashes the key and indexes directly into a bucket array. Worst case degrades to O(n) if many keys collide, which is why keys need a good GetHashCode implementation. Enumeration order is not guaranteed.

    31. How do you implement a custom iterator with yield return?

    A method returning IEnumerable<T> can use yield return to produce elements lazily, one at a time, without building a collection. The compiler turns the method into a state machine, so execution pauses at each yield and resumes on the next MoveNext. This makes infinite or streaming sequences possible.

    static IEnumerable<int> Evens(int limit)
{
    for (int i = 0; i <= limit; i += 2)
        yield return i;
}

foreach (var n in Evens(6)) Console.WriteLine(n);

    32. What does the using statement guarantee and what pattern does it rely on?

    The using statement guarantees that Dispose is called when the block exits, even if an exception is thrown. It works with any type implementing IDisposable, and a using declaration (without braces) disposes at the end of the enclosing scope. This is how files, connections and locks are released deterministically.

    using var stream = File.OpenRead("data.txt");
using var reader = new StreamReader(stream);
string content = reader.ReadToEnd();

    33. What is the difference between throw and throw ex inside a catch block?

    throw on its own rethrows the current exception and preserves the original stack trace, while throw ex resets the stack trace to the current line, hiding where the error really started. Always prefer bare throw unless you intentionally want to hide frames. Wrapping is better done with throw new Exception("context", ex).

    34. When should you catch an exception rather than let it propagate?

    Catch only when you can actually handle the failure: retry, fall back, translate to a domain error, or add context. Catching Exception silently or using exceptions for normal control flow hides bugs and hurts performance. Let unexpected errors bubble up to a global handler that logs them.

    35. What is the purpose of the finally block?

    The finally block runs whether the try block completed normally, threw, or returned, which makes it suitable for cleanup that must always happen. It executes after any catch blocks. With using statements most cleanup is already handled, but finally is still useful for flags or metrics.

    36. What is a namespace and how do using directives work?

    A namespace is a logical container that groups related types and prevents name collisions; it does not affect assembly boundaries. A using directive imports a namespace so type names can be written unqualified, and a using alias can shorten or disambiguate a name. Global usings and implicit usings reduce boilerplate in modern projects.

    using System.Text;
using Json = System.Text.Json.JsonSerializer;

    37. What is the difference between DateTime, DateTimeOffset and TimeSpan?

    DateTime holds a date and time with an ambiguous Kind (Utc, Local or Unspecified), DateTimeOffset adds an explicit offset from UTC, and TimeSpan represents a duration rather than a point in time. Prefer DateTimeOffset for values that cross time zones or get persisted. Use TimeSpan for elapsed time and intervals.

    38. How should you store and compare timestamps correctly?

    Store timestamps in UTC (DateTime.UtcNow or DateTimeOffset.UtcNow) and convert to local time only for display. Compare DateTimeOffset values rather than DateTime with mixed kinds, because comparison of Unspecified values ignores offsets. TimeZoneInfo handles conversions when a specific zone is needed.

    39. How do you format a number or date with a custom format string?

    Standard format specifiers like "C", "N2" or "yyyy-MM-dd" describe the output, and they can be passed to ToString or used inside interpolation. Custom patterns use placeholder characters such as dd, MM, HH and mm. Culture affects separators, currency symbols and month names.

    decimal price = 1234.5m;
Console.WriteLine(price.ToString("C2"));      // culture aware
Console.WriteLine(DateTime.Now.ToString("yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm"));

    40. What does string interpolation do and how does it differ from string.Format?

    Interpolation embeds expressions directly in a string literal prefixed with $, and the compiler turns it into a format call or a builder. It is more readable and compile-checked than positional placeholders, since a wrong expression fails at compile time. You can add alignment and format specifiers after a colon.

    string name = "Ann";
int score = 7;
Console.WriteLine($"{name,-10}|{score,5:N1}");

    41. Why does culture matter when parsing or formatting strings?

    Different cultures use different decimal separators, date orders and currency symbols, so "1,5" may parse as 15 or 1.5 depending on culture. Use CultureInfo.InvariantCulture for machine-readable formats such as JSON, logs or file names, and the current culture for user-facing text. Always be explicit when the input source is unknown.

    double v = double.Parse("1.5", CultureInfo.InvariantCulture);
string s = v.ToString("0.00", CultureInfo.InvariantCulture);

    42. What is the difference between Math.Round, Math.Floor and Math.Ceiling?

    Floor always moves down to the nearest integer, Ceiling always moves up, and Round goes to the nearest value using banker's rounding by default. That default means 2.5 rounds to 2, not 3, which surprises many developers. Pass MidpointRounding.AwayFromZero when you expect school-style rounding.

    43. How do you read a text file line by line in C#?

    File.ReadLines returns a lazy sequence that streams the file, so it does not load everything into memory, whereas File.ReadAllLines materializes an array. Wrap stream access in using so the handle is released promptly. Use async variants in I/O-bound code.

    foreach (string line in File.ReadLines("log.txt"))
{
    if (line.Contains("ERROR"))
        Console.WriteLine(line);
}

    44. How do you write text to a file and ensure the handle is closed?

    File.WriteAllText is the simplest option for small content and closes the file itself. For larger or incremental writes, open a StreamWriter inside a using block so buffered data is flushed and the handle released. Consider passing an encoding explicitly when non-ASCII text matters.

    45. What is the difference between File and FileInfo?

    File is a static helper with one-shot methods like Exists, ReadAllText and Delete, while FileInfo is an instance that caches metadata and exposes properties such as Length and LastWriteTime. Use FileInfo when you perform several operations on the same path. Both ultimately call the same underlying APIs.

    46. How do you serialize an object to JSON with System.Text.Json?

    JsonSerializer.Serialize converts an object graph to a JSON string using public properties by default, and Deserialize reverses it. Property names follow the C# names unless you configure a naming policy such as camelCase. Options are passed as a JsonSerializerOptions instance, ideally cached and reused.

    var options = new JsonSerializerOptions
{
    PropertyNamingPolicy = JsonNamingPolicy.CamelCase,
    WriteIndented = true
};

string json = JsonSerializer.Serialize(person, options);

    47. How do you handle a JSON property whose name differs from the C# property?

    Apply the [JsonPropertyName] attribute to map an external name onto the property, which is common when consuming APIs that use snake_case. Unknown members are ignored by default, and missing members leave the property at its default value. Mark required members if their absence should fail deserialization.

    class User
{
    [JsonPropertyName("user_name")]
    public string UserName { get; set; } = "";
}

    48. What does async/await actually do?

    async marks a method that may suspend, and await releases the current thread while waiting for an incomplete task, resuming the continuation when the operation finishes. It is not about creating threads; it is about not blocking one. The compiler rewrites the method into a state machine and returns a Task or Task<T>.

    static async Task<string> LoadAsync(string path)
{
    string text = await File.ReadAllTextAsync(path);
    return text.Trim();
}

    49. Why is calling .Result or .Wait() on a task dangerous?

    Blocking on an async operation can deadlock in contexts with a synchronization context, and it ties up a thread that async was meant to free. It also wraps failures in AggregateException, hiding the real error. Make the call chain async all the way, or use GetAwaiter().GetResult() only as a last resort at the very top.

    50. What is the difference between Task and Task<T>?

    Task represents an operation that completes without producing a value, while Task<T> produces a result of type T accessible through await or Result. Both carry completion state and exceptions. Use ValueTask<T> only when a hot path frequently completes synchronously.

    51. What are top-level statements and when are they appropriate?

    Top-level statements let you write executable code directly in a .cs file without an explicit Main method; the compiler generates the entry point. They suit small console apps, scripts and samples, keeping boilerplate low. Larger applications should still use explicit classes for structure and testability.

    // Program.cs
var names = new[] { "Ann", "Bob" };
foreach (var n in names)
    Console.WriteLine($"Hello {n}");

    52. How does operator precedence affect the result of an expression?

    Operators with higher precedence bind tighter, so multiplication happens before addition and assignment comes last. When two operators share precedence, associativity decides the order, usually left to right. Parentheses override everything and are the clearest way to express intent.

    int a = 2 + 3 * 4;      // 14
int b = (2 + 3) * 4;    // 20
bool c = !true || true; // true

    53. What is the difference between && and & in a boolean expression?

    && short-circuits: the right operand is not evaluated when the left is false, and || skips the right when the left is true. The single-character & and | are non-short-circuiting logical or bitwise operators and always evaluate both sides. Prefer the short-circuit forms for null checks.

    if (list != null && list.Count > 0) { }  // safe
// if (list != null & list.Count > 0) would throw

    54. What do the null-coalescing operators ?? and ??= do?

    ?? returns the left operand if it is not null, otherwise the right operand, which makes it a compact fallback. ??= assigns the right value only when the left variable is null. Both avoid verbose if-null blocks and work with nullable value and reference types.

    string name = input ?? "guest";
cache ??= new Dictionary<string, int>();

    55. What is the difference between the prefix and postfix increment operators?

    ++x increments first and then yields the new value, while x++ yields the current value and increments afterwards. The difference is invisible when the result is discarded, but matters inside larger expressions. Mixing increments into complex expressions hurts readability and is best avoided.

    int x = 5;
int a = ++x;   // x=6, a=6
int b = x++;   // b=6, x=7

    56. How does the conditional operator ?: work and when should you use it?

    The ternary operator evaluates a condition and returns one of two expressions, both of which must be compatible with the target type. It works well for short, side-effect-free choices and can replace a small if/else that only assigns a value. Avoid nesting it deeply, since readability drops quickly.

    string label = count == 1 ? "item" : "items";

    57. What is the difference between break and continue inside a loop?

    break exits the loop immediately, while continue skips the rest of the current iteration and moves to the next one. Neither affects code after the loop; break jumps there directly. In nested loops both act only on the innermost loop.

    58. Does a C# switch statement allow fall-through between cases?

    No, C# forbids implicit fall-through: each non-empty section must end with break, return, throw, continue or goto, so no execution leaks into the next case. Multiple case labels may share one section, which is the supported way to group values. goto case or goto default can be used for explicit jumps, though it is rarely a good idea.

    switch (code)
{
    case 0:
    case 1:
        Console.WriteLine("low");
        break;
    default:
        Console.WriteLine("high");
        break;
}

    59. What is a switch expression and how does it differ from a switch statement?

    A switch expression produces a value, uses arrow arms instead of case labels, and requires an exhaustive set of arms (often with a discard _). It is more compact and composes well inside assignments. A switch statement is still appropriate when each branch performs statements or has side effects.

    string Size(int n) => n switch
{
    < 0 => "invalid",
    0 => "empty",
    <= 10 => "small",
    _ => "large"
};

    60. When would you use foreach instead of a for loop?

    foreach is the clearest choice when you only need each element and do not modify the collection structure, because it works with any IEnumerable and hides index bookkeeping. A for loop is needed when you require the index, iterate backwards, skip elements, or mutate array elements in place. Modifying a collection during foreach throws InvalidOperationException for most collections.

    61. What is the difference between a while loop and a do-while loop?

    while checks the condition before each iteration, so the body may never run. do-while checks after the body, guaranteeing at least one execution, which suits input validation loops. Both need something inside to change the condition or they loop forever.

    string input;
do
{
    input = Console.ReadLine();
} while (string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(input));

    62. How do you exit a nested loop cleanly?

    break only leaves the innermost loop, so the usual clean solutions are a flag checked by the outer loop, a return from a helper method, or a goto label placed after both loops. Extracting the search into a method with return is generally the most readable option.

    static bool Contains(int[,] grid, int target)
{
    foreach (var v in grid)
        if (v == target) return true;
    return false;
}

    63. What is boxing and unboxing?

    Boxing wraps a value type in a heap object when it is assigned to object or an interface it implements; unboxing extracts the value back with an explicit cast. Both cost allocations and copies, so they hurt performance in tight loops. Generic collections avoid boxing because they store T directly.

    object boxed = 42;      // boxing
int value = (int)boxed; // unboxing

    64. What is the difference between a cast, Convert.ToInt32, int.Parse and int.TryParse?

    A cast requires the source to already be a numeric type and does no string conversion, while int.Parse converts a string and throws FormatException on bad input. Convert.ToInt32 accepts strings and nulls, returning 0 for null, and int.TryParse returns false instead of throwing. TryParse is the safe default for user input.

    if (int.TryParse(input, out int qty))
    Console.WriteLine(qty * 2);
else
    Console.WriteLine("not a number");

    65. What happens on integer overflow and how do checked and unchecked change it?

    By default arithmetic overflow in unchecked context silently wraps around, producing a wrong but valid value. The checked keyword or a checked block makes overflow throw OverflowException instead. Compiler settings can make the whole project checked by default.

    int max = int.MaxValue;
int wrapped = max + 1;                  // int.MinValue
// int error = checked(max + 1);        // OverflowException

    66. When should you use decimal instead of double?

    decimal is a base-10 type with high precision and is the right choice for money and other exact decimal quantities. double is binary floating point, faster and wider in range, but cannot represent values like 0.1 exactly. Comparisons of double values should use a tolerance rather than equality.

    double d = 0.1 + 0.2;     // 0.30000000000000004
decimal m = 0.1m + 0.2m;  // 0.3

    67. What is the difference between a char and a string containing one character?

    char is a 16-bit value type holding a single UTF-16 code unit and uses single quotes, while string is an immutable reference type that may hold any number of characters and uses double quotes. A char can be compared and used in arithmetic through its numeric value. Converting between them uses ToString or the string indexer.

    68. How should you compare two strings correctly?

    == on strings performs an ordinal value comparison, which is usually what you want for equality. For explicit intent and culture control use string.Equals with a StringComparison value, and use Compare or string.Compare for ordering. Avoid ToLower comparisons, which allocate and behave oddly for some cultures.

    bool same = string.Equals(a, b, StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase);
int order = string.Compare(a, b, StringComparison.Ordinal);

    69. What is the difference between string.Empty, "" and null?

    string.Empty and "" are both non-null empty strings (and Empty is the same interned instance as the literal in practice), while null means no string object at all. Calling a member on null throws NullReferenceException, so validate with string.IsNullOrEmpty or IsNullOrWhiteSpace. Prefer string.Empty over "" only for style consistency.

    70. How do you split a string and skip empty entries?

    string.Split accepts a StringSplitOptions value that removes empty results and can also trim whitespace around entries. Without the option, consecutive separators produce empty strings. The overload taking char[] plus options covers most parsing needs.

    var parts = csv.Split(',', StringSplitOptions.RemoveEmptyEntries
                            | StringSplitOptions.TrimEntries);

    71. What is a verbatim string literal and when do you use it?

    A verbatim literal prefixed with @ treats backslashes literally and allows newlines, so file paths and regex patterns stay readable. To include a double quote inside it, double the quote. Interpolation can be combined as $@ or @$.

    string path = @"C:\logs\app.log";
string quoted = @"He said ""hi""";

    72. What members does a derived class inherit from its base class?

    A derived class inherits all accessible members: public and protected fields, properties, methods and nested types, but not constructors or private members. Constructors must be chained explicitly with base(...) when arguments are needed. Inheritance expresses an is-a relationship and enables substitution through the base type.

    73. What is the difference between virtual/override and new?

    A virtual member can be replaced by an override in a derived class, and calls through the base reference dispatch to the override at runtime. The new keyword hides a base member instead, so the behavior depends on the static type of the reference. Hiding is a common source of subtle bugs; override is usually what you want.

    class A { public virtual string Name => "A"; }
class B : A { public override string Name => "B"; }

A obj = new B();
Console.WriteLine(obj.Name);   // B

    74. What is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?

    An abstract class can hold state, constructors and implemented members, and a class may inherit only one of them, so it suits a shared partial implementation. An interface declares a contract and a class can implement many, which favors decoupling and testability. Modern C# allows default interface members, but interfaces still cannot hold instance state.

    75. What does the sealed keyword do?

    A sealed class cannot be inherited, and a sealed override prevents further overriding of that member in derived classes. Sealing is useful for security-sensitive or performance-critical types and communicates that the design is final. Sealed classes are also slightly cheaper for some runtime checks.

    76. In what order do constructors run in an inheritance hierarchy?

    Base class field initializers and the base constructor run first, then the derived field initializers, then the derived constructor body. That is why a virtual call from a base constructor reaches an override whose fields are not yet initialized. Constructor execution therefore goes from the most general to the most specific type.

    77. What is polymorphism and how does C# implement runtime polymorphism?

    Polymorphism lets one reference type express several concrete behaviors, so callers depend on an abstraction instead of a specific class. C# implements runtime polymorphism through virtual methods and interface dispatch, resolved via the object's actual type. Compile-time polymorphism comes from overload resolution and generics.

    78. Can an interface contain an implementation in modern C#?

    Yes, since C# 8 interfaces may declare default implementations for members, which lets library authors add members without breaking implementers. Default members are reached through the interface reference, not through the class unless the class redeclares them. Interfaces still cannot declare instance fields or constructors.

    79. What is explicit interface implementation and why use it?

    Explicit implementation names the member as InterfaceName.Member, so it is only callable through that interface reference and is not part of the class's public surface. It resolves name collisions when two interfaces declare the same member and keeps the API clean. A common example is IEnumerable.GetEnumerator.

    class Repo : IDisposable
{
    void IDisposable.Dispose() { /* cleanup */ }
}

    80. How do you decide between an interface and an abstract base class?

    Use an interface when unrelated types should share a capability and you want multiple inheritance of contract, or when you need easy mocking in tests. Use an abstract class when implementations share state or common logic that should not be duplicated. Many designs combine both: an abstract class implementing an interface.

    81. What is a partial class used for?

    partial splits one type's definition across several files that the compiler merges, which suits generated code (designers, source generators) separated from hand-written code. All parts must share the same namespace, name and accessibility. Partial methods allow the generated part to declare a hook that the manual part may implement.

    // Order.generated.cs
public partial class Order { public int Id { get; set; } }

// Order.cs
public partial class Order { public decimal Total() => 0m; }

    82. What are partial methods and what are their constraints?

    A partial method is declared in one part of a partial type, usually by generated code, and optionally implemented in another part. If no implementation exists and the method returns void, the compiler removes the call entirely. Newer versions allow return values and accessibility, but then an implementation becomes mandatory.

    83. What is a record and what makes it different from a class?

    A record is a reference type with compiler-generated value equality, a readable ToString, and support for non-destructive mutation via with. Equality compares all positional and init-only properties rather than object identity. Records are ideal for DTOs, messages and domain values that are defined by their data.

    public record Person(string Name, int Age);

var a = new Person("Ann", 30);
var b = a with { Age = 31 };
Console.WriteLine(a == new Person("Ann", 30));   // True

    84. What is the difference between a record class and a record struct?

    A record class is a reference type with value-based equality, while a record struct is a value type that also gets value equality plus a mutable or readonly layout. record struct avoids heap allocation for small data but is copied on assignment. Both support positional syntax and with expressions.

    85. What does the with expression do on a record?

    with creates a shallow copy of a record and applies the listed changes, leaving the original untouched. This makes immutable update patterns cheap to express. Because the copy is shallow, nested reference members are still shared between the two instances.

    var original = new Person("Ann", 30);
var older = original with { Age = 31 };

    86. What are the requirements for defining an extension method?

    An extension method must be declared in a static non-generic (or generic) static class, be static itself, and have this as the first parameter modifier. The class must be in scope via a using directive. Extension methods cannot access private members and are not virtual.

    public static class StringExtensions
{
    public static bool IsBlank(this string? s)
        => string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(s);
}

bool blank = "  ".IsBlank();

    87. If an extension method and an instance method have the same signature, which wins?

    The instance method always wins; extension methods are only considered when no applicable instance member exists. This means adding a method to a type later can silently change which code runs. Extension methods also cannot override virtual members.

    88. What does deferred execution mean in LINQ?

    A LINQ query over IEnumerable is not executed when it is defined; it runs when you enumerate it, and it re-runs on every enumeration. This allows composition and streaming but can cause repeated database or file work. Materialize with ToList, ToArray or a count when you need a stable snapshot.

    var query = numbers.Where(n => n > 2);   // nothing happens
var list = query.ToList();               // executes now

    89. What is the difference between Where and Select?

    Where filters elements and keeps the original type, while Select projects each element into a new shape or type. Both are lazy and can be chained in any order, though filtering first usually does less work. Select is the equivalent of map in functional terms.

    var names = people
    .Where(p => p.Age >= 18)
    .Select(p => p.Name)
    .ToList();

    90. How do OrderBy, ThenBy and OrderByDescending work together?

    OrderBy establishes the primary sort and returns an IOrderedEnumerable, on which ThenBy adds secondary keys. Calling OrderBy a second time replaces the previous ordering instead of refining it. Use OrderByDescending for a descending primary key.

    var sorted = people
    .OrderBy(p => p.LastName)
    .ThenByDescending(p => p.Age);

    91. What is the difference between First, FirstOrDefault and Single?

    First throws when the sequence is empty, FirstOrDefault returns default(T) or null instead, and Single throws unless exactly one element matches. SingleOrDefault throws when more than one match exists. Choose based on whether "no match" and "many matches" are expected or exceptional.

    92. Why is Any() preferred over Count() > 0?

    Any stops as soon as the first element is found, while Count enumerates the whole sequence (or triggers a full count query). On large or remote sources this is a significant difference. The same idea applies to using Any(predicate) instead of Where(...).Any().

    93. What does ToString() return by default and how do you customize it?

    The base implementation returns the fully qualified type name, which is rarely useful in logs or UI. Override ToString to return a meaningful representation, and prefer it over concatenating properties manually. Records generate a helpful ToString automatically.

    public override string ToString() => $"{Name} ({Age})";

    94. What contract must Equals and GetHashCode satisfy?

    If two objects are equal, they must return the same hash code, and equality must be reflexive, symmetric and transitive over an object's lifetime. GetHashCode must stay stable while the object sits in a hash-based collection. Overriding one without the other breaks Dictionary and HashSet lookups.

    public override bool Equals(object? o)
    => o is Point p && p.X == X && p.Y == Y;

public override int GetHashCode() => HashCode.Combine(X, Y);

    95. What is a .csproj file and what does it typically contain?

    The .csproj file is the MSBuild project definition: it lists the target framework, output type, package references and build settings. SDK-style projects are compact because the SDK supplies default item globs, so .cs files are included automatically. It is XML and should be edited with understanding, since mistakes break restore and build.

    <Project Sdk="Microsoft.NET.Sdk">
  <PropertyGroup>
    <OutputType>Exe</OutputType>
    <TargetFramework>net8.0</TargetFramework>
    <Nullable>enable</Nullable>
  </PropertyGroup>
</Project>

    96. What is the difference between TargetFramework and TargetFrameworks?

    TargetFramework is singular and names one framework such as net8.0, while TargetFrameworks takes a semicolon-separated list to build the same code for several frameworks. Multi-targeting is used by libraries that must support both .NET Framework and modern .NET. The build then produces one output per framework.

    97. What do dotnet new, dotnet build and dotnet run do?

    dotnet new scaffolds a project from a template, dotnet build compiles it and writes binaries to bin, and dotnet run builds if needed and then executes the app. dotnet run is for local development, not deployment, because it depends on the SDK. All commands accept the project or solution path.

    dotnet new console -n Demo
dotnet build Demo/Demo.csproj -c Release
dotnet run --project Demo

    98. How do you add a NuGet package and what happens during restore?

    dotnet add package writes a PackageReference with a version into the .csproj, and restore resolves the full dependency graph, downloading packages into the global cache. Restore also writes the assets file that the compiler uses to resolve references. Transitive dependencies are resolved automatically but can still cause version conflicts.

    dotnet add package Serilog --version 3.1.1
dotnet restore

    99. What is the difference between Debug and Release build configurations?

    Debug emits full symbol information and disables most optimizations so breakpoints and stepping behave predictably. Release enables optimizations, may inline methods, and strips some debug metadata, producing faster and smaller output. Always validate performance and behavior with a Release build.

    100. What is the difference between dotnet build and dotnet publish?

    build compiles the project into bin for local execution, while publish produces a deployable folder including dependencies, runtime configuration and optionally the runtime itself. Publish supports self-contained and framework-dependent modes and can trim or single-file the output. Deploy the publish output, never raw build artifacts.

    101. What is a .sln file used for?

    A solution file groups multiple projects so tools can build and manage them together, recording project paths and build configurations. It is a container, not a build input for the compiler, and each project still builds from its own .csproj. The newer .slnx format is a lighter XML alternative.

    102. What is Directory.Build.props used for?

    It is an MSBuild file automatically imported by every project under its directory, so shared settings such as LangVersion, Nullable or TreatWarningsAsErrors are defined once. This keeps individual .csproj files small and consistent across a repository. Directory.Packages.props serves the same purpose for central package versions.

    103. What do the ImplicitUsings and Nullable properties in the csproj change?

    ImplicitUsings adds a set of common global using directives (System, System.Linq, System.Collections.Generic and others) so files need fewer usings. Nullable enable activates nullable reference type analysis and warnings. Both are on by default in new .NET templates.

    104. How do you run unit tests from the command line?

    dotnet test discovers and runs the test projects in the solution, restoring and building them first. Filters narrow the run to a class, category or name, and a trx logger produces a machine-readable report. Exit code is non-zero when tests fail, which is how CI detects problems.

    dotnet test --filter "FullyQualifiedName~OrderTests"

    105. How do breakpoints and stepping work in a .NET debugger?

    A breakpoint pauses execution at a line so you can inspect locals, arguments and the call stack. Step Into (F11) enters the called method, Step Over (F10) executes it without entering, and Step Out (Shift+F11) finishes the current method. Continue (F5) resumes until the next breakpoint or exception.

    106. When is a conditional breakpoint useful?

    A conditional breakpoint pauses only when an expression is true, such as a specific id or an iteration count above a threshold. This avoids stopping thousands of times in a loop while hunting one bad case. Hit-count breakpoints are a variant that triggers after N hits.

    // condition on a breakpoint
order.Id == 4711 && order.Total < 0

    107. Where does Debug.WriteLine output go?

    Debug.WriteLine writes to attached trace listeners, which the Visual Studio Output window shows during debugging. Console.WriteLine writes to standard output, which is visible in a console app but lost in a GUI or service. The calls are conditional on the DEBUG symbol and are removed from Release builds; Trace.WriteLine remains.

    108. How do you read a call stack when debugging an exception?

    The call stack lists frames from the current method outward, and the topmost frame is where execution stopped, which is where the exception was thrown for unhandled errors. Reading outward shows how that code was reached, which reveals the triggering input. Frames with optimized-away code may be missing in Release builds.

    109. What are the Watch and Immediate windows used for?

    The Watch window continuously evaluates expressions against the current debug context while you step. The Immediate window executes statements and method calls on the fly, which is handy for querying a collection or testing a fix. Both can change state, so use them carefully.

    110. How do you debug async code that seems to hang?

    First look for blocking calls such as .Result, .Wait() or Thread.Sleep in the call stack, since they cause the classic deadlock. The Tasks or Parallel Stacks window shows which tasks are awaiting what. Async methods also make stepping non-linear, so breakpoints after an await are often more useful than single-stepping.

    111. What does enabling nullable reference types actually do?

    It turns on compiler flow analysis for reference types: a non-nullable reference is expected never to be null, and a nullable one must be checked before use. Violations become warnings, not runtime errors, because the runtime is unchanged. The value is catching likely NullReferenceExceptions at compile time.

    #nullable enable
string name = null;      // warning CS8600
string? nickname = null; // fine
int len = nickname.Length; // warning CS8602

    112. What does the null-forgiving operator ! do?

    Appending ! to an expression suppresses nullable warnings by asserting that the value is not null. It changes no runtime behavior, so if the assertion is wrong you still get a NullReferenceException. Use it only where the compiler cannot see an invariant that you have verified.

    string? name = Environment.GetEnvironmentVariable("USER");
Console.WriteLine(name!.Length);   // compiler is silenced, not protected

    113. What is the difference between is null and == null?

    is null is a pattern that cannot be overloaded, so it always performs a true reference or nullable check. == null uses the type's overloaded equality operator, which may behave unexpectedly for types like Unity's Object or custom structs. Prefer is null and is not null for clarity and safety.

    114. How do the null-conditional operators ?. and ?[] behave?

    They short-circuit to null when the receiver is null instead of throwing, and the result type becomes nullable. This makes chained access safe without nested if blocks. Note that the whole chain yields null, so a following member access still needs care.

    int? count = order?.Items?.Count;
string label = order?.Customer?.Name ?? "unknown";

    115. How do you fix a CS8602 possible null reference warning properly?

    Add a real check or guard clause before dereferencing so the compiler's flow analysis can see the invariant. Alternatives are the null-coalescing operator to supply a default, a pattern like "if (x is { } value)", or the [NotNull] style attributes on helper methods. Suppressing with ! should be the last resort.

    if (customer is null)
    throw new ArgumentNullException(nameof(customer));

Console.WriteLine(customer.Name);   // no warning

    116. What is a static class and when should you use one?

    A static class cannot be instantiated or inherited and may contain only static members, which makes it a natural home for stateless helper methods or extension methods. It is convenient but hard to mock, so avoid it for logic that needs testing with dependencies. Static state is shared across the whole process and must be thread-safe.

    117. What are object and collection initializers?

    An object initializer sets properties or fields right after construction, and a collection initializer adds elements in the same expression. Both reduce boilerplate and work with any type exposing a parameterless constructor or an Add method. They do not replace a constructor for enforcing invariants.

    var p = new Person { Name = "Ann", Age = 30 };
var tags = new List<string> { "a", "b" };

    118. What is the default literal and what value does it produce?

    default produces the default value of the inferred type: zero for numeric types, false for bool, and null for reference types and nullable value types. default(T) is the explicit generic form. A struct's default has all fields zeroed, which may be an invalid state for a struct with invariants.

    int a = default;          // 0
string? s = default;      // null
DateTime d = default;     // 01/01/0001

    119. What is the difference between a shallow copy and a deep copy?

    A shallow copy duplicates the outer object but shares referenced child objects, so changing a child is visible from both copies. A deep copy recursively clones the whole graph, giving fully independent instances. MemberwiseClone and record with expressions produce shallow copies.

    120. What is the difference between a static member and an instance member?

    An instance member belongs to each object and can access its state through this, while a static member belongs to the type and is shared by all instances. Static members cannot use this or instance fields. Static mutable state is a common source of concurrency bugs and test pollution.

    121. What is the nameof operator good for?

    nameof returns the compile-time name of a variable, member or type as a string, so refactoring renames update it automatically. It is commonly used in ArgumentNullException and logging where a string name is required. It has no runtime cost because the compiler substitutes a constant.

    if (input is null)
    throw new ArgumentNullException(nameof(input));

    122. What is the difference between Environment.CurrentDirectory and AppContext.BaseDirectory?

    Environment.CurrentDirectory is the process working directory, which can change at runtime and differs by how the app is launched. AppContext.BaseDirectory is the folder containing the application's assemblies and is stable. Use BaseDirectory to locate files shipped with the app and CurrentDirectory only when the user's context genuinely matters.

    123. Why should you use Path.Combine instead of string concatenation for paths?

    Path.Combine inserts the correct directory separator for the platform and handles trailing separators, avoiding malformed paths. Manual concatenation with "\\" breaks on non-Windows systems. Use Path.Join when you do not want later segments to override earlier absolute ones.

    string full = Path.Combine(AppContext.BaseDirectory, "config", "app.json");

    124. What is a GUID and when do you generate one?

    A GUID is a 128-bit identifier that is practically unique without coordination, represented by the Guid struct. Guid.NewGuid creates a random (version 4) value, which is useful for entity keys, correlation ids and file names. It is not sequential, so it can fragment indexes compared with an integer or sequential GUID key.

    Guid id = Guid.NewGuid();
Console.WriteLine(id.ToString("N"));   // 32 hex chars

    125. How do you measure how long a piece of code takes?

    Stopwatch uses a high-resolution timer and is far more accurate than subtracting DateTime values. Start it, run the code, stop it, and read Elapsed or ElapsedMilliseconds. For real benchmarks use BenchmarkDotNet, since a single measurement is noisy.

    var sw = Stopwatch.StartNew();
DoWork();
sw.Stop();
Console.WriteLine($"{sw.ElapsedMilliseconds} ms");

    126. How do you validate a string format with a regular expression?

    Regex.IsMatch returns true when the pattern matches anywhere in the input, so anchor with ^ and $ for whole-string validation. Compile or cache the Regex instance when it is reused in a hot path. Regex is powerful but hard to read, so keep patterns simple and documented.

    var zip = new Regex(@"^\d{5}$");
bool ok = zip.IsMatch("12345");

    127. How do you add days or hours to a DateTime?

    DateTime is immutable, so AddDays, AddHours and similar methods return a new value instead of modifying the original. Combine them with a TimeSpan for durations, and use DateTimeOffset when offsets matter. Forgetting to use the returned value is a classic bug.

    var now = DateTime.UtcNow;
var due = now.AddDays(7).AddHours(-2);
var span = due - now;   // TimeSpan

    128. How do you join a collection of strings into one string?

    string.Join concatenates elements with a separator and handles null elements as empty strings, which is safer than manual concatenation with separators. It accepts any IEnumerable and an optional start index and count. For object collections, project to strings first with Select.

    string csv = string.Join(", ", names);
string line = string.Join(';', items.Select(i => i.Id));

    129. What is the difference between IEnumerable<T> and List<T>?

    IEnumerable<T> is a read-only, forward-only sequence contract that supports lazy evaluation and can represent a query, a file or a database result. List<T> is a concrete mutable collection with indexing, Add and Remove. Accept IEnumerable in method parameters for flexibility and return List or IReadOnlyList when callers need materialized data.

    130. What is the difference between IEnumerable and IQueryable?

    IQueryable carries an expression tree so a provider such as EF Core can translate the query into SQL and execute it remotely. IEnumerable executes delegates in memory, which means filtering happens after all rows are fetched. Mixing them by calling AsEnumerable too early is a common performance mistake.

    131. What is a lambda expression and how do Func and Action relate to it?

    A lambda is an anonymous function written as parameters => body, which the compiler converts into a delegate or expression tree. Func<T> represents a method with a return value and Action represents one without. Lambdas capture variables from the enclosing scope, which the compiler lifts into a closure.

    Func<int, int> square = x => x * x;
Action<string> log = msg => Console.WriteLine(msg);

Console.WriteLine(square(4));

    132. What is a delegate in C#?

    A delegate is a type-safe reference to a method with a specific signature, so behavior can be passed as a value. Custom delegates are declared with the delegate keyword, though Func and Action cover most cases now. Multicast delegates hold several targets and invoke them in order.

    public delegate int Operation(int a, int b);
Operation add = (a, b) => a + b;
int result = add(2, 3);

    133. What is the difference between Array.Sort and LINQ OrderBy?

    Array.Sort sorts in place and mutates the array, while OrderBy returns a new lazy sequence and leaves the source unchanged. OrderBy is stable, so equal keys keep their original order, whereas Array.Sort is not guaranteed stable. Sorting in place is faster and allocation-free when mutation is acceptable.

    134. How do you remove duplicates from a collection?

    Passing the sequence to a HashSet removes duplicates by equality, and Distinct does the same lazily while preserving first occurrence order. For custom types, equality depends on Equals and GetHashCode or on a custom IEqualityComparer. Choose based on whether you need the result as a set or as an ordered list.

    var unique = items.Distinct().ToList();
var set = new HashSet<string>(names);

    135. Why do int and Int32 mean the same thing?

    Keywords such as int, string and bool are aliases for the BCL types System.Int32, System.String and System.Boolean, so they compile to identical code. The lowercase keyword is preferred by convention in C# code. Using the alias or the full name has no runtime cost.

    136. What does a #nullable directive control?

    #nullable enable, disable, restore and annotations change the nullable analysis context inside a file, which is useful when a project enables nullable globally but a legacy file is not ready. restore returns to the project setting, so it pairs with enable. The directive affects compiler warnings only.

    137. What is an exception filter with when?

    An exception filter adds a condition to a catch clause, so the handler runs only when the predicate is true; otherwise the search for a handler continues without unwinding the stack. This is useful for inspecting an error code or logging selectively. Filters cannot contain code with side effects that must run.

    try { Call(); }
catch (HttpRequestException ex) when (ex.StatusCode == HttpStatusCode.NotFound)
{
    return null;
}

    138. How do you define a custom exception type?

    Derive from Exception, provide the three standard constructors (parameterless, message, message plus inner exception), and mark the type serializable if needed. Name it with an Exception suffix and keep it focused on one failure condition. Throw it only for genuinely exceptional situations.

    public class OrderNotFoundException : Exception
{
    public OrderNotFoundException(int id)
        : base($"Order {id} was not found.") { }
}

    139. What is the multiple enumeration problem with IEnumerable?

    Because a sequence may be lazy, enumerating it twice can execute the underlying query or I/O twice, wasting work or producing different results. This often appears when you call Count() and then foreach over the same query. Materialize once with ToList when you need several passes.

    var list = query.ToList();   // one execution
int n = list.Count;
foreach (var x in list) { }

    140. How do you convert a string to an enum safely?

    Enum.TryParse returns false instead of throwing when the text is not a valid name or number, and ignoreCase controls matching. A successful parse of a number can still produce an undefined value, so validate with Enum.IsDefined when that matters. Enum.Parse is the throwing alternative.

    if (Enum.TryParse<Status>("Active", true, out var status)
    && Enum.IsDefined(status))
{
    Console.WriteLine(status);
}

    141. What is encapsulation and how is it implemented in C#?

    Encapsulation means hiding internal state and exposing only a controlled public surface, so callers cannot put an object into an invalid state. In C# it is implemented with access modifiers, properties that validate values, and private fields. The goal is not secrecy but protecting invariants and allowing the implementation to change without breaking consumers.

    public class Account
{
    private decimal _balance;

    public decimal Balance => _balance;

    public void Deposit(decimal amount)
    {
        if (amount <= 0) throw new ArgumentOutOfRangeException(nameof(amount));
        _balance += amount;
    }
}

    142. What is the difference between a field and a property?

    A field is a storage location; a property is a pair of accessors (get/set) that behave like a field to callers but can contain logic. Properties support validation, lazy initialization, change notification, and different access levels per accessor. Public fields cannot do any of that and also break binary compatibility when you later convert them into properties, so the convention is private fields plus public properties.

    143. Why should public fields generally be avoided?

    Public fields expose raw storage, so any caller can bypass validation and invariants, and you cannot add logging or notifications later without breaking callers. Changing a field to a property is a binary-breaking change for compiled assemblies, while changing a property's body is not. Interfaces also cannot declare fields, which limits abstraction.

    144. What access modifiers exist in C# and what does each allow?

    public is visible everywhere, private only inside the containing type, protected to derived types, and internal within the same assembly. protected internal is the union of protected and internal, while private protected is the intersection (derived types in the same assembly). Top-level types can only be public or internal; members default to private.

    145. What is inheritance and what are its limitations in C#?

    Inheritance lets a class reuse and specialize a base class, establishing an "is-a" relationship. C# supports single class inheritance only, although a class can implement many interfaces. Inheritance creates tight coupling, so composition is often preferred when the relationship is really "has-a" or when behavior varies independently.

    146. What is polymorphism in C# and what forms does it take?

    Polymorphism is the ability to treat objects of different types through a common abstraction. C# has compile-time polymorphism (overloading, resolved by the compiler from static types) and runtime polymorphism (virtual/abstract members dispatched through the object's actual type). Interface dispatch is the usual way to get runtime polymorphism without a shared base class.

    abstract class Shape { public abstract double Area(); }
class Circle : Shape
{
    public double R { get; init; }
    public override double Area() => Math.PI * R * R;
}

Shape s = new Circle { R = 2 };
Console.WriteLine(s.Area()); // resolved at runtime

    147. What is the difference between overloading and overriding?

    Overloading means several members with the same name but different signatures in the same type; the compiler picks one from the static argument types. Overriding replaces a virtual or abstract base implementation at runtime, and the signature must match exactly. Overloading is resolved at compile time, overriding at run time.

    148. What is an abstract class?

    An abstract class cannot be instantiated and may declare abstract members that derived classes must implement, while also providing shared state and concrete behavior. It is appropriate when related types share an implementation and a partial contract. A derived class must implement every abstract member unless it is itself abstract.

    149. What is an interface in C#?

    An interface declares a contract of members that implementing types must provide, without specifying state. Since C# 8 interfaces may also contain default implementations, static members, and private helpers, but they still cannot hold instance fields. Interfaces enable multiple inheritance of behavior contracts and loose coupling.

    150. When should you prefer an abstract class over an interface?

    Choose an abstract class when implementations share real code or state and the types form a genuine "is-a" hierarchy with a single base. Choose an interface when unrelated types must satisfy a capability and you want multiple implementation or versioning through default members. Many designs use both: an interface for the contract plus an abstract base class supplying a default implementation.

    151. Can a class inherit from one base class and implement several interfaces?

    Yes. C# allows exactly one base class but any number of interfaces, and the base class must be listed first in the declaration. If two interfaces declare the same member signature, a single public implementation satisfies both, while explicit implementation can keep them separate. Structs can implement interfaces but cannot inherit from a class.

    152. What is the difference between virtual, override, and new?

    virtual declares a member that derived classes may replace and that is dispatched through the runtime type. override replaces an inherited virtual member and extends the virtual slot. new hides an inherited member by name instead of overriding it, and which member is called then depends on the static type of the reference.

    153. What does the sealed keyword do?

    On a class, sealed prevents further inheritance; on an override, it stops further overriding of that member in derived types. Sealing can help the JIT devirtualize calls and it documents design intent, but it closes extensibility. A sealed class can still implement interfaces and be composed.

    154. Can you override a member that was marked sealed override?

    No. A sealed override occupies the virtual slot permanently, so any derived class that declares the same member must use new, which hides instead of overriding. That is a common way to lock down behavior inherited from a framework base type.

    155. What is method hiding and why can it be dangerous?

    Hiding declares a member with the same name in a derived class without override, so the base member still exists and is called whenever the reference type is the base class. The result depends on the static type of the variable, which surprises readers and breaks polymorphism. The compiler only warns (CS0108), so accidental hiding can ship silently.

    class A { public void Run() => Console.WriteLine("A"); }
class B : A { public new void Run() => Console.WriteLine("B"); }

A x = new B();
x.Run();          // "A" - hiding, not polymorphism
((B)x).Run();     // "B"

    156. What is the purpose of the base keyword?

    base gives access to the inherited implementation or constructor, which is essential when an override wants to extend rather than replace behavior. In a constructor it forwards arguments to a specific base constructor. It always refers to the immediate base class, not the root type.

    157. How does abstraction differ from encapsulation?

    Abstraction reduces complexity by exposing a simplified model of a concept and hiding irrelevant detail; encapsulation is the mechanism that enforces the boundary. You design an abstraction (an interface or abstract class) and use encapsulation (access modifiers, properties) to protect it. In practice abstraction is a design decision, encapsulation is an implementation technique.

    158. What is the difference between Object.Equals and the == operator?

    == is a static, overloadable operator resolved at compile time, while Equals is a virtual method dispatched at runtime. For reference types without an overload, == compares references; for string and some value types it compares values. Collections and generic code call Equals, so overriding one without the other produces inconsistent behavior.

    159. Why must GetHashCode be overridden together with Equals?

    Hash-based collections require that equal objects produce the same hash code, otherwise a lookup can miss an object that is present. If two objects are equal by Equals but have different hashes, HashSet and Dictionary treat them as different keys. The hash must be built from the same members used for equality and must not change while the object is in a hash collection.

    public override bool Equals(object? obj) =>
    obj is Order o && o.Id == Id;

public override int GetHashCode() => HashCode.Combine(Id);

    160. What is the difference between reference equality and value equality?

    Reference equality asks whether two variables point to the same object instance, which is ReferenceEquals or the default == for classes. Value equality asks whether two objects represent the same data, which requires an Equals override, a record, or a struct with implemented comparison. Structs get field-by-field value equality by default; classes do not.

    161. What does ToString do and why override it?

    ToString returns a string representation and is called implicitly by string interpolation, concatenation, and debuggers. The default returns the type's full name, which is rarely useful in logs. Override it for diagnostic output, but keep it cheap, side-effect free, and culture-aware when the text is user-facing.

    162. What is IComparable used for?

    IComparable<T> defines the natural ordering of a type through CompareTo, returning a negative number, zero, or a positive number. Implementing it enables List.Sort(), Array.Sort, and default comparers in sorted collections. It should be consistent with Equals to avoid surprising results.

    public int CompareTo(Money? other) =>
    other is null ? 1 : Amount.CompareTo(other.Amount);

    163. What is IComparer and when do you use it instead of IComparable?

    IComparer<T> is an external comparison strategy passed to sort methods, so it lets you order a type in several ways without changing the type itself. Use it when you do not own the type, when ordering depends on the context, or when you need multiple orderings. Comparer<T>.Create gives a quick way to build one from a lambda.

    var byName = Comparer<Person>.Create((a, b) =>
    string.Compare(a.Name, b.Name, StringComparison.Ordinal));
people.Sort(byName);

    164. What is the difference between IEquatable and overriding Equals(object)?

    IEquatable<T> provides a strongly typed Equals(T) that avoids boxing and casting in generic collections, which matters for structs. Overriding Equals(object) is still required for non-generic callers, so a well-behaved value type usually does both. Generic collections check for IEquatable<T> first, so implementing it is a real performance win.

    165. What are generics and what problem do they solve?

    Generics let you define types and methods parameterized by type, giving compile-time type safety and avoiding boxing, casts, and duplicated code. Before generics, reusable containers were built on object, which pushed type errors to runtime. Generic instantiations over reference types share one JIT-compiled implementation, so there is little code bloat.

    166. What generic constraints are available in C#?

    The main constraints are class, struct, new(), notnull, unmanaged, a base class, an interface, and another type parameter (where T : U). Constraints are checked at compile time for callers and enable members of the constrained type inside the generic body. Multiple constraints are combined in one where clause, with the class type first and new() last.

    167. What does the new() constraint require?

    new() requires the type argument to have a public parameterless constructor, which allows the generic code to call new T(). It cannot be combined with struct (structs already satisfy it) or with an abstract class constraint. It also implies the type is not nullable in the reference-type sense.

    168. What is the difference between where T : class and where T : struct?

    class constrains to reference types and allows null; struct constrains to non-nullable value types and gives access to default(T) without null checks. With struct the compiler knows T is a value type and never boxes in comparisons against default. Note that Nullable<T> does not satisfy struct.

    169. What is variance in generic interfaces?

    Covariance (out T) lets you use a more derived type argument where a less derived one is expected, as with IEnumerable<string> to IEnumerable<object>. Contravariance (in T) goes the other way and is used for consumers such as IComparer<object> to IComparer<string>. Variance requires reference types and is only allowed on interfaces and delegates where the parameter position permits it.

    170. How does generic type inference work?

    When you call a generic method without explicit type arguments, the compiler infers them from the argument types and their implicit conversions. If inference is ambiguous or fails, you must specify the arguments explicitly. Inference does not consider the return type, which is why some APIs need an explicit type argument or a helper class.

    171. What is the difference between a generic method and a generic type?

    A generic type parameterizes the whole type, so every member can use it, while a generic method parameterizes only that method and can appear in a non-generic type. Generic methods make static helper APIs easier to call because inference works per call site. Both compile to a single definition with type parameters substituted at runtime.

    172. How do you get the default value of a generic type parameter?

    Use default(T) or the target-typed default literal, which yields null for reference types and zeroed bits for value types. With C# 9 and nullable reference types you may need T? or default! to express intent. Comparing a T against default with == requires an unconstrained-safe pattern such as EqualityComparer<T>.Default.

    173. Why can't you reliably use == with an unconstrained generic type parameter?

    The compiler does not know whether T is a reference type with an overloaded ==, a value type, or a type without that operator, so a == b does not compile for unconstrained T. Use EqualityComparer<T>.Default.Equals(a, b) instead, which respects IEquatable<T>, overrides, and null semantics. That also avoids boxing for value types.

    174. What is a static constructor and when does it run?

    A static constructor runs once per closed type, before the first static member access or first instance creation, and the runtime guarantees it is not run concurrently twice. It cannot take parameters or access modifiers and exceptions from it surface as TypeInitializationException, permanently breaking the type. Prefer static field initializers or lazy initialization when failure must be recoverable.

    175. What is the difference between static and instance members?

    Static members belong to the type and are shared by all instances; instance members belong to a specific object and can access its state. Static state is effectively global within the application domain, so it must be thread-safe and is harder to test. Static methods cannot use this or be virtual.

    176. Can a static class have a constructor, and can it be instantiated?

    A static class cannot be instantiated or inherited and may contain only static members; it can have a static constructor to initialize static fields. The compiler seals and abstracts it automatically. Extension methods require a static, non-nested, non-generic class.

    177. What are nested types and when are they useful?

    A nested type is declared inside another type and can access the enclosing type's private members, which makes it useful for implementation details such as builders, enumerators, or caches. Nesting also expresses a strong ownership relationship and keeps the namespace clean. Nested types do not automatically get an instance of the outer type, unlike in Java.

    178. What is a partial class used for?

    partial splits one type across several files that the compiler merges, which is how source generators and designers add code without touching your file. Attributes and interfaces are combined across parts, but each part must be marked partial and be in the same assembly. It is purely a compile-time feature with no runtime cost.

    179. What are partial methods?

    A partial method declares a signature in one part of a partial type and optionally implements it in another; if no implementation exists, calls are removed by the compiler. Classic partial methods must return void and cannot have out parameters, though C# 9 lifted most of these restrictions when an implementation is required. They are typically used by code generators to expose optional hooks.

    180. What is an indexer?

    An indexer lets an object be indexed with [ ] syntax by declaring this[parameter] with get and/or set accessors. It can take any parameter types and be overloaded, which makes wrapper types feel like collections. Indexers are members, not properties, and can be virtual or implemented explicitly from an interface.

    public class Grid
{
    private readonly int[,] _cells = new int[10, 10];
    public int this[int row, int col]
    {
        get => _cells[row, col];
        set => _cells[row, col] = value;
    }
}

    181. Can a type declare more than one indexer?
