    Yes, indexers can be overloaded as long as the parameter lists differ, for example by index type or arity. They can also differ in return type only if the parameter list differs, because the signature is based on parameters. A common pattern is an int indexer plus a string key indexer.

    182. What is explicit interface implementation and when do you need it?

    Explicit implementation declares a member as void IFoo.Bar(), so it is reachable only through the interface reference and is hidden from the class's public surface. You need it when two interfaces declare conflicting members with the same signature, or when you want a member used only via the abstraction. It cannot be virtual, abstract, or accessed through the class type.

    class Logger : IDisposable
{
    void IDisposable.Dispose() => Flush();

    public void Flush() { /* ... */ }
}

    183. What happens when a class implements two interfaces with the same member signature?

    One public member satisfies both interfaces if the signature and return type match, which is usually what you want. If the behaviors must differ, implement at least one explicitly so callers get the right one through each interface. This is exactly how generic and non-generic GetEnumerator coexist on collection types.

    184. What is operator overloading and when should you avoid it?

    Operator overloading defines what +, ==, !=, implicit conversions, and similar operators mean for your type, which is appropriate for value-like types such as Money or Vector. Avoid it when the meaning is not obvious or when it hides expensive work, and never make it surprising or lossy. Overloaded == and != must be defined as a pair.

    public readonly record struct Money(decimal Amount)
{
    public static Money operator +(Money a, Money b) =>
        new(a.Amount + b.Amount);
}

    185. Which operators must be overloaded together?

    == and != must both be defined, and if either is overloaded the compiler expects Equals and GetHashCode overrides too for consistency. Comparison operators < and > must be paired, as must <= and >=. Implicit and explicit conversion operators are independent but should not bypass validation.

    186. What are object initializers and collection initializers?

    Object initializers assign properties after construction with new T { A = 1, B = 2 }, while collection initializers add elements with new List<int> { 1, 2, 3 } by calling Add. Both are purely syntactic sugar over constructor plus assignments, so they require an accessible parameterless constructor unless you also pass arguments. Collection initializers require the type to implement IEnumerable and have an applicable Add method.

    187. What is the difference between an object initializer and a constructor?

    A constructor runs inside the type and can enforce invariants across all arguments; an object initializer runs after construction and sets members one by one, so intermediate invalid states are possible. Initializers also cannot be intercepted by the type, which matters for immutability. For immutable types prefer constructors or init accessors.

    188. What is the difference between an array and a List<T>?

    An array has a fixed length and is allocated as one contiguous block, so it has the lowest overhead but cannot grow. List<T> wraps an array, grows it geometrically by doubling, and exposes Add, Remove, and other conveniences at the cost of extra indirection and unused capacity. Use arrays for fixed-size, hot data and lists for general-purpose mutable sequences.

    189. When would you use a Dictionary instead of a List?

    Use a Dictionary<TKey, TValue> when you need lookup by key in near-constant time; a list lookup is a linear scan and becomes slow as it grows. A dictionary costs more memory and loses insertion order, so a list is better for small collections, ordered iteration, or index-based access. If you need both, keep a list and a dictionary in sync.

    190. What is a HashSet and when is it preferable to a List?

    HashSet<T> stores unique elements with O(1) average Contains, Add, and Remove, making it ideal for membership tests and de-duplication. A list preserves order and allows duplicates and indexing, which a set does not. HashSet also offers set operations such as UnionWith and IntersectWith.

    var seen = new HashSet<string>(StringComparer.OrdinalIgnoreCase);
foreach (var tag in tags)
    if (seen.Add(tag)) Console.WriteLine(tag); // unique only

    191. What is the difference between Queue and Stack?

    Queue<T> is first-in-first-out with Enqueue/Dequeue, while Stack<T> is last-in-first-out with Push/Pop. Both are O(1) amortized and both offer Peek and TryDequeue/TryPop. Use a queue for breadth-first or work distribution, a stack for depth-first or undo.

    192. What are the main trade-offs of Dictionary<TKey, TValue>?

    It offers O(1) average lookup but relies on good hash distribution, so a poor GetHashCode degrades it to linear probing and can cause severe slowdowns. It is not thread-safe for concurrent writes and does not preserve insertion order. It also throws on duplicate keys with Add but overwrites with the indexer, so choose deliberately.

    193. What happens if you mutate an object used as a dictionary key?

    The entry is stored in the bucket computed from the original hash, so after mutation the lookup searches a different bucket and reports the key as missing. The item is effectively lost and cannot be removed reliably until rehashed. Keys should therefore be immutable, or at least not participate in GetHashCode after insertion.

    194. What is IEnumerable<T> and what does it guarantee?

    IEnumerable<T> exposes a forward-only cursor over a sequence through GetEnumerator, and that is all it guarantees: you can iterate, possibly many times, possibly with different results. It says nothing about count, indexing, or mutability. It is the minimal contract that LINQ and foreach build on.

    195. What is the difference between IEnumerable, ICollection, and IList?

    IEnumerable<T> only supports iteration, ICollection<T> adds Count, Add, Remove, and Contains, and IList<T> adds index access and Insert. Each step trades generality for capability, so accept the weakest interface your method actually needs. Returning IReadOnlyList<T> is a good compromise for read-only indexed data.

    196. What is the difference between IEnumerable and IQueryable?

    IEnumerable<T> executes delegates in memory with LINQ-to-Objects, while IQueryable<T> carries an expression tree that a provider translates, for example into SQL. With IQueryable the filtering happens on the server only if you keep composing before materialization. Calling AsEnumerable too early silently pulls the whole table into memory.

    197. What is deferred execution in LINQ?

    Most LINQ operators only build a pipeline; nothing is enumerated until you iterate or call a materializing operator such as ToList, Count, or First. This allows composition and lets providers translate the whole query, but it also means the source can change before execution. Operators that must return a single value or a collection execute immediately.

    var query = numbers.Where(n => n > 2); // nothing runs yet
numbers.Add(99);
foreach (var n in query) Console.WriteLine(n); // 99 is included

    198. What is the problem with enumerating a sequence more than once?

    A deferred query re-executes on every enumeration, so repeated iteration can cause duplicate database round-trips, repeated file reads, or non-deterministic results if the source changed. It is easy to do accidentally, for example calling Count() and then iterating. Materialize once with ToList when you need multiple passes.

    199. How do you detect or prevent accidental multiple enumeration?

    Accept and return IReadOnlyList<T> or ICollection<T> when the data will be used more than once, and materialize at the boundary where the query is defined. Analyzers such as the Roslyn multiple-enumeration rules catch the pattern statically. Inside a method, call ToList() once and use the list everywhere.

    200. How does GroupBy work in LINQ?

    GroupBy builds a lookup keyed by the selector and buffers the whole sequence, because it must read everything before yielding the first group. Each result is an IGrouping<TKey, TElement> that is itself enumerable. Use the element selector and result selector overloads to avoid projecting twice.

    var byCity = people
    .GroupBy(p => p.City, p => p.Name)
    .Select(g => new { City = g.Key, Count = g.Count() });

    201. What is the difference between Select and SelectMany?

    Select maps each element one-to-one, producing a sequence of the same length, while SelectMany maps each element to a sequence and flattens the results into one. SelectMany is the operator for nested collections and for cross joins. Forgetting to flatten leaves you with IEnumerable<IEnumerable<T>>.

    202. How does Join work and how does it differ from GroupJoin?

    Join performs an inner equi-join, emitting one result per matching pair and dropping unmatched elements from both sides. GroupJoin emits one result per outer element with all its matches, which you can flatten to emulate a left outer join. Both use a hash lookup on the inner sequence, so the key selectors should be simple and consistent.

    var q = from o in orders
        join c in customers on o.CustomerId equals c.Id
        select new { c.Name, o.Total };

    203. What does Aggregate do and when is it appropriate?

    Aggregate folds a sequence into a single value by repeatedly applying a function with an accumulator, optionally starting from a seed. It is appropriate for custom folds that no built-in operator covers, such as running products or building a string. For simple sums, counts, or concatenations the dedicated operators are clearer and often faster.

    var product = numbers.Aggregate(1, (acc, n) => acc * n);

    204. Why is Any() better than Count() > 0?

    Any() stops after the first element, while Count() > 0 may enumerate the entire sequence. Against a database, Count() becomes a SELECT COUNT(*) over the whole set, which is far more expensive than an existence check. Any(predicate) also short-circuits, unlike Count(predicate) == 0.

    205. What is the difference between All and Any?

    Any() asks whether at least one element matches, while All() asks whether every element matches. Both short-circuit: Any stops at the first match, All at the first mismatch. Note that All on an empty sequence returns true, which is mathematically correct but can surprise callers.

    206. When should you use First, FirstOrDefault, or Single?

    First takes the first match and throws if none exists, FirstOrDefault returns the default instead, and Single throws unless exactly one match exists. Use Single to assert uniqueness, such as lookup by primary key. Prefer the *OrDefault variants only when absence is a normal, expected case.

    207. What is the difference between Where(...).Count() and Count(predicate)?

    The overload Count(predicate) does the filtering and counting in one pass with no intermediate iterator allocation. Where(...).Count() composes two operators, adding a small amount of overhead and an extra allocation per call. The result is identical, so prefer the predicate overload in hot paths.

    208. What is a lambda expression?

    A lambda is an anonymous function written inline that can be converted to a delegate or an expression tree. Its parameter types are usually inferred, and it can capture variables from the enclosing scope. Statement-bodied lambdas use braces, expression-bodied ones do not.

    209. What is a closure and what are its pitfalls?

    A closure is created when a lambda captures a local variable or parameter; the compiler lifts that variable into a compiler-generated class so it outlives the stack frame. Captured variables are shared by reference, so all delegates see the latest value, and the captured state keeps objects alive longer than expected. In hot paths, closures also allocate, which matters for performance-sensitive code.

    Func<int> MakeCounter()
{
    int count = 0;
    return () => ++count;   // count is lifted to a closure
}

    210. Why did capturing a loop variable used to be a bug, and what changed?

    Before C# 5, foreach declared a single variable reused across iterations, so lambdas created in the loop all captured the same variable and observed its final value. Since C# 5, foreach creates a fresh variable per iteration, so the pattern works naturally. A plain for loop still declares one variable, so you must copy it into a local inside the body.

    for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++)
{
    int copy = i;
    actions.Add(() => Console.WriteLine(copy)); // 0,1,2
}

    211. What is the difference between Func, Action, and Predicate?

    Func<...> returns a value, Action<...> returns void, and Predicate<T> is a specialized delegate returning bool for a single argument. Predicate<T> predates Func<T, bool> and appears mostly in older APIs such as List.Find. They are not interchangeable without an adapter because they are distinct delegate types.

    212. What is the difference between Func<T> and Expression<Func<T>>?

    Func<T> is compiled code you can invoke directly, while Expression<Func<T>> is a data structure describing the code, which a provider can inspect and translate. LINQ-to-Entities uses expression trees so it can generate SQL instead of running the lambda in memory. You can compile an expression with .Compile(), but that loses translatability and costs startup time.

    213. What is a delegate?

    A delegate is a type-safe reference to a method with a specific signature; an instance holds a target object and a method pointer, so it can represent instance, static, or lambda code. Delegates are immutable, so += creates a new combined delegate rather than modifying the original. They are the foundation of callbacks, events, and LINQ.

    214. How do multicast delegates behave with return values?

    A multicast delegate holds an invocation list and calls every target in order, but only the return value of the last one is observed, so return values are effectively lost. Exceptions stop the chain, so later subscribers never run. This is why events are normally typed as void-returning delegates.

    215. What is an event and how does it differ from a public delegate field?

    An event exposes only += and -= to outside code, so subscribers cannot invoke it, replace the whole invocation list, or clear other handlers; a public delegate field allows all of that. Events therefore protect the publisher's contract and are the correct choice for notifications. Inside the declaring class the event behaves like a delegate field.

    public event EventHandler<EventArgs>? Changed;

protected virtual void OnChanged() =>
    Changed?.Invoke(this, EventArgs.Empty);

    216. Why check an event for null before raising it?

    An event with no subscribers is null, so invoking it throws NullReferenceException. Copying it into a local first avoids a race where a subscriber unsubscribes between the null check and the invocation. The null-conditional call Changed?.Invoke(...) is the concise modern form.

    217. Can events cause memory leaks?

    Yes. The publisher holds a reference to every subscriber through the delegate, so a long-lived publisher keeps short-lived subscribers alive until they unsubscribe. This is a classic leak in static events, long-lived services, and UI handlers. Always unsubscribe in Dispose or use weak event patterns for long-lived publishers.

    218. What is an anonymous type?

    An anonymous type is an immutable compiler-generated class created with new { A = 1, B = "x" }, useful mainly for projections in LINQ queries. It gets value equality, a readable ToString, and read-only properties. It cannot be returned from a method typed as object and later used statically, because its name is not accessible.

    219. Why can't an anonymous type cross a method boundary?

    Its type has no accessible name outside the assembly, so you cannot declare a variable, parameter, or return type for it. You can pass it as object or use dynamic, but both lose compile-time safety. For public APIs use a named type, a tuple, or a record instead.

    220. What are nullable reference types?

    Nullable reference types are a compile-time annotation system: with <Nullable>enable</Nullable>, string means "not expected to be null" and string? means "may be null", and the compiler warns about possible dereferences. The annotations are erased at runtime and the CLR still allows null, so they are contracts and tooling rather than enforcement. They also make intent explicit in public signatures.

    221. What does the null-forgiving operator do?

    The postfix ! suppresses the nullable warning for an expression without changing runtime behavior. It is appropriate when you have knowledge the compiler lacks, such as a field initialized by a framework or a validated parameter. Using it routinely hides real bugs, so prefer restructuring the code or adding a proper guard.

    string? input = Console.ReadLine();
string value = input!; // compiler trusts you; still null at runtime

    222. What is the difference between ?. and ?? and ??=?

    ?. short-circuits member access when the receiver is null and yields null, ?? supplies a fallback for a null left operand, and ??= assigns only when the target is null. They compose well, for example cache[key] ??= Build(). ?. on a value type returns T?, so unwrap carefully.

    223. How does garbage collection work in .NET?

    The GC is a tracing, generational collector that finds live objects starting from roots such as stack locals, statics, and handles, then reclaims everything unreachable and compacts the heap. It runs on a background thread for most generations and can pause application threads briefly. Because it moves objects, references are updated automatically, which is why you cannot pin objects without cost.

    224. What are the GC generations and why do they exist?

    Gen 0 holds new short-lived objects, Gen 1 is a buffer for objects that survived one collection, and Gen 2 holds long-lived objects. The generational hypothesis is that most objects die young, so collecting Gen 0 frequently is cheap and efficient. Surviving objects are promoted, which is why keeping large graphs alive briefly is expensive.

    225. What is the Large Object Heap?

    Objects of 85,000 bytes or more are allocated on the Large Object Heap, which is collected only with Gen 2 and is not compacted by default. That leads to fragmentation and pause times when large buffers are allocated repeatedly. Reuse buffers with ArrayPool<T> instead of allocating big arrays per request.

    226. What typically triggers a Gen 2 collection?

    A Gen 2 collection happens when the Gen 0 or Gen 1 budget is exceeded after promotion, when memory pressure is high, or when GC.Collect is called explicitly. Since it must trace the whole heap, it is the most expensive collection, so GC.Collect in production code usually hurts throughput. Let the runtime tune itself unless you have measured evidence.

    227. What is the difference between managed and unmanaged resources?

    Managed resources are .NET objects the GC reclaims automatically; unmanaged resources are things like file handles, sockets, database connections, and native memory that the GC does not understand. Unmanaged resources must be released deterministically, which is what IDisposable and finalizers exist for. A managed wrapper can still hold an unmanaged handle, so disposing the wrapper matters.

    228. Why do finalizers make garbage collection more expensive?

    An object with a finalizer cannot be reclaimed in the collection that finds it unreachable; it is queued for the finalizer thread and promoted to an older generation. That means it survives at least one extra collection and often reaches Gen 2 before being collected, and the finalizer thread can become a bottleneck. This is the main reason to avoid finalizers and prefer IDisposable.

    229. What is IDisposable for?

    IDisposable provides deterministic cleanup of resources through Dispose, which the using statement and declaration call automatically, even when an exception occurs. It complements the GC, which only reclaims memory and at an unpredictable time. Anything holding a handle, connection, or unmanaged buffer should implement it.

    using var stream = File.OpenRead(path);
using var reader = new StreamReader(stream);
Console.WriteLine(reader.ReadLine());

    230. What does the full dispose pattern look like?

    The pattern has a public Dispose() that disposes managed and unmanaged resources, calls GC.SuppressFinalize(this), and a protected Dispose(bool disposing) that only touches managed objects when disposing is true. A finalizer calls Dispose(false) to release unmanaged handles only. Derived classes override Dispose(bool) and call base.Dispose(disposing).

    public void Dispose()
{
    Dispose(true);
    GC.SuppressFinalize(this);
}

protected virtual void Dispose(bool disposing)
{
    if (_disposed) return;
    if (disposing) _managed?.Dispose();
    _handle.Close();      // unmanaged
    _disposed = true;
}

    231. When do you actually need a finalizer?

    Only when the type owns unmanaged memory or a native handle that is not already wrapped by a safe handle, and you cannot rely on callers to dispose. If you wrap a SafeHandle, the finalizer is unnecessary because the safe handle finalizes itself. Finalizers should never block, allocate heavily, or touch other managed objects.

    232. What is the difference between the stack and the heap?

    The stack is a per-thread, LIFO region for method frames and locals; allocation is just a pointer move, and memory is reclaimed when the method returns. The heap is a shared region managed by the GC for objects whose lifetime is not tied to a single frame. Stack access is faster, but its size is limited and contents cannot outlive the call.

    233. Are structs always allocated on the stack?

    No. A struct is allocated inline wherever it is stored: on the stack as a local, inside its containing object on the heap as a field, or inside an array's contiguous buffer. Boxing a struct moves a copy to the heap. The correct mental model is "value types are stored inline, reference types are stored by reference".

    234. What are boxing and unboxing?

    Boxing wraps a value type in a heap object so it can be used as object or an interface; unboxing extracts the value with a type check and copy. Both cost an allocation or a cast, and unboxing to the wrong type throws InvalidCastException. Generics and IEquatable<T> exist largely to avoid this.

    int n = 42;
object boxed = n;      // boxing: new heap object
int back = (int)boxed; // unboxing: copy

    235. What is reflection and when is it appropriate?

    Reflection lets code inspect and invoke types, members, and attributes at runtime through System.Reflection. It is appropriate for frameworks, serializers, dependency injection containers, and plugin loading where types are unknown at compile time. In ordinary application code it usually signals a design that could be expressed with an interface instead.

    236. Why is reflection considered slow, and how do you mitigate it?

    Reflection bypasses static binding, allocates metadata objects, and often prevents inlining and devirtualization, so a naive per-call lookup is orders of magnitude slower than a direct call. The standard mitigation is to cache MethodInfo or PropertyInfo objects, or to compile delegates once with Expression.Lambda(...).Compile(). Source generators and compiled expressions are the fastest options for hot paths.

    private static readonly Func<Order, int> GetId =
    (Func<Order, int>)Delegate.CreateDelegate(
        typeof(Func<Order, int>),
        typeof(Order).GetProperty(nameof(Order.Id))!.GetGetMethod()!);

    237. What is an attribute?

    An attribute is declarative metadata attached to an assembly, type, or member and stored in the assembly's metadata table. It does not change behavior by itself; some consumer, often reflection or the compiler, must read and act on it. Examples include [Obsolete], [Serializable], and [JsonPropertyName].

    238. How do you read attributes at runtime?

    Call GetCustomAttribute<T> or GetCustomAttributes<T> on a Type, MemberInfo, or assembly, optionally with inherit: true to walk the base chain. Attribute instances are created on demand, so cache them if you read them frequently. Attribute.IsDefined is a cheaper existence check.

    var marker = method.GetCustomAttribute<ObsoleteAttribute>();
if (marker is not null)
    Console.WriteLine($"{method.Name} is obsolete: {marker.Message}");

    239. How do you define a custom attribute?

    Derive from Attribute, by convention name the class with an Attribute suffix, and expose the data through constructor parameters or settable properties with simple types. Keep it passive and immutable; the logic belongs to the code that reads it. Mark it [AttributeUsage] to control where it may be applied.

    [AttributeUsage(AttributeTargets.Class, AllowMultiple = false)]
public sealed class CachePolicyAttribute : Attribute
{
    public CachePolicyAttribute(int seconds) => Seconds = seconds;
    public int Seconds { get; }
}

    240. What does AttributeUsage control?

    AttributeTargets restricts where the attribute may be applied, AllowMultiple permits more than one instance on the same target, and Inherited controls whether derived types or overrides see it when queried with inherit: true. Getting these wrong produces either compile errors for valid usage or duplicate data at runtime. The defaults are all targets, single use, and inherited.

    241. What is the recommended way to write a catch block?

    Catch only exceptions you can actually handle, and catch the most specific type first, because a general clause earlier makes later ones unreachable. Handle means recover, translate, or add context; otherwise let the exception propagate. Log at the boundary where you stop the propagation, not at every level.

    242. Why is catching Exception generally discouraged?

    catch (Exception) also swallows OutOfMemoryException, StackOverflowException-adjacent failures, and programming bugs such as NullReferenceException, hiding defects and leaving the process in an unknown state. It is acceptable only at a top-level boundary that logs and terminates or returns a controlled error. Never catch and ignore silently.

    243. How should a custom exception be designed?

    Derive from Exception or a meaningful framework base, keep the three standard constructors, mark it [Serializable] when you need cross-process scenarios, and add only properties that carry useful context. Name it with an Exception suffix and make it immutable. Avoid deep hierarchies; one exception type per failure category is usually enough.

    public sealed class InsufficientFundsException : Exception
{
    public InsufficientFundsException(decimal requested, decimal available)
        : base($"Requested {requested} but only {available} is available.")
    {
        Requested = requested;
        Available = available;
    }

    public decimal Requested { get; }
    public decimal Available { get; }
}

    244. What is an inner exception used for?

    An inner exception preserves the original cause when you wrap it in a more meaningful exception, so diagnostics keep the full chain instead of only the outer message. Always pass it to the constructor and prefer throw new MyException("context", ex) over rethrowing. Logging tools print the inner chain, which is often where the real root cause lives.

    245. What is the difference between throw; and throw ex;?

    throw; rethrows the current exception and preserves its original stack trace, while throw ex; restarts the trace at the current line, destroying the evidence of where the failure actually happened. Inside a catch block, always use bare throw; unless you intentionally wrap. Wrapping with a new exception that carries ex as inner is the other correct option.

    246. Does finally run if the try block returns or throws?

    Yes, finally runs on normal completion, on return, on break/continue, and on an unhandled exception as it leaves the block. A return inside finally is legal but overrides the pending result and swallows exceptions, so it should never be used. Environment.FailFast and process termination are the main cases where it does not run.

    247. What are exception filters and when are they useful?

    Filters use catch (Exception ex) when (condition) to decide whether a handler applies before the stack is unwound, so if the condition is false the exception continues with its original trace intact. They are useful for inspecting error codes or state without catching and rethrowing. Filter expressions should be side-effect free and fast.

    catch (HttpRequestException ex) when (ex.StatusCode == HttpStatusCode.NotFound)
{
    return null; // treat only 404 as "absent"
}

    248. Are exceptions expensive in .NET?

    Throwing is expensive because the runtime captures a stack trace, unwinds frames, and allocates the exception object, and it prevents some optimizations in the containing method. Catching a thrown exception is far costlier than a normal branch. The cost is acceptable for genuinely exceptional conditions but not for expected, frequent outcomes.

    249. Should exceptions be used for normal control flow?

    No. Using exceptions for expected outcomes such as a failed lookup makes code slower, harder to read, and harder to debug because the debugger breaks on every throw. Prefer TryParse-style patterns, nullable returns, or result types for expected failures. Reserve exceptions for violated preconditions and truly exceptional states.

    250. Where should exceptions be logged and rethrown?

    Log once, at the layer that makes the decision: an infrastructure boundary, a message handler, or the application entry point. Intermediate layers should add context and rethrow with throw; so the stack trace stays intact. Logging at every level produces duplicate noise and inflates log volume.

    251. When would you use try/finally instead of using?

    using is the right choice whenever the resource implements IDisposable, because it is exception-safe and concise. Use an explicit try/finally for cleanup that is not an IDisposable call, such as resetting a flag, releasing a lock by hand, or restoring ambient state. Both compile to the same shape when a using is present.

    252. What does immutability mean for a type?

    An immutable type cannot change after construction: all state is set in the constructor and exposed read-only. This makes instances inherently thread-safe, safe to use as dictionary keys, and easy to reason about. Reusable types often combine immutable state with methods that return new instances.

    public sealed class Temperature
{
    public Temperature(double celsius) => Celsius = celsius;
    public double Celsius { get; }
    public Temperature WarmerBy(double d) => new(Celsius + d);
}

    253. What are the trade-offs of immutability?

    Immutability removes whole classes of concurrency and aliasing bugs and makes equality and caching trivial, but copying large objects on every change costs allocations and memory. Persistent collections and builders mitigate that by sharing structure. Use immutability for value-like objects and small messages, and measure before making large graphs immutable.

    254. What is the difference between a record, a class, and a struct?

    A class is a reference type with reference equality by default; a struct is a value type copied on assignment with field-wise value equality; a record is a reference type that adds value equality, a readable ToString, with expressions, and deconstruction. Records express data, classes express behavior and identity, and structs suit small, short-lived values. A record struct combines record semantics with value-type storage.

    255. When should you use a record struct instead of a record?

    Use record struct for small data values where avoiding heap allocation matters, and prefer readonly record struct to keep it immutable. Because structs are copied, large instances become expensive to pass around, so keep them small. A reference-type record is the safer default when the type is used polymorphically or is larger than a few fields.

    256. How does == differ from Equals for strings?

    For string, == is overloaded to compare values using ordinal (case-sensitive, culture-insensitive) comparison, so it matches Equals(string) behavior. Both differ from culture-aware comparison, which is what you want for user-facing sorting rather than equality. For explicit intent, call string.Equals(a, b, StringComparison.Ordinal).

    257. Why is comparing strings with ToLower() a bad idea?

    ToLower() allocates a new string on every comparison and depends on the current culture, which can produce different results on different machines; the Turkish "i" is the classic case. Allocating also turns an O(1) comparison into extra GC pressure inside loops. Use StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase or OrdinalIgnoreCase on a comparer instead.

    258. When should you use Ordinal versus culture-aware string comparison?

    Use Ordinal or OrdinalIgnoreCase for identifiers, keys, protocol values, file paths, and any programmatic equality, because it is fast and stable across cultures. Use CurrentCulture for sorting or comparing text displayed to users, where linguistic rules matter. Use InvariantCulture only for culture-independent, non-linguistic formatting and parsing.

    259. Why are strings immutable, and what is interning?

    String immutability makes them safe to share, hash, and use as dictionary keys, and allows the runtime to reuse literals. The CLR interns compile-time literals in a pool so identical literals are the same instance, which is why ReferenceEquals("a", "a") can be true. Concatenations and runtime-built strings are not interned, so never rely on reference equality for strings.

    260. What are the main pitfalls with DateTime?

    DateTime carries a Kind that is often ignored, so mixing Now (local) and UtcNow silently produces wrong offsets. Store and transmit UTC, convert to local only for display, and be explicit about DateTimeKind. Prefer DateTimeOffset when the original offset must be preserved.

    261. When should you use DateTimeOffset instead of DateTime?

    DateTimeOffset stores the UTC instant plus the original offset, so it round-trips the wall-clock time the user saw. Use it for timestamps that cross time zones or must be displayed as originally recorded, such as event schedules. DateTime in UTC is fine for pure storage and duration math.

    262. Why should durations be stored as TimeSpan rather than DateTime?

    TimeSpan represents an elapsed interval and has no calendar semantics, so adding and subtracting is unambiguous. DateTime arithmetic can be misleading across daylight-saving transitions and month lengths. Also be aware that DateTime.AddMonths is calendar-aware while AddDays(30) is not the same as adding a month.

    263. When should you use decimal instead of double?

    Use decimal for money and any value where exact base-10 representation matters, because it avoids binary rounding errors such as 0.1 + 0.2 not equalling 0.3. double is faster and has a much wider range, so it suits scientific and geometric calculations. Never compare floating-point values with ==; compare against a tolerance.

    Console.WriteLine(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3);            // false
Console.WriteLine(0.1m + 0.2m == 0.3m);        // true
Console.WriteLine(Math.Abs(a - b) < 1e-9);     // tolerant compare

    264. When is float or double the correct choice?

    Choose them for physical measurements, graphics, statistics, and machine learning where approximate values and speed matter more than exact decimal digits. float halves the memory of double and is common in GPU and large-array scenarios. Avoid both for currency and for identifiers you must compare exactly.

    265. What do checked and unchecked do?

    checked makes integral overflow and invalid conversions throw OverflowException; unchecked silently truncates or wraps. C# is unchecked by default at runtime, though constant expressions are checked at compile time. You can enable project-wide checking with <CheckForOverflowUnderflow> and use checked blocks for risky arithmetic.

    int max = int.MaxValue;
int wrapped = unchecked(max + 1);   // int.MinValue
int boom = checked(max + 1);        // throws

    266. What happens silently when arithmetic overflows by default?

    The value wraps around modulo 2^n, so a large positive number can become negative without any error, which is a subtle source of financial and index bugs. Division by zero for integers still throws DivideByZeroException regardless of the checked context. Use checked, wider types, or explicit range validation in code that must be correct.

    267. When should you use StringBuilder instead of string concatenation?

    Because strings are immutable, each concatenation allocates a new string and copies both operands, making a loop quadratic. StringBuilder mutates an internal buffer with amortized append cost, so use it for loops and dynamic building. For a fixed, small number of concatenations the compiler's string.Concat or interpolation is already optimal.

    var sb = new StringBuilder(capacity: 256);
foreach (var row in rows) sb.Append(row).Append('\n');
string text = sb.ToString();

    268. What is nameof for?

    nameof returns the simple name of a variable, type, or member as a compile-time constant string, so refactoring renames it automatically. It is ideal for argument exceptions, property change notifications, and logging. There is no runtime cost because the value is baked into the assembly.

    if (value is null) throw new ArgumentNullException(nameof(value));

    269. What does the default literal do?

    default produces the default value of the target type: null for reference types, zero for numeric types, and a zeroed value for structs. Target-typed, it avoids repeating the type name, so CancellationToken token = default; is idiomatic. For generic code, default! may be needed to satisfy nullable analysis when T is unconstrained.

    270. What is the is not pattern?

    is not is a negated pattern test that reads better than !(x is T) and works with type, constant, property, and relational patterns. It also enables the idiomatic guard if (value is not null). Combined with pattern variables it keeps the value strongly typed after the check.

    if (shape is not Circle { Radius: > 0 })
    throw new ArgumentException("Expected a positive circle.", nameof(shape));

    271. What is a switch expression?

    A switch expression returns a value, uses pattern-based arms, and requires exhaustiveness, so the compiler warns when a case is missing. It replaces verbose switch statements with assignment and is expression-bodied and immutable-friendly. Use _ as the discard arm for the default case.

    string label = status switch
{
    Status.New => "new",
    Status.Paid => "paid",
    Status.Cancelled => "cancelled",
    _ => throw new ArgumentOutOfRangeException(nameof(status))
};

    272. What is target-typed new?

    new() without a type name infers the type from the declaration or argument position, reducing repetition such as List<Order> orders = new();. It is compile-time only, so readability depends on the declared type being visible nearby. Avoid it when the target type is far away or ambiguous to a reader.

    273. What is the difference between const and static readonly?

    const is a compile-time literal baked into every consuming assembly, so changing it requires recompiling all callers; it is limited to primitives, strings, and null. static readonly is evaluated once at type initialization and is a runtime field, so it can hold any type and object references. Use const for true constants and static readonly for values computed at startup.

    274. What is an extension method?

    An extension method is a static method whose first parameter is marked this, allowing it to be called with instance syntax on that type. It must live in a static, non-nested, non-generic class and be visible via a using directive. It cannot access private members, so it extends the public surface only.

    public static class StringExtensions
{
    public static bool IsBlank(this string? s) =>
        string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(s);
}

    275. How does the compiler choose between an instance method and an extension method?

    Instance methods always win: an extension method is only considered when no applicable instance member exists. Among extensions, the best match by argument types wins, with closer namespaces and fewer using hops preferred over those from far-away namespaces. If two candidates are equally good, the call is ambiguous and does not compile.

    276. Can an extension method override a virtual method or be dispatched polymorphically?

    No. Extension methods are static and resolved from the compile-time type, so they never participate in virtual dispatch and cannot be overridden. A derived class declaring an instance method with the same name will hide the extension for callers typed as the derived class. If polymorphism is needed, use an interface or virtual member instead.

    277. What is array covariance?

    Arrays of reference types are covariant: string[] can be assigned to object[] because string derives from object. This is checked at runtime, not compile time, so writing an incompatible element throws ArrayTypeMismatchException. Value-type arrays are not covariant, so int[] cannot be treated as object[].

    object[] items = new string[1];
items[0] = 42; // compiles, throws ArrayTypeMismatchException

    278. Why did array covariance turn out to be a design flaw?

    It trades compile-time safety for compatibility with Java-style APIs, deferring a detectable type error to runtime. Every array write must therefore be type-checked, which costs performance on hot paths. Generic collections are invariant precisely to avoid this hole.

    279. Why can't you pass a List<Derived> where List<Base> is expected?

    Generic type arguments are invariant, because List<T> is mutable: the callee could add a Base that is not a Derived, corrupting the caller's list. Arrays allow it and pay for it with runtime checks. Use interfaces with out T such as IEnumerable<Base>, or project with Cast<Base>() to get a safe read-only view.

    280. What are the main differences between a struct and a class?

    A struct is a value type: it is copied on assignment, cannot inherit or be inherited, cannot be null unless nullable, and gets field-wise value equality by default. A class is a reference type with reference semantics, inheritance, and a finalizer option. Structs are good for small, immutable, short-lived data; classes for identity and behavior.

    Intermediate 340 questions

    Async and concurrency, language depth, dependency injection, and ASP.NET Core: routing, binding, validation, filters, auth, REST design.

    281. What does the compiler actually generate when you mark a method async?

    It generates a nested struct (or class) that implements IAsyncStateMachine, plus a stub method that creates and starts it. The state machine stores local variables as fields so they survive suspension, and it uses an AsyncTaskMethodBuilder to create the returned Task and to complete it later. Each await point becomes a state number plus a call to AwaitUnsafeOnCompleted that registers a continuation. When the awaited operation finishes, MoveNext is re-entered, the state number routes execution to the right place, and locals are restored from fields.

    [AsyncStateMachine(typeof(<GetAsync>d__1))]
public Task<string> GetAsync() {
    var sm = new <GetAsync>d__1();
    sm.<>t__builder = AsyncTaskMethodBuilder<string>.Create();
    sm.<>1__state = -1;
    sm.<>t__builder.Start(ref sm);
    return sm.<>t__builder.Task;
}

    282. Does async/await create a new thread?

    No. async/await is a scheduling construct, not a threading construct: the method runs on the caller's thread until the first incomplete await, and the continuation runs on whatever thread the awaited operation's scheduler provides. For I/O work, no thread is blocked while the operation is in flight, which is why async scales better than a blocking call on a thread pool thread. Extra threads appear only when the continuation has to be posted back to a specific context (UI, legacy ASP.NET) or when you explicitly call Task.Run.

    283. When would you choose ValueTask over Task?

    Use ValueTask when the operation frequently completes synchronously and is called in hot paths, because it avoids a heap allocation for the Task object. The benefit only materialises if you also avoid allocation elsewhere, so it is a micro-optimisation for measured hotspots such as a caching layer or a parser. Never use it when the result must be awaited multiple times, stored, or consumed concurrently, and never block on it with .Result.

    public ValueTask<int> ReadAsync(Memory<byte> buffer) {
    if (_position < _cached.Length)
        return new ValueTask<int>(Copy(_cached, buffer));
    return ReadSlowAsync(buffer);
}

    284. Why is it unsafe to await a ValueTask more than once?

    A ValueTask is a struct that may wrap either a result or a pooled IValueTaskSource, and the underlying source is recycled by the producer as soon as it is consumed. A second await can therefore observe corrupted state or an already-released object, and concurrent awaits are outright undefined behaviour. If you need multi-consumption semantics, call AsTask() once to get a regular Task and await that.

    ValueTask<int> vt = ReadAsync(buf);
Task<int> t = vt.AsTask();   // safe to await many times
int a = await t, b = await t;

    285. What exactly does ConfigureAwait(false) change?

    It tells the awaiter not to capture the current SynchronizationContext or TaskScheduler, so the continuation may run on any thread pool thread instead of being marshalled back. It does not change which thread starts the method, and it does not prevent the continuation from running on the original thread by coincidence. The only guarantee is the absence of a forced post back.

    286. Do you still need ConfigureAwait(false) in ASP.NET Core application code?

    In ASP.NET Core there is no SynchronizationContext, so ConfigureAwait(false) is a no-op for deadlock avoidance and is mostly noise in application code. It still matters in reusable libraries, which may be consumed by WPF, WinForms, or legacy ASP.NET where a context exists. A common pragmatic rule is: use it in library code, skip it in app code unless you rely on the minor scheduling optimisation.

    287. How should a CancellationToken be propagated through an async call chain?

    Accept a CancellationToken as the last parameter of every async method that can be cancelled, give it a default only on public entry points, and pass the same token down to every awaitable call. Never wrap it in a new CancellationTokenSource unless you genuinely need to combine or time-limit the operation. Do not store the token in a field or a singleton, since it is tied to one logical operation.

    public async Task<Report> BuildAsync(int id, CancellationToken ct) {
    var data = await _repo.LoadAsync(id, ct);
    await _cache.SetAsync(id, data, ct);
    return Map(data);
}

    288. How do you implement a timeout that also cancels the underlying work?

    Create a linked token source with CancelAfter, pass its token to the operation, and translate the resulting OperationCanceledException into a timeout result when the timeout token is the one that fired. In .NET 8 and later, CancellationTokenSource.CancelAfter plus checking ct.IsCancellationRequested is the idiomatic approach; Task.WaitAsync(TimeSpan) is a shortcut when you only need to stop waiting and are willing to let the underlying work continue.

    using var cts = CancellationTokenSource.CreateLinkedTokenSource(ct);
cts.CancelAfter(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(3));
try { return await _client.GetAsync(url, cts.Token); }
catch (OperationCanceledException) when (!ct.IsCancellationRequested) {
    throw new TimeoutException("Upstream call timed out.");
}

    289. What is the difference between Task.WhenAll and Task.WhenAny?

    WhenAll returns a single task that completes when every input task has completed, which makes it the tool for parallel fan-out where all results are needed. WhenAny returns a task whose result is the first input task to finish, which is the tool for timeouts, racing replicas, or reacting to whichever completes first. Both are non-blocking, unlike WaitAll and WaitAny, and neither starts the tasks for you.

    290. How does Task.WhenAll report multiple failures?

    The returned task faults with the first exception and exposes all failures through its Exception.InnerExceptions collection, so awaiting it with a plain await rethrows only the first one. If you catch, inspect task.Exception or catch the AggregateException explicitly to see every failure. Tasks that never faulted still completed their work, so partial side effects may already have happened.

    var tasks = ids.Select(id => _svc.ProcessAsync(id));
try { await Task.WhenAll(tasks); }
catch {
    var errors = tasks.Where(t => t.IsFaulted)
                      .SelectMany(t => t.Exception!.InnerExceptions);
    _log.LogError("Failures: {Count}", errors.Count());
}

    291. How do you await a heterogeneous set of tasks and collect all results?

    Start the tasks first, then await each one individually; awaiting sequentially is fine because the work is already running concurrently. This gives type-safe results without casting and preserves per-call error handling. Task.WhenAll is a poor fit when the result types differ.

    var userTask = _users.GetAsync(id, ct);
var ordersTask = _orders.ForUserAsync(id, ct);
var flagsTask = _flags.GetAsync(id, ct);
var user = await userTask;
var orders = await ordersTask;
var flags = await flagsTask;

    292. Where does an exception thrown inside an async method surface?

    It is captured and stored on the returned Task, then rethrown at the await site with its original stack trace preserved via ExceptionDispatchInfo. Code before the first await still throws synchronously if the method is not async, but an async method always returns a faulted task instead. Using await rather than .Wait or .Result preserves the original exception type instead of wrapping it in AggregateException.

    293. Why are async void methods dangerous?

    An async void method returns nothing to await, so the caller cannot know when it finishes or whether it failed. Exceptions escape to the SynchronizationContext or the thread pool and typically crash the process, and the method cannot be composed, retried, or cancelled by the caller. Use async Task everywhere except UI event handlers.

    294. If async void is so risky, why is it allowed at all?

    It exists for the event handler pattern, where a delegate signature such as EventHandler is fixed to void and cannot be changed by the subscriber. The acceptable pattern is a thin async void handler that immediately delegates to an async Task method and catches everything, so nothing escapes. In libraries, offer an async Task overload instead of an async void API.

    private async void OnSaveClicked(object? s, EventArgs e) {
    try { await SaveAsync(); }
    catch (Exception ex) { ShowError(ex); }
}

    295. How does await foreach work with IAsyncEnumerable?

    The compiler calls GetAsyncEnumerator, then awaits MoveNextAsync before each iteration and disposes the enumerator asynchronously at the end. This lets the producer yield items as they arrive, for example from a database reader or a paginated API, without buffering the whole sequence. A CancellationToken can be passed via WithCancellation and is checked by the producer.

    await foreach (var row in _source.ReadRowsAsync(ct).WithCancellation(ct)) {
    Process(row);
}

    296. What does WithCancellation on IAsyncEnumerable actually do?

    It attaches the token to the enumerable so that the enumerator receives it in GetAsyncEnumerator(cancellationToken) and can observe it in MoveNextAsync. The producer must honour the token; WithCancellation alone guarantees nothing. It is also the standard way to flow the caller's cancellation into an await foreach loop.

    297. Can you control buffering when consuming an IAsyncEnumerable?

    Yes, via System.Linq.Async or the built-in ChannelReader.ReadAllAsync plus operators such as WithCancellation; some providers expose options for batch size and prefetch. On the producing side, you decide the buffering by how many items you yield ahead of consumption, and a bounded channel is the usual way to apply backpressure. There is no global buffer setting on the interface itself.

    298. Why does calling .Result on an async method deadlock in some applications?

    The synchronous caller blocks its thread while holding the SynchronizationContext, and the async continuation is queued back to that same single-threaded context, which can never run because the thread is blocked. UI applications and classic ASP.NET exhibit this. Fixing it means making the call chain async all the way, and never mixing blocking waits with async code.

    // Deadlock in a UI app: the context is captured and blocked
var data = GetDataAsync().Result;

// Correct
var data = await GetDataAsync();

    299. There is no SynchronizationContext in ASP.NET Core, so is .Result safe there?

    It will not deadlock the same way, but it is still harmful: it blocks a thread pool thread, can starve the pool under load, and wraps exceptions in AggregateException. Under legacy thread-affinity assumptions or inside code that does need the context, it can still hang. Treat sync-over-async as a defect regardless of the hosting model.

    300. Compare Thread, ThreadPool and Task.

    A Thread is a dedicated OS thread with full control over apartment state and priority, which is rarely needed and expensive to create. The ThreadPool is a shared pool of worker threads suited to short CPU-bound work, with heuristics that inject threads slowly when the queue is starved. Task is a promise of future completion scheduled on the pool by default, composing cleanly with async I/O and continuations, which makes it the default choice for both I/O and parallelism.

    301. What are the symptoms of thread pool starvation and how do you fix it?

    Latency climbs while CPU stays low, throughput collapses, and the pool injects a new thread roughly every 500 ms, so requests queue behind blocking calls. The usual cause is sync-over-async or long blocking work on pool threads. Fix it by making the path truly async, moving long blocking work to dedicated threads or a queue, and using the ThreadPool starvation metrics available in .NET 6+ to confirm.

    302. Compare lock, Monitor, Mutex and SemaphoreSlim.

    lock is syntactic sugar for Monitor.Enter/Exit and provides mutual exclusion within a single process with a highly optimised fast path. Mutex is a kernel object that can be system-wide and named, at the cost of much higher overhead, and it is thread-affine. SemaphoreSlim is a lightweight in-process counter that limits concurrency, supports WaitAsync, and is the right primitive when you need an async-compatible lock.

    303. Why can't you await inside a lock statement?

    Monitor is thread-affine: the lock must be released by the same thread that acquired it, but an await can resume on a different thread and the compiler forbids the construct outright. Even if it were allowed, holding a lock across an I/O await would block every other caller for the entire duration. Use SemaphoreSlim.WaitAsync or restructure so the critical section contains no awaits.

    304. How do you implement an async lock?

    Use SemaphoreSlim with an initial count of one and always release it in a finally block so an exception cannot leak the permit. Dispose the semaphore when the owning object is disposed, and never re-enter it reentrantly, because a SemaphoreSlim is not reentrant and will deadlock.

    private readonly SemaphoreSlim _gate = new(1, 1);

public async Task<T> RunAsync<T>(Func<Task<T>> work, CancellationToken ct) {
    await _gate.WaitAsync(ct);
    try { return await work(); }
    finally { _gate.Release(); }
}

    305. When is Interlocked enough instead of a lock?

    Interlocked is enough for single-variable atomic operations such as increment, exchange, compare-and-swap and additive updates, and it is lock-free and fast. As soon as the invariant spans two variables or requires a read-modify-write across multiple steps, you need a lock or an immutable snapshot. Interlocked on a long is atomic on 64-bit platforms, but compound invariants still need protection.

    Interlocked.Increment(ref _counter);
Interlocked.CompareExchange(ref _state, Running, Idle);

    306. What is the difference between volatile, Interlocked and lock?

    volatile only prevents reordering and caching of a single field's reads and writes, providing visibility but no atomicity for compound operations. Interlocked provides atomic read-modify-write with full memory barriers for one location. lock provides mutual exclusion for a whole critical section and also acts as a full fence, which is what you need when several fields must change together.

    307. What is the classic pitfall with ConcurrentDictionary.GetOrAdd?

    The value factory is not guaranteed to run only once: two threads can both miss and both invoke it, and only one result is stored. That is harmless for pure factories but dangerous when the factory performs I/O, registers a subscription, or has side effects. Use Lazy<T> as the value type when exactly-once initialisation is required.

    var lazy = _cache.GetOrAdd(key,
    k => new Lazy<Client>(() => CreateClient(k), LazyThreadSafetyMode.ExecutionAndPublication));
var client = lazy.Value;

    308. Are compound operations on ConcurrentDictionary atomic?

    Each individual method is thread-safe, but a sequence of them is not, so read-then-write patterns have races. AddOrUpdate, GetOrAdd and TryUpdate are atomic per key, but conditions spanning multiple keys or a check-then-act across calls need external synchronisation. Also remember that enumeration is a moving snapshot and does not lock the dictionary.

    309. What are Channels and when do you use them?

    System.Threading.Channels is a modern async producer/consumer queue with a single-reader or multi-reader and single-writer or multi-writer configuration. Producers call WriteAsync, consumers await ReadAllAsync or ReadAsync, and a bounded capacity applies backpressure instead of unbounded memory growth. It is the default choice for in-process pipelines, background queues, and decoupling ingestion from processing.

    var ch = Channel.CreateBounded<WorkItem>(new BoundedChannelOptions(1000) {
    FullMode = BoundedChannelFullMode.Wait,
    SingleReader = true
});
await ch.Writer.WriteAsync(item, ct);
await foreach (var i in ch.Reader.ReadAllAsync(ct)) Handle(i);

    310. How does a consumer know a channel has no more data?

    The producer calls Writer.Complete(), which signals completion; ReadAllAsync then ends gracefully after draining remaining items, and a direct ReadAsync throws ChannelClosedException. Always complete the writer in a finally block so consumers do not hang forever when a producer faults. Completion can carry an exception that surfaces to the reader.

    311. Why prefer Channel over BlockingCollection?

    BlockingCollection is synchronous, so an async producer must block a thread to add items, and it relies on blocking waits that do not scale. Channels expose WriteAsync/ReadAsync and integrate naturally with cancellation and await foreach, and they have lower overhead and finer configuration. BlockingCollection remains fine for purely synchronous legacy pipelines.

    312. How does Parallel.ForEachAsync differ from Parallel.ForEach?

    Parallel.ForEachAsync takes an async body with a CancellationToken and honours a MaxDegreeOfParallelism limit, awaiting each asynchronous iteration instead of blocking a thread per item. Parallel.ForEach is for CPU-bound synchronous work and will simply block threads on async delegates, which defeats the purpose. ForEachAsync is the correct tool for a bounded fan-out of I/O calls.

    await Parallel.ForEachAsync(ids, new ParallelOptions {
    MaxDegreeOfParallelism = 8, CancellationToken = ct
}, async (id, token) => await _svc.SyncAsync(id, token));

    313. When is PLINQ a good idea?

    PLINQ pays off for CPU-bound work over large in-memory collections where each element is expensive enough to dwarf the partitioning and merging overhead. It is a poor fit for I/O-bound work and for small collections, where the parallel overhead dominates. Use WithDegreeOfParallelism and AsOrdered deliberately, and remember that exceptions surface as an AggregateException.

    314. What are the trade-offs of AsOrdered and AsUnordered in PLINQ?

    AsOrdered preserves source order at the cost of extra buffering and coordination, reducing speedup. AsUnordered removes that constraint and lets results stream as they complete, which is faster but nondeterministic. Only ask for ordering if downstream logic actually depends on it, and consider ordering once at the end instead of after every operator.

    315. How do you choose between Channel, BlockingCollection and TPL Dataflow for a producer/consumer design?

    Use a Channel for a simple async pipeline with backpressure and minimal ceremony, which covers most application needs. BlockingCollection suits synchronous code that already blocks threads. TPL Dataflow is the right choice when you need a graph of blocks with different parallelism, batching, joining and completion propagation, at the cost of more complexity.

    316. What is the difference between System.Threading.Timer, System.Timers.Timer and PeriodicTimer?

    System.Threading.Timer is the lowest-level pool-based callback timer with no UI marshalling. System.Timers.Timer wraps it with events and an AutoReset option, mainly for legacy component use. PeriodicTimer (since .NET 6) is the async-friendly choice: await WaitForNextTickAsync in a loop, so ticks cannot overlap and cancellation is natural.

    using var timer = new PeriodicTimer(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(5));
while (await timer.WaitForNextTickAsync(ct))
    await PollAsync(ct);

    317. Why can callbacks from System.Threading.Timer overlap?

    The timer queues a callback on the thread pool every period regardless of whether the previous callback finished, so a slow callback overlaps with the next one. Guard against reentrancy with a flag, an Interlocked exchange, or a SemaphoreSlim with wait-zero semantics, or switch to PeriodicTimer which serialises ticks by design. Overlapping callbacks also mean concurrent access to shared state.

    318. What is TaskCompletionSource used for?

    It lets you expose an existing callback-based or event-based API as an awaitable Task, bridging code that completes later into async code. You set the result, exception or cancellation exactly once, and the awaiter resumes accordingly. Always use RunContinuationsAsynchronously in library code to avoid running the awaiting continuation inline on the completing thread.

    var tcs = new TaskCompletionSource<Message>(
    TaskCreationOptions.RunContinuationsAsynchronously);
_connection.OnMessage += m => tcs.TrySetResult(m);
using var reg = ct.Register(() => tcs.TrySetCanceled(ct));
return await tcs.Task;

    319. Why is fire-and-forget with Task.Run discouraged in ASP.NET Core?

    The request-scoped services and HttpContext may be disposed while the background task is still running, and the task's exceptions are unobserved. There is also no backpressure, so a burst of requests can flood the thread pool. Use a hosted background service with a bounded queue or Channel instead.

    320. What does AsyncLocal flow across and when does it not?

    AsyncLocal values flow along async continuations and through ExecutionContext, so a value set before an await is visible after it, including in Task.Run work started from that context. They do not flow to threads started manually with new Thread, nor backwards to the parent, and mutations inside a child scope are not visible to the caller. This is the mechanism behind logging scopes and Activity correlation.

    321. What is ExecutionContext.SuppressFlow for?

    It prevents the current ExecutionContext, including AsyncLocal values such as Activity and logging scope, from being captured when starting a task or thread. Use it for background work that must not inherit request-scoped ambient state, and dispose the returned object promptly to restore flow. Suppressing flow can also improve throughput by avoiding context copies in very hot paths.

    322. What is a SynchronizationContext and how does await interact with it?

    It is an abstraction representing where a continuation should run, such as the UI thread or the classic ASP.NET request context. When you await, the awaiter captures SynchronizationContext.Current and posts the continuation there unless ConfigureAwait(false) suppresses it. ASP.NET Core installs no context, so continuations run on the pool.

    323. What is the difference between Task.Yield and Task.Delay(0)?

    Task.Yield forces an asynchronous yield, always producing an incomplete awaitable that schedules the rest of the method back onto the current context or scheduler, which is useful to keep a UI responsive. Task.Delay(0) returns an already-completed task, so awaiting it does not yield at all and the method continues synchronously. Use Yield for fairness, not Delay(0).

    324. What are the risks of registering many callbacks on a CancellationToken?

    Each registration allocates and is retained by the token source until disposed, so long-lived tokens with per-operation registrations leak memory and slow cancellation. Always dispose the registration, for example with a using on the returned CancellationTokenRegistration. For repeated operations, prefer a linked token created per operation and disposed with it.

    using var reg = ct.Register(static s => ((Socket)s!).Dispose(), socket);

    325. How do you safely convert a synchronous method into an async one without breaking callers?

    Add an async path alongside the existing one, keeping the synchronous signature as a thin wrapper only for compatibility, and move I/O to truly asynchronous APIs rather than wrapping everything in Task.Run. Async-all-the-way is the goal because Task.Run over blocking I/O merely moves the blocked thread. Where a sync API must exist, document it and keep it out of ASP.NET Core request paths.

    326. What kinds of generic constraints exist and what do they enable?

    Constraints are class/struct/unmanaged/notnull, base class or interface constraints, new(), and since C# 11 the allows ref struct anti-constraint for generic math and span scenarios. Their real value is that they let the compiler bind members at compile time, avoiding boxing and runtime casts. Prefer the narrowest constraint that satisfies the code, because every constraint narrows the API's usability.

    public static T Max<T>(T a, T b) where T : IComparable<T>
    => a.CompareTo(b) >= 0 ? a : b;

    327. What does the new() constraint require and what are its limits?

    new() requires a public parameterless constructor and lets you write new T(), which is essential for factories and generic pools. It excludes abstract types and interfaces, and it cannot be combined with struct in the same constraint list. For types with private constructors you must fall back to Activator.CreateInstance or a factory delegate.

    328. How does generic math work in modern .NET?

    Static abstract members in interfaces allow operators to be expressed as constraints, so a single generic method can add or compare any numeric type without boxing or dynamic. INumber<T> unifies the numeric types and enables algorithms written once for int, double, decimal or a custom type. The JIT can inline the static calls, so performance matches hand-written overloads.

    public static T Sum<T>(IEnumerable<T> values) where T : INumber<T> {
    T total = T.Zero;
    foreach (var v in values) total += v;
    return total;
}

    329. What do out and in mean on a generic type parameter?

    out declares covariance: the type parameter appears only in output positions, so IEnumerable<string> can be used as IEnumerable<object>. in declares contravariance: it appears only in input positions, so IComparer<object> can be used as IComparer<string>. Variance is only allowed on interfaces and delegates, and only for reference types.

    330. Why is List<object> not assignable to List<string> even though object is a base type?

    Generic classes are invariant, so List<T> exposes T in both input and output positions and the compiler cannot guarantee safety. Allowing the assignment would let you add an object that is not a string and later read it as one. Interfaces such as IEnumerable<out T> and IReadOnlyList<out T> express covariance precisely because they are read-only.

    331. How does variance apply to delegates such as Func and Action?

    Func<in T, out TResult> is contravariant in its input and covariant in its result, so a Func<object, string> can be used where Func<string, object> is expected. Action<in T> is contravariant only. This is what allows a handler written for a base EventArgs to be registered for a derived event.

    Func<object, string> f = o => o.ToString()!;
Func<string, object> g = f;  // valid: in/out variance

    332. What is an expression tree and what makes it different from a delegate?

    An Expression<TDelegate> is a data structure describing code as nodes rather than compiled instructions, so a consumer can inspect, rewrite or translate it. Delegates are opaque executable code and cannot be analysed. Expression trees are the basis of IQueryable providers, ORMs, and dynamic rule engines, and they can be turned into delegates with Compile().

    333. When is Expression<Func<T,bool>> required instead of Func<T,bool>?

    When the consumer must translate the predicate instead of executing it, which is exactly the IQueryable case where EF Core converts nodes into SQL. Passing a Func to a queryable overload silently switches to client evaluation or fails to compile. Conversely, use a plain Func inside in-memory LINQ to avoid the translation and compilation cost.

    334. What kinds of code break EF Core query translation?

    Custom methods with no translator, arbitrary string manipulation, dynamic or reflection-based expressions, and some date/time and collection operations either throw or force client evaluation. The reliable pattern is to keep the predicate translatable and move anything else after AsEnumerable or after the final projection. Always check the generated SQL for unexpected client-side evaluation.

    335. What is the cost of Expression.Compile and how do you mitigate it?

    Compile emits IL at runtime, which is expensive relative to normal method calls, so compiling per request is a real cost. Cache the compiled delegate keyed by the expression, or use EF Core's compiled queries for hot paths. Compile(preferInterpretation: true) can be faster for one-off use where JIT cost dominates.

    336. How does IQueryable differ from IEnumerable?

    IEnumerable represents a sequence you enumerate in memory, so every operator runs as CLR code. IQueryable carries an expression tree plus a provider, so operators build a query that the provider translates, typically into SQL executed remotely. The distinction decides whether filtering happens in the database or after materialising everything.

    337. Why is deferred execution a source of bugs?

    A LINQ query is a recipe, not a result: it re-executes every time it is enumerated, so the data may have changed and side effects may run repeatedly. Enumerating the same query twice in one method means two database round-trips. Materialise with ToList when you need a stable snapshot or will iterate more than once.

    var q = ctx.Orders.Where(o => o.Total > 100);
var count = q.Count();      // query 1
var list = q.ToList();      // query 2, possibly different data

    338. Where exactly should AsEnumerable be placed in a query?

    Place it after the last operation that can be translated and before operations that cannot, so the database does the filtering and projection and only the reduced result set is processed in memory. Placing it too early pulls the whole table into memory. AsNoTracking is a separate concern and does not affect where execution happens.

    339. What is the difference between tracking and no-tracking queries in EF Core?

    Tracking queries register entities in the change tracker so updates are detected on SaveChanges, at the cost of identity resolution and memory. AsNoTracking is faster and cheaper for read-only endpoints and is safe when you never mutate or rely on identity fixup. Use AsNoTrackingWithIdentityResolution when you need one instance per key without change tracking.

    340. What problem does Span<T> solve?

    Span<T> is a stack-only view over contiguous memory, whether an array, stack memory or unmanaged buffer, so you can slice and parse without allocating substrings or copies. It unifies the API surface for string, array and native memory processing. It is a ref struct, so it cannot be boxed, stored on the heap, or used across await.

    ReadOnlySpan<char> span = "2026-10-22".AsSpan();
var year = span[..4];
if (int.TryParse(year, out var y)) Console.WriteLine(y);

    341. Why does Memory<T> exist when Span<T> is faster?

    Memory<T> is a heap-friendly view that can be stored in fields, captured by lambdas and used across await, then converted to Span only when you actually process it. Async APIs therefore accept Memory<T> while synchronous hot paths accept Span<T>. The conversion is cheap but must not outlive the await.

    public async Task ReadAsync(Memory<byte> buffer, CancellationToken ct) {
    await _stream.ReadAsync(buffer, ct);
    var span = buffer.Span;   // valid only for this synchronous block
    Process(span);
}

    342. Why can't a Span be used inside an async method?

    Span is a ref struct that may point at stack memory, and the compiler must guarantee it never escapes to the heap; an async state machine lifts locals into a heap object across awaits, which would break that guarantee. The same restriction applies to capturing a Span in a lambda or using it as a generic type argument. Use Memory<T> or a scoped synchronous helper instead.

    343. How do you use ArrayPool correctly?

    Rent a buffer sized to your need, use only the requested length, and always return it in a finally block with the exact same array. Do not return a different array, do not keep references after returning, and clear only when the buffer held sensitive data. Pooling removes GC pressure for large transient buffers but adds correctness risk if the return is skipped.

    var buf = ArrayPool<byte>.Shared.Rent(4096);
try { var read = await s.ReadAsync(buf.AsMemory(0, 4096), ct); Use(buf, read); }
finally { ArrayPool<byte>.Shared.Return(buf); }

    344. What are the practical limits of a ref struct?

    It cannot be boxed, stored in a field of a class, used as a generic type argument (before the allows ref struct anti-constraint), captured in a lambda, or used across await or yield. Those restrictions are exactly what allow the runtime to guarantee it never escapes the stack. Use them for short-lived, high-performance buffer code.

    345. When is stackalloc safe?

    Only for small, fixed, bounded sizes, because stack space is limited (typically around 1 MB per thread) and an overflow terminates the process rather than throwing. Never stackalloc based on untrusted or unbounded input; threshold-check and rent from ArrayPool instead. Since C# 7.2 stackalloc can target Span<T>, which makes slicing and bounds checks safe.

    Span<byte> buffer = stackalloc byte[256];   // small and constant
if (length > 256) return RentAndProcess(length);

    346. What does the in modifier do on a parameter?

    It passes a struct by reference read-only, avoiding the copy cost of a large struct while promising the callee will not mutate it. The compiler enforces immutability and may create a defensive copy when the argument is not readonly. Use it for structs larger than roughly a couple of machine words on hot paths.

    public static double Length(in Vector3 v) => Math.Sqrt(v.X * v.X + v.Y * v.Y + v.Z * v.Z);

    347. What is the difference between ref readonly return and in parameter?

    ref readonly on a return exposes a reference to existing storage that callers can read but not write, avoiding a copy of a large struct. in restricts the callee instead, promising not to modify the argument. Both are performance tools that leak implementation details about storage lifetime, so use them sparingly and document them.

    348. How do you decide between a struct and a class?

    Choose a struct for small, immutable, value-semantic data that is short-lived and not frequently boxed, such as coordinates, money or identifiers. Choose a class when you need identity, inheritance, nullability, or the object is large or long-lived, since copying a big struct is expensive. Never make a mutable struct, because copies are easy to create accidentally.

    349. What do readonly struct and readonly members protect against?

    A readonly struct guarantees no field can be mutated after construction, which removes defensive copies when it is passed as in or accessed through a readonly reference. Without readonly, the compiler copies the struct to call a non-readonly member on a readonly location, silently hurting performance. This is why immutable structs should be declared readonly.

    350. What are the equality semantics of a record?

    Records generate value equality over all instance fields and properties, plus consistent GetHashCode, == and != operators, ToString and Deconstruct. Equality uses the runtime type, so a record and its derived record with the same values are not equal. Use record struct for a value type with the same generated members.

    351. How do init accessors and with expressions work together?

    init allows a property to be set only during object initialisation or from a constructor, making immutability the default after construction. The with expression performs a shallow copy and then applies the listed changes, which is how you produce a modified immutable value. Because the copy is shallow, nested reference members are still shared with the original.

    var a = new Order(Id: 1, Total: 100m);
var b = a with { Total = 120m };   // a is unchanged

    352. What do required members add over a constructor?
