    required forces callers to set a property in an object initialiser or via SetsRequiredMembers, and it works with object initialisers and JSON deserialisation where a constructor cannot express every combination. It removes the need for dozens of constructor overloads while keeping the compiler's guarantee. The setter still exists, so it protects against omission, not against later mutation.

    353. How do primary constructors on a class differ from those on a record?

    On a record, primary constructor parameters become public init-only properties and participate in equality. On a plain class, the parameters are just captured values available to the body; they are not properties unless you declare them, so they are not serialised or compared. This makes class primary constructors useful mainly for dependency injection without boilerplate.

    public sealed class Orders(IOrderRepo repo, ILogger<Orders> log) {
    public Task<Order?> GetAsync(int id) => repo.FindAsync(id);
}

    354. What is a property pattern and when does it simplify code?

    A property pattern matches an object by testing members, optionally with nested patterns, and it replaces chains of if-statements with casting and null checks. It is null-safe: if the value is null the pattern simply does not match. It reads well for decision logic over DTOs and domain results.

    if (order is { Status: OrderStatus.Paid, Total: > 1000m })
    FlagForReview(order);

    355. How do positional patterns work?

    A positional pattern deconstructs the value using an accessible Deconstruct method or the record-generated one, then matches each element. This makes records and tuples natural to match on and keeps the code declarative. Non-record types need a Deconstruct overload with out parameters to participate.

    var label = point switch {
    (0, 0) => "origin",
    (var x, 0) => $"on X axis at {x}",
    var (x, y) => $"({x},{y})"
};

    356. What are list patterns used for?

    List patterns match sequences by position, length and slice, so you can express validation of small collections without index arithmetic. They work on any type with a Length or Count and an indexer, and require the type to be countable and indexable. They are most useful for parsers, protocol headers, and argument validation.

    string Describe(int[] v) => v switch {
    [] => "empty",
    [var only] => $"single {only}",
    [var first, .., var last] => $"{first}..{last}"
};

    357. Does a switch expression guarantee exhaustiveness?

    Only for closed sets the compiler understands, such as enums and sealed hierarchies; otherwise you need a discard arm, and without one the compiler emits a warning and the code throws at runtime for unmatched values. A discard arm is also where you should throw rather than silently return a default. Non-exhaustive switches over enums are a common source of production surprises after adding a new member.

    358. When would you use record struct instead of record class?

    Use record struct for small, value-semantic data that benefits from avoiding heap allocation, such as keys, coordinates or small DTOs inside loops. Because records are copy-on-assign, a large record struct still pays copy costs. record class remains the default for domain entities with identity and reference semantics.

    359. What is the purpose of static abstract interface members?

    They let an interface declare static members that implementing types must supply, enabling generic code to call operators, Parse, or factory-like statics through a type parameter. This is how generic math works and how you can write algorithms constrained to "any type with a TryParse". Previously such patterns required reflection or a separate factory abstraction.

    360. What are default interface methods and what problems do they introduce?

    They let an interface provide a method body, so implementers do not have to, which enables evolving an interface without breaking every implementation. The catch is that the default is only reached through the interface, so a class method with the same name does not override it, and calling through the class type skips it. Multiple interfaces with conflicting defaults require an explicit implementation in the class.

    361. How does the single responsibility principle look in real C# code?

    It means one reason to change, so a class mixes fewer concerns: an OrderService orchestrates, a repository persists, a mapper translates, a validator validates. The tell is a class name with "and" in it or a test that needs a database to check formatting. Splitting too aggressively is also a smell, so group by axis of change rather than by line count.

    362. Give a concrete violation of the Liskov substitution principle in C#.

    A Square deriving from Rectangle and overriding both dimensions breaks code that sets width and height independently, because the invariant of the base type no longer holds. The classic C# symptom is a derived override that throws NotSupportedException for a base operation. Prefer composition or a shared abstraction that does not promise the broken behaviour.

    public class Rectangle { public virtual int W { get; set; } public virtual int H { get; set; } }
public class Square : Rectangle {
    public override int W { set => base.W = base.H = value; }   // breaks callers
}

    363. How does the dependency inversion principle relate to DI containers?

    DIP says high-level policy should depend on abstractions, not on concrete infrastructure, which allows both to vary independently. A DI container is only a mechanism for supplying those abstractions at runtime and is not required by the principle. You can satisfy DIP with constructor injection and hand-built composition roots, which is often clearer for small applications.

    364. Explain singleton, scoped and transient lifetimes and their risks.

    Singleton is created once per root container and is shared by everything, so it must be thread-safe and must not hold request state. Scoped is created once per request or scope, which is right for DbContext and per-request caches. Transient is created per resolution, which is cheap for stateless services but can surprise you when a transient is injected into several consumers.

    365. What is a captive dependency and how does it happen?

    A captive dependency occurs when a longer-lived service (singleton) holds a shorter-lived one (scoped or transient), so the short-lived instance is effectively promoted and never released per scope. A typical case is a singleton capturing a scoped DbContext, which then throws or shares state across requests. The fix is to inject IServiceScopeFactory or a scoped-only refactoring, never to widen the DbContext's lifetime.

    public sealed class Cache(IServiceScopeFactory factory) : IHostedService {
    using var scope = factory.CreateScope();
    var db = scope.ServiceProvider.GetRequiredService<AppDb>();
}

    366. How do you detect captive dependencies and scope misuse?

    Build the host with ValidateScopes and ValidateOnBuild enabled in non-production environments, which makes the container throw when a singleton resolves a scoped service or a scope is used after disposal. In ASP.NET Core, the Development environment enables scope validation by default. Additionally, register DbContext as scoped and let integration tests exercise real request paths.

    Host.CreateDefaultBuilder()
    .UseDefaultServiceProvider(o => {
        o.ValidateScopes = true;
        o.ValidateOnBuild = true;
    });

    367. What is the difference between Add, TryAdd and AddEnumerable?

    Add always appends another registration, so the last one wins for a single resolve. TryAdd registers only if no service of that type is present, which lets a library provide a default that the application can override. TryAddEnumerable registers only if no descriptor with the same implementation type exists, preventing duplicate handlers when a module is scanned twice.

    368. When would you use keyed services instead of separate interfaces?

    Keyed services, added in .NET 8, let one abstraction have multiple registrations distinguished by a key, which suits payment providers, storage backends, or per-tenant implementations. Resolve with GetRequiredKeyedService or the [FromKeyedServices] attribute. Separate marker interfaces remain clearer when the variants have genuinely different contracts.

    builder.Services.AddKeyedSingleton<IPaymentGateway, StripeGateway>("stripe");
app.MapPost("/pay", ([FromKeyedServices("stripe")] IPaymentGateway gw) => gw.Pay());

    369. How do you implement the factory pattern idiomatically with DI?

    Register a factory delegate or a small factory class that resolves from IServiceProvider at call time, so the created object can have its own dependencies resolved by the container. Avoid new-ing services that have dependencies, because you lose decoration and lifetime management. Prefer a typed factory interface over injecting IServiceProvider everywhere.

    370. How does the strategy pattern map onto DI in ASP.NET Core?

    Define the strategy interface, register several implementations, and inject IEnumerable<IStrategy> into a resolver that selects one by a key or predicate. This keeps selection logic in one place and makes adding a strategy a matter of adding a class and a registration. It removes switch statements from the consumer.

    public sealed class Router(IEnumerable<IExporter> exporters) {
    public IExporter For(string format) =>
        exporters.First(e => e.Format == format);
}

    371. How do you add cross-cutting behaviour without touching every class?

    Use the decorator pattern: implement the same interface, wrap the inner implementation, and add logging, caching or validation around the call. Registration order determines nesting, and libraries such as Scrutor can apply decorators automatically. This is preferable to inheritance and keeps the original class open for extension but closed for modification.

    public sealed class CachedCatalog(ICatalog inner, IMemoryCache cache) : ICatalog {
    public Task<Item?> GetAsync(int id, CancellationToken ct) =>
        cache.GetOrCreateAsync($"cat:{id}", e => {
            e.AbsoluteExpirationRelativeToNow = TimeSpan.FromMinutes(5);
            return inner.GetAsync(id, ct);
        });
}

    372. When is the adapter pattern the right choice in C#?

    Use it to wrap a third-party or legacy API behind an interface your domain owns, so the rest of the code depends on your abstraction rather than on a vendor's shape. The adapter translates types, exceptions and semantics, which also gives you a single place to add retries or logging. It is the standard remedy for a leaky external SDK in the application core.

    public sealed class StripeAdapter(StripeClient api) : IPaymentGateway {
    public async Task<Receipt> ChargeAsync(Money amount, CancellationToken ct) {
        var r = await api.Charges.CreateAsync(amount.Cents, ct);
        return new Receipt(r.Id, r.Status == "succeeded");
    }
}

    373. Explain the difference between IOptions, IOptionsSnapshot and IOptionsMonitor.

    IOptions is a singleton that reads configuration once, so it never sees later changes. IOptionsSnapshot is scoped and re-reads per request, which is right for request-scoped code but must not be injected into singletons. IOptionsMonitor is a singleton that always returns the current value through .CurrentValue and raises OnChange, which is what long-lived services should use.

    374. Why does injecting IOptionsSnapshot into a singleton fail?

    IOptionsSnapshot is registered as scoped, so resolving it from a singleton creates a captive dependency that either throws under scope validation or silently keeps a stale instance. Use IOptionsMonitor, which is designed for singleton consumers, or inject IOptions if the value never changes. This is one of the most common DI lifetime mistakes.

    375. How do named options work and when do you need them?

    Named options let several configurations of the same type coexist, for example two storage accounts or three external APIs, selected by name. You register them with Configure(name, ...) and resolve with IOptionsMonitor.Get(name). They avoid inventing a wrapper class per variant.

    services.Configure<ApiOptions>("billing", cfg.GetSection("Billing"));
services.Configure<ApiOptions>("shipping", cfg.GetSection("Shipping"));

var billing = provider.GetRequiredService<IOptionsMonitor<ApiOptions>>().Get("billing");

    376. How do you validate options at startup rather than at first use?

    Chain ValidateDataAnnotations and Validate with a predicate when registering, then call ValidateOnStart so a bad configuration fails the host immediately instead of throwing on the first request. This turns a runtime surprise into a deployment failure, which is far cheaper to diagnose. Validation runs for every named instance.

    services.AddOptions<SmtpOptions>()
    .Bind(cfg.GetSection("Smtp"))
    .ValidateDataAnnotations()
    .Validate(o => o.Port > 0, "Port must be positive")
    .ValidateOnStart();

    377. How does configuration precedence work across providers?

    Providers are consulted in reverse registration order, so the last one added wins for a duplicated key. The default host adds appsettings.json, then appsettings.{Environment}.json, then environment variables, then command line, which is why environment variables override files. Understanding this order explains most "why is my setting ignored" incidents.

    378. How do you bind configuration to a strongly typed object, and when does it reload?

    Call Bind or GetSection().Get<T>() once at composition time, or use the options pattern so lifetimes and change tracking are handled for you. Binding a plain object does not track later changes; only IOptionsMonitor or an explicit reload token gives you reload semantics. Weakly typed IConfiguration access scattered through the code is a maintainability smell.

    379. How should secrets be handled across environments?

    Never commit secrets; use user secrets for local development, environment variables or a managed vault for deployed environments, and a managed identity to avoid stored credentials entirely. Configuration keys are read the same way regardless of the provider, so the code does not change. Rotate secrets and keep them out of logs and exception messages.

    380. Why use ILogger instead of Console.WriteLine?

    ILogger gives structured, categorised, levelled output that can be filtered and routed per provider without changing code, and it flows ambient context such as scopes and trace identifiers. Console output is unstructured, unfilterable, and untestable. Logging abstraction also lets tests substitute a fake logger to assert on behaviour.

    381. What are logging scopes and what do they add?

    A scope attaches ambient key-value data to every log entry written inside it, such as a request id, tenant or order id, without repeating those values in each call. Providers that support scopes include them in the output, which is what makes correlated logs possible. Scopes rely on AsyncLocal, so they flow across awaits but not to manually created threads.

    using (_logger.BeginScope(new Dictionary<string, object> { ["OrderId"] = id })) {
    _logger.LogInformation("Processing order");
}

    382. How do log levels and categories interact with filtering?

    Each log entry has a level and a category, normally the full type name, and rules can set a minimum level per category or provider. This lets you keep Information globally while raising a noisy framework namespace to Warning. Configure rules in appsettings under Logging:LogLevel, remembering that the most specific rule wins.

    383. Why prefer LoggerMessage or the source-generated logging API over string interpolation?

    Interpolated strings allocate and format even when the level is disabled, whereas LoggerMessage skips all work when the level is off and caches the template parsing. The source generator produces strongly typed, allocation-light methods from a partial class. In hot paths this difference is measurable.

    [LoggerMessage(Level = LogLevel.Information, Message = "Order {OrderId} processed in {Ms} ms")]
private static partial void LogProcessed(ILogger logger, int orderId, long ms);

    384. What is the difference between IHostedService and BackgroundService?

    IHostedService is the minimal contract with StartAsync and StopAsync, giving full control over lifetime. BackgroundService is an abstract base that runs ExecuteAsync on a background task and wires StopAsync to a cancellation token, which covers most long-running loops. Implement IHostedService directly when you need custom start/stop semantics or a short one-shot startup task.

    385. What happens if ExecuteAsync throws in a BackgroundService?

    The exception faults the background task and, depending on the host configuration and .NET version, stops the host, so an unhandled exception in a loop silently kills your worker. Always catch inside the loop, log, and decide whether to continue or stop. .NET 6+ also supports BackgroundServiceExceptionBehavior to configure this explicitly.

    protected override async Task ExecuteAsync(CancellationToken ct) {
    while (!ct.IsCancellationRequested) {
        try { await WorkAsync(ct); }
        catch (OperationCanceledException) when (ct.IsCancellationRequested) { break; }
        catch (Exception ex) { _log.LogError(ex, "Worker iteration failed"); }
        await Task.Delay(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(10), ct);
    }
}

    386. How does a BackgroundService shut down gracefully?

    The host cancels the token passed to ExecuteAsync and waits for StopAsync to complete within the shutdown timeout, so honour the token in every await and exit the loop promptly. Register cleanup in finally blocks or IAsyncDisposable, and avoid blocking the token in a way that ignores cancellation. Long-running items should be checkpointed so a restart does not lose or repeat work.

    387. How does a BackgroundService use scoped services?

    It receives IServiceScopeFactory and creates a scope per unit of work, resolving scoped services like DbContext inside that scope, because the service itself is a singleton. Reusing one scope for the whole process lifetime effectively makes the DbContext a singleton and breaks change tracking. Keep scopes short and dispose them with using.

    388. How do you queue work for background processing without losing items?

    Expose a singleton queue backed by a bounded Channel, enqueue from request handlers, and have a BackgroundService consume it with await foreach. Bounded capacity gives backpressure, and completion signals drain. This pattern is fine for single-node workloads but is not durable, so use a real broker when items must survive a crash.

    public sealed class Queue {
    private readonly Channel<int> _ch = Channel.CreateBounded<int>(100);
    public ValueTask EnqueueAsync(int id, CancellationToken ct) => _ch.Writer.WriteAsync(id, ct);
    public IAsyncEnumerable<int> ReadAllAsync(CancellationToken ct) => _ch.Reader.ReadAllAsync(ct);
}

    389. Why does creating a new HttpClient per request cause problems?

    HttpClient is disposable and holds an HttpMessageHandler with connection pools; disposing it per request forces new TCP and TLS handshakes, causing socket exhaustion under load (TIME_WAIT accumulation). A single static client fixes that but prevents DNS refresh and centralised configuration. IHttpClientFactory solves both by pooling handlers and rotating them.

    390. What does IHttpClientFactory actually manage?

    It pools the underlying HttpMessageHandler, recycling each after HandlerLifetime (two minutes by default) so DNS changes are picked up, while handing out a cheap new HttpClient per resolve that can be disposed safely. It also centralises base address, default headers and resilience policies per named client. Disposing the client returned by the factory does not dispose the pooled handler.

    services.AddHttpClient<IWeatherApi, WeatherApi>(c => {
    c.BaseAddress = new Uri("https://api.example.com/");
    c.Timeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(5);
});

    391. Compare typed clients, named clients and raw IHttpClientFactory usage.

    A typed client is a class that takes HttpClient in its constructor, giving compile-time discoverability and a natural place for endpoint methods; it is the best default. A named client resolves by string and is convenient when configuration is dynamic or you only need a configured client inline. Injecting IHttpClientFactory directly should be reserved for cases where the client must be created lazily per call.

    392. Where should resilience policies be attached?

    Attach them to the HttpClient pipeline via AddResilienceHandler or AddPolicyHandler so every call through that client gets consistent behaviour, rather than sprinkling try/catch around call sites. Keep retry counts low, use exponential backoff with jitter, and only retry idempotent operations. Policies should be configured per dependency because each upstream has different failure characteristics.

    393. What is dangerous about blindly retrying an HTTP call?

    Retrying a non-idempotent POST can duplicate a payment or an order, and retrying a slow dependency amplifies load during an outage. Retry only on transient conditions (timeouts, 5xx, 429) with bounded attempts and jitter, and require idempotency keys for writes. A retry policy is not a substitute for a timeout.

    394. Explain the states of a circuit breaker.

    Closed lets calls through and counts failures; when the failure threshold is crossed it trips to Open and fails fast for a cool-down period. Then it moves to Half-Open, allowing a limited number of probe calls, and closes again on success or reopens on failure. The point is to stop hammering a failing dependency so it can recover and to fail fast for callers.

    395. How does a Polly timeout policy differ from HttpClient.Timeout?

    HttpClient.Timeout is a hard limit for the whole request including content reading and produces a TaskCanceledException that is hard to distinguish from caller cancellation. A resilience timeout can be per attempt, composes with retries, and lets you distinguish timeout from cancellation. The usual setup is a short per-attempt timeout plus an overall budget.

    396. How do you compose retry, timeout and circuit breaker in Polly v8?

    Register them in one resilience pipeline in a deliberate order: the outermost policy sees the operation first, so timeout usually sits outside retry per attempt, and the circuit breaker wraps the inner calls. Polly v8 uses ResiliencePipelineBuilder with AddRetry, AddTimeout and AddCircuitBreaker, replacing the older policy-wrap syntax. Order matters far more than the individual settings.

    services.AddResiliencePipeline("http", b => b
    .AddRetry(new RetryStrategyOptions { MaxRetryAttempts = 3, Delay = TimeSpan.FromMilliseconds(200), UseJitter = true })
    .AddCircuitBreaker(new CircuitBreakerStrategyOptions { FailureRatio = 0.5, BreakDuration = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30) })
    .AddTimeout(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(5)));

    397. What problem do the bulkhead and hedging strategies address?

    A bulkhead caps concurrent calls to one dependency so a slow upstream cannot consume all resources and take down unrelated features. Hedging sends a duplicate request after a delay and takes the first success, reducing tail latency at the cost of extra load. Both are advanced tools that should follow measurement, not be added by default.

    398. How do you choose between System.Text.Json and Newtonsoft.Json?

    System.Text.Json is the default in modern .NET, is faster and allocation-light, and is what ASP.NET Core uses, so prefer it for new code. Newtonsoft.Json still wins for legacy behaviours, very flexible converters, or libraries that depend on it. Mixing both in one request pipeline creates confusing, inconsistent output.

    399. When is System.Text.Json source generation useful?

    Source generation moves serialisation metadata to compile time, which removes reflection and start-up cost, and it is required for trimming and Native AOT scenarios. It also surfaces unsupported constructs at build time rather than at runtime. Use JsonSerializerContext and pass its type info to the serialiser.

    [JsonSerializable(typeof(Order))]
internal partial class AppJson : JsonSerializerContext { }

var json = JsonSerializer.Serialize(order, AppJson.Default.Order);

    400. How do you serialise a polymorphic hierarchy with System.Text.Json?

    Since .NET 7 you can annotate the base type with JsonDerivedType attributes or a type discriminator, which writes a discriminator property and restores the concrete type on deserialisation. Without it, deserialising into the base type yields only the base members. The discriminator must be stable, because it becomes part of your wire contract.

    [JsonDerivedType(typeof(CardPayment), "card")]
[JsonDerivedType(typeof(BankPayment), "bank")]
public abstract record Payment(decimal Amount);

    401. Where do you configure JSON options for ASP.NET Core?

    Use AddControllers().AddJsonOptions for MVC and ConfigureHttpJsonOptions for minimal APIs; they are separate option objects, which is a frequent source of "my setting has no effect". Property naming policy, converters and reference handling should be set once at the composition root. Note that minimal APIs reuse the same options for both requests and responses.

    402. What are the trade-offs of using AutoMapper?

    It removes repetitive mapping code and centralises conventions, which pays off with many similar DTOs. The costs are indirection that is hard to debug, runtime configuration errors that surface late, and mappings that silently pick the wrong member when names drift. ProjectTo does give efficient SQL projections, but manual mapping or a compile-time generator is often clearer and faster.

    403. What is the case for manual or source-generated mapping instead of reflection-based mapping?

    Hand-written mappings are explicit, debuggable, and fail at compile time when a property is renamed, and source generators such as Mapperly give the same safety with less typing. They also avoid reflection cost and work under trimming and AOT. The downside is more code, which is usually cheaper than a hidden mapping bug in production.

    404. How do DataAnnotations validation and FluentValidation differ?

    DataAnnotations are attribute-based, declared on the model, and integrate automatically with MVC model validation, which suits simple rules. FluentValidation keeps rules in a separate class, supports conditional and cross-property rules, dependency injection in validators, and testable rule units. Choose FluentValidation once rules involve conditionals, collections or external checks.

    405. Where should validation live in a layered application?

    Input-shape validation belongs at the boundary, so malformed requests are rejected cheaply, while business invariants belong in the domain so they hold regardless of entry point. Duplicating a rule in both places is acceptable only if the domain remains authoritative. Relying solely on UI or controller validation lets invalid state in through other callers.

    406. How should validation failures be returned from an API?

    Return 400 with a ProblemDetails payload, or ValidationProblemDetails with an errors dictionary keyed by field, so clients can map messages to inputs. Keep the shape consistent across the API and avoid leaking internal exception text. ASP.NET Core produces this automatically when ModelState is invalid with [ApiController].

    407. What are the limits of IMemoryCache?

    It is per-process, so it is not shared across instances and cannot be invalidated cluster-wide, which matters for horizontally scaled apps. It also stores references, so mutable cached objects can be mutated by consumers. Set size limits and expirations, and treat cached values as immutable snapshots.

    408. What is a cache stampede and how do you prevent it with IMemoryCache?

    When a popular entry expires, many concurrent requests miss simultaneously and all hit the database, which can overload it. GetOrCreateAsync does not deduplicate the factory across concurrent callers by default. Serialise the refresh with a per-key SemaphoreSlim or a Lazy<Task<T>> entry, and consider staggered or sliding expiration.

    private readonly SemaphoreSlim _gate = new(1, 1);

public async Task<Report> GetAsync(int id, CancellationToken ct) {
    if (_cache.TryGetValue(id, out Report? cached)) return cached!;
    await _gate.WaitAsync(ct);
    try {
        if (_cache.TryGetValue(id, out cached)) return cached!;
        var fresh = await _repo.LoadAsync(id, ct);
        _cache.Set(id, fresh, TimeSpan.FromMinutes(10));
        return fresh;
    } finally { _gate.Release(); }
}

    409. How do you design cache keys and invalidation?

    Derive keys from a stable, versioned template that includes every input that changes the result, such as tenant and entity id, so a schema change can be handled by bumping the version prefix. Prefer short TTLs plus explicit eviction on writes over complex dependency graphs. Document who invalidates what, because unowned invalidation logic is where stale data bugs live.

    410. What does IDistributedCache give you and what does it not?

    It abstracts a shared cache such as Redis or SQL Server so all instances see the same entries, using byte[] or string values. It is a low-level primitive with no tagging, no atomic operations and no stampede protection, so you build those or use a richer client. Serialisation is your responsibility, along with key design.

    411. When is a hybrid cache appropriate?

    Hybrid caching combines a local in-memory layer with a distributed backplane so hot reads avoid network hops while the distributed layer keeps instances coherent. .NET 9's HybridCache adds stampede protection, tagging and configurable serialisation on top of that idea. It is worth the complexity when read latency and database load dominate.

    412. How do you decide what to abstract for testability?

    Abstract the things that are slow, nondeterministic or external, such as clocks, I/O, message buses and third-party APIs, and keep pure logic concrete. Injecting an interface for every class produces ceremony without test value, since a class with no dependencies can be tested directly. Test through the smallest seam that makes the behaviour deterministic.

    413. How do you make time-dependent code testable?

    Inject TimeProvider (or an equivalent clock abstraction) and call GetUtcNow and CreateTimer instead of DateTime.UtcNow and new Timer. Tests can then use FakeTimeProvider to advance time deterministically without sleeping. This removes the flaky delay-based tests that plague retry and expiry logic.

    public sealed class TokenService(TimeProvider clock) {
    public bool IsExpired(Token t) => t.ExpiresAt < clock.GetUtcNow();
}

    414. How do you test async code without flakiness?

    Await the operations instead of using sleeps, use deterministic fakes for time and I/O, and control concurrency with TaskCompletionSource so you can assert intermediate states. Use timeouts as a safety net that fails the test rather than as a synchronisation mechanism. Flaky async tests usually indicate a real race or a missing await in the code under test.

    415. How does WebApplicationFactory support integration testing?

    It boots the real application in memory with TestServer, so middleware, routing, DI and serialisation are exercised without network calls. You can replace registrations with fakes via ConfigureTestServices, and the returned HttpClient drives real requests. This catches wiring and configuration bugs that unit tests cannot see.

    public class ApiTests(WebApplicationFactory<Program> f) : IClassFixture<WebApplicationFactory<Program>> {
    [Fact]
    public async Task Health_returns_ok() {
        var res = await f.CreateClient().GetAsync("/health");
        res.EnsureSuccessStatusCode();
    }
}

    416. Why is lock(this) or locking on a string considered bad practice?

    Locking on this or a Type exposes the lock object to external code, which can take the same lock and deadlock you or block unrelated callers. Locking on a string literal is worse because literals are interned and shared across the whole process. Use a private readonly object dedicated to the lock, or a private readonly Lock field in .NET 9+.

    private readonly object _gate = new();   // not this, not typeof(X), not "lock"

    417. When is ReaderWriterLockSlim better than lock?

    It pays off when reads vastly outnumber writes and the critical section is long enough to matter, since concurrent readers proceed in parallel. For short or mixed workloads its overhead exceeds a plain Monitor and it is slower. It is also not async-compatible, and it is not reentrant by default.

    418. How do you throttle concurrency to a fixed number of parallel operations?

    Use Parallel.ForEachAsync with MaxDegreeOfParallelism, or a SemaphoreSlim acquired around each operation when you need finer control over scheduling or dynamic limits. Avoid unbounded Task.WhenAll over thousands of items, which floods the pool and downstream services. Always release the semaphore in finally so a fault does not leak permits.

    var gate = new SemaphoreSlim(8);
var tasks = ids.Select(async id => {
    await gate.WaitAsync(ct);
    try { await _api.SendAsync(id, ct); }
    finally { gate.Release(); }
});
await Task.WhenAll(tasks);

    419. How do you avoid deadlocks when code needs more than one lock?

    Establish a global ordering of lock objects and always acquire them in that order, so a cycle cannot form. Keep critical sections short and free of calls into unknown code that might take other locks. Where possible eliminate nested locks entirely by copying data out, releasing, then acting.

    420. When would you use ConcurrentQueue instead of a Channel?

    ConcurrentQueue is a lock-free collection for short, synchronous handoffs, for example accumulating completed results that are drained later. It has no async waiting and no built-in backpressure, so a consumer must poll or use its own signalling. When you need awaitable reads and a bounded capacity, a Channel is the better abstraction.

    421. When are immutable collections the right choice?

    They suit state shared across threads without locks, especially when readers must see a consistent snapshot and updates are relatively rare. Writes copy the structure and are more expensive than mutable adds, but reads are lock-free. They pair well with a volatile field or Interlocked swap holding the current version.

    422. What are the Lazy<T> thread-safety modes?

    ExecutionAndPublication is the default and guarantees the factory runs once with locking, which is nearly always what you want. PublicationOnly may run the factory several times and keep the first published value, used when the factory is safe to duplicate. None is unsynchronised and only appropriate for single-threaded access.

    423. Is double-checked locking safe in C#?

    Yes, if the shared field is volatile or access is done via Interlocked/Volatile.Read, because the write must publish the fully constructed object before the reference becomes visible. Without that, another thread can observe a non-null reference to a partially constructed object. In modern C#, Lazy<T> expresses the same intent more safely.

    private volatile Config? _config;

public Config Current => _config ??= LoadConfig();   // safe because the field is volatile

    424. What is SpinWait for and when is spinning appropriate?

    SpinWait burns CPU cycles in a tight loop waiting for a very short condition, avoiding the context switch cost of blocking. It only makes sense when the wait is expected to be shorter than a context switch and threads are not oversubscribed. Long or unpredictable waits must block or await, since spinning wastes cores and can starve other work.

    425. Why can't a constructor be async, and what do you do instead?

    async requires a return type of void or a task-like type, and a constructor has neither, so the object would be handed out before its asynchronous initialisation completes. Use an async factory method or a static CreateAsync, or move the initialisation into an explicit InitializeAsync called by the composition root. Lazy async initialisation inside members is another option.

    public sealed class Client {
    private Client() { }
    public static async Task<Client> CreateAsync(CancellationToken ct) {
        var c = new Client();
        await c.ConnectAsync(ct);
        return c;
    }
}

    426. When do you need IAsyncDisposable and await using?

    When disposal itself performs asynchronous work, such as flushing a stream or closing a connection, so a synchronous Dispose would have to block. await using calls DisposeAsync, which is often used with IAsyncEnumerable and channels. Types that own both sync and async resources should implement both interfaces.

    await using var conn = new DbConnection(cs);
await conn.OpenAsync(ct);

    427. When is an exception filter better than a catch-and-rethrow?

    An exception filter (catch ... when (...)) inspects the exception before unwinding the stack, so if the filter returns false the stack trace is untouched and the cost is lower. It also keeps the handler logic declarative and avoids catching exceptions you cannot handle. Filters must be side-effect free and cheap.

    catch (HttpRequestException ex) when (ex.StatusCode == HttpStatusCode.NotFound) {
    return null;
}

    428. How do you rethrow an exception without destroying its stack trace?

    Use a bare throw inside the catch block, which preserves the original trace, rather than throw ex which resets it to the current line. To rethrow later or outside the catch, capture with ExceptionDispatchInfo.Capture and call Throw(). This matters because the original frames are usually the only useful diagnostic information.

    catch (Exception ex) { _log.LogError(ex, "failed"); throw; }

    429. Why should you call Flatten on an AggregateException?

    Nested awaits and Task.WhenAll can wrap inner AggregateExceptions, producing deeply nested trees that are awkward to inspect. Flatten collapses them into a single level so InnerExceptions lists the real failures. This is a common need when reporting all errors from a batch operation.

    430. Should a library method wrap synchronous work in Task.Run?

    No. A library should expose async only where it is genuinely async, and let the caller decide whether to offload CPU-bound work, because Task.Run steals a pool thread and hides the cost. Wrapping blocking I/O in Task.Run is especially harmful since it fakes asynchrony while still blocking a thread. Document the true nature of the method instead.

    431. How does a correlation id flow across async calls and service boundaries?

    Inside a process, store it in AsyncLocal or rely on Activity/ILogger scopes, which flow with the execution context. Across HTTP boundaries it must be put in a header explicitly and re-established on the receiving side, usually through middleware or an HttpClient delegating handler. Baggage in OpenTelemetry automates propagation when tracing is configured.

    432. How do you register an open generic service in DI?

    Register the open generic type against the open generic interface, and the container closes it on demand for each type argument. This is how IRepository<T> or IValidator<T> patterns are wired without registering every closed type. The implementation must satisfy the constraints on the type parameter.

    services.AddScoped(typeof(IRepository<>), typeof(EfRepository<>));

    433. In what order does IEnumerable<T> resolution return registrations?

    In registration order, which makes the order of Add calls semantically meaningful for pipelines such as middleware handlers or validators. This is convenient but fragile if registrations come from several modules, so document any order dependence. Many frameworks provide an explicit Order property precisely because relying on registration order is brittle.

    434. When should you inject IServiceScopeFactory instead of IServiceProvider?

    Inject IServiceScopeFactory when a long-lived service needs to create and dispose a scope for short-lived work, because it makes the scope ownership explicit and hard to forget. IServiceProvider alone allows Resolve without a scope, which silently captures scoped services. Both are service-locator flavoured, so keep their use at infrastructure edges.

    435. Who is responsible for disposing services the container created?

    The container owns and disposes anything it instantiated, at the end of the scope or when the root is disposed, so you must not dispose injected dependencies yourself. Disposing an injected DbContext or HttpClient breaks other consumers of the same instance. Only dispose objects you created, or register a factory and let the container track them.

    436. Why is the service locator pattern discouraged?

    Resolving dependencies from a container inside a class hides its requirements, so the constructor no longer documents what the type needs and tests must configure a container. It also hides lifetime bugs, because any scope can be reached. Constructor injection keeps dependencies explicit and failures discoverable at composition time.

    437. How do you apply decorators to many registrations at once?

    Scan the assembly with Scrutor or manual reflection, then use Decorate to wrap each matching registration while preserving its lifetime and order. This avoids writing a decorator registration per service and keeps cross-cutting concerns in one place. Be careful that the decorator is not itself scanned as a target, which produces infinite nesting.

    services.Scan(s => s.FromAssemblyOf<IOrderService>()
    .AddClasses(c => c.AssignableTo<IOrderService>())
    .AsImplementedInterfaces().WithScopedLifetime());

services.Decorate<IOrderService, LoggingOrderService>();

    438. How does the open/closed principle show up in C# without over-engineering?

    New behaviour should arrive as a new implementation rather than an edit to a switch inside an existing class, so the extension point is an interface resolved through DI. That is the practical version of OCP; it does not mean never editing a file. Applying it everywhere produces needless abstraction, so reserve it for areas known to vary.

    439. What does interface segregation look like in a real codebase?

    Split a fat service interface into role interfaces such as IOrderReader and IOrderWriter so consumers depend only on what they use, which also makes fakes trivial. The smell is an interface where every test fake must throw NotImplementedException for half the members. Smaller interfaces also improve the ability to decorate selectively.

    440. Compare anemic and rich domain models.

    An anemic model is a bag of properties with logic living in services, which is simple and fine for CRUD-heavy applications but lets invariants be bypassed. A rich model puts behaviour and invariant enforcement in the entities, which suits complex domains but complicates persistence and mapping. The right choice depends on how much domain logic actually exists, not on dogma.

    441. What is primitive obsession and how do records help?

    Primitive obsession is representing domain concepts as string or decimal everywhere, so a customer id and an order id are interchangeable and no validation is enforced. A small value object such as a record struct with a validating factory makes the concept explicit and type-safe. The compiler then prevents mixing unrelated ids.

    public readonly record struct CustomerId(Guid Value) {
    public static CustomerId New() => new(Guid.NewGuid());
    public override string ToString() => Value.ToString("N");
}

    442. When should you return a result object instead of throwing?

    Use a result type for expected, recoverable outcomes that callers must handle, such as validation failures or a not-found lookup, because exceptions are for exceptional conditions and are costly. Exceptions remain appropriate for programming errors and truly unexpected faults. Mixing both without a convention is what makes codebases confusing.

    public readonly record struct Result<T>(bool Ok, T? Value, string? Error) {
    public static Result<T> Success(T v) => new(true, v, null);
    public static Result<T> Fail(string e) => new(false, default, e);
}

    443. What makes a retried operation idempotent in practice?

    An operation is idempotent when repeating it produces the same result, which you achieve with an idempotency key persisted by the server, upserts instead of inserts, or conditional writes. For HTTP this typically means a client-supplied request key with a short TTL, plus returning the original response on replay. Without it, retries duplicate side effects.

    444. What problem does the outbox pattern solve?

    It solves the dual-write problem: writing to the database and publishing a message are two systems, so one can succeed and the other fail. The message is stored in an outbox table within the same transaction as the state change, and a background relay publishes it with at-least-once delivery. Consumers must then be idempotent.

    445. How do you keep feature flags from becoming untestable complexity?

    Wrap flag evaluation behind a small interface, keep flag names and defaults in one place, and treat each combination that matters as an explicit test case. Remove flags shortly after rollout instead of letting them accumulate, because stale flags create untested branches. Inject the flag provider so tests can force each state deterministically.

    446. How do you verify that a Span or ValueTask optimisation actually helped?

    Measure before and after with BenchmarkDotNet, checking allocations as well as time, because these optimisations trade readability for allocation savings. A micro-benchmark in isolation can mislead if the real workload is dominated by I/O. Revert the change when the numbers do not justify the complexity.

    [MemoryDiagnoser]
public class ParseBench {
    [Benchmark(Baseline = true)] public int Substring() => Parse(_s.Substring(0, 4));
    [Benchmark] public int Span() => Parse(_s.AsSpan(0, 4));
}

    447. When is defining a custom exception type justified?

    When callers need to catch it specifically and act differently, or when it carries structured data that helps handling, such as the conflicting resource id. Otherwise a built-in exception with a clear message is enough and does not expand the API surface. Custom exceptions should be serialisable, documented, and never used for control flow that happens routinely.

    448. How do you keep a complex multi-step async workflow readable?

    Extract each step into a named method that returns a meaningful result, so the orchestrator reads as a sequence of intentions, and pass one CancellationToken through all of them. Compensating actions belong in explicit try/catch or a saga-style coordinator rather than deep nesting. If the flow has many branches and retries, model it as a state machine instead of straight-line code.

    449. How do you bound memory in a long-running consumer?

    Use a bounded queue or channel so producers block when consumers fall behind, and process in batches with a fixed maximum. Avoid unbounded caches and unbounded Task.WhenAll fan-out, both of which grow with load. Monitor queue depth and memory as first-class metrics so the bound is visible before an OOM.

    450. What concurrency red flags do you look for in a code review?

    Blocking calls such as .Result and .Wait in async paths, async void outside event handlers, fire-and-forget tasks without error handling, and shared mutable state on singletons. I also look for cancellation tokens that are accepted but never passed on, and lock objects that are publicly reachable. Each of these is cheap to spot and expensive to debug in production.

    451. What is the generic host in ASP.NET Core and how does it differ from the older WebHost?

    The generic host (Host.CreateDefaultBuilder) is a general-purpose host that can run web and non-web workloads, while WebHost was web-only. The generic host exposes a single DI container, configuration, logging and lifetime management for any hosted service, with ConfigureWebHostDefaults layering Kestrel and the ASP.NET Core pipeline on top.

    var builder = WebApplication.CreateBuilder(args);
builder.Services.AddHostedService<Worker>();
var app = builder.Build();
app.MapGet("/", () => "ok");
app.Run();

    452. What does WebApplication.CreateBuilder do for you by default?

    It creates a pre-configured builder that already wires configuration (appsettings.json, environment variables, command line), logging providers, Kestrel, routing, and the default service provider. It also sets the content root and environment from environment variables such as ASPNETCORE_ENVIRONMENT. You then register your own services and call Build() to get a WebApplication that acts as both host and pipeline.

    453. How do you configure Kestrel endpoints and limits in ASP.NET Core?

    Endpoints and limits are configured through KestrelServerOptions, either in code via builder.WebHost.ConfigureKestrel or through the Kestrel section of configuration. You can set listen options (URLs, HTTPS certificates, HTTP protocols) and limits such as max request body size, header size and concurrent connections.

    builder.WebHost.ConfigureKestrel(o =>
{
    o.Limits.MaxRequestBodySize = 10 * 1024 * 1024;
    o.ListenAnyIP(5001, l => l.UseHttps());
});

    454. When would you host ASP.NET Core behind IIS or a reverse proxy instead of exposing Kestrel directly?

    Kestrel is fully capable of serving traffic directly, but a reverse proxy adds TLS termination, centralized certificate management, request buffering, load balancing and an extra security boundary. Behind IIS the app runs with the ASP.NET Core Module and Kestrel listens on a loopback or named pipe. In that setup you must enable forwarded headers so the app sees the original scheme and client IP.

    455. What is the ASP.NET Core middleware pipeline and how is it composed?

    Middleware are components registered in order on IApplicationBuilder, each receiving a RequestDelegate for the next component. Requests flow down the chain and responses flow back up, so the order of registration determines behaviour. A middleware can short-circuit by not calling next, which is how authentication or static files can end a request early.

    app.Use(async (ctx, next) =>
{
    var sw = Stopwatch.StartNew();
    await next();
    _log.LogInformation("{Path} took {Ms}ms", ctx.Request.Path, sw.ElapsedMilliseconds);
});

    456. Why does middleware ordering matter, and what is a typical correct order?

    Each middleware only sees requests that reach it, so anything that must run for all requests (exception handling, HSTS, forwarded headers) has to be registered before components that can short-circuit. A common order is exception handler, HSTS/HTTPS redirection, routing, CORS, authentication, authorization, rate limiting and finally endpoints. Getting it wrong produces subtle bugs such as 401 responses without CORS headers or exceptions that bypass your error handler.

    457. What is the difference between UseRouting and UseEndpoints, and how did minimal hosting change it?

    UseRouting matches the incoming request to an endpoint and stores it on the HttpContext; UseEndpoints executes the selected endpoint. Middleware between them can inspect the matched endpoint, which is how authorization metadata is applied. With WebApplication, MapGet and friends register endpoints and routing/endpoint middleware are added automatically at the right position.

    458. How do you write a custom middleware class and register it correctly?

    A conventional middleware is a class with a constructor taking RequestDelegate and an InvokeAsync(HttpContext) method, registered with UseMiddleware<T>(). Constructor dependencies are resolved once from the root provider, so scoped services must be injected into InvokeAsync instead. Alternatively you can implement IMiddleware, which is resolved per request from the request scope.

    public sealed class TimingMiddleware(RequestDelegate next)
{
    public async Task InvokeAsync(HttpContext ctx, ILogger<TimingMiddleware> log)
    {
        await next(ctx);
    }
}

    459. How does branching work with Map and MapWhen in the pipeline?

    Map branches on a path prefix and re-joins only if the branch calls next; MapWhen branches on an arbitrary predicate such as a header or query string. Branches are terminal by default once they handle the request. These are useful for isolating health endpoints or legacy pipelines, though endpoint routing is usually preferred today.

    460. How does attribute routing differ from conventional routing?

    Attribute routing declares templates directly on controllers and actions, giving each action an explicit, local URL shape. Conventional routing defines global templates with placeholders such as {controller}/{action}/{id?} and relies on naming conventions. Attribute routing is the default choice for APIs because it is explicit and supports per-action constraints and versioning.

    [ApiController]
[Route("api/orders")]
public class OrdersController : ControllerBase
{
    [HttpGet("{id:int}")]
    public ActionResult<OrderDto> Get(int id) => ...;
}

    461. What route constraints are available and when should you use them?

    Constraints such as int, guid, bool, datetime, min, max, length, alpha and regex restrict which values match a template. They prevent ambiguous matches and reject obviously invalid URLs before model binding runs. Prefer built-in constraints over regex for readability and performance.

    [HttpGet("{id:guid}")]
[HttpGet("range/{page:int:min(1)}")]

    462. How do you create a custom route constraint?

    Implement IRouteConstraint with a Match method, then register it with options.ConstraintMap.Add("slug", typeof(SlugConstraint)) in AddRouting. Reference it in a template as {slug:slug}. Constraints should be cheap and side-effect free because they run during route matching.

    463. What are the model binding sources in ASP.NET Core and what are their defaults?

    Values can come from the route, query string, form body, headers, or the request body as JSON. Simple types bind from route and query by default, complex types bind from the body, and [FromBody] allows only one parameter. Explicit attributes such as [FromRoute], [FromQuery], [FromHeader] and [FromForm] remove ambiguity.

    public IActionResult Search([FromQuery] string q, [FromQuery] int page = 1) => ...;

    464. Why can only one parameter bind from the body in a controller action?

    The request body is a forward-only stream, so the framework can only read and deserialize it once. If you need several values, wrap them in a single DTO. Minimal APIs solved this differently by generating code that reads the body once and distributes the values.

    465. How does model validation work with DataAnnotations in ASP.NET Core?

    Validation attributes on the model are evaluated by the model binder and recorded in ModelState. With [ApiController] an invalid ModelState automatically produces a 400 ProblemDetails response, so controllers rarely need explicit checks. Attributes such as [Required], [Range], [StringLength] and [RegularExpression] cover most rules.

    public record CreateOrderDto(
    [Required, StringLength(50)] string Customer,
    [Range(1, 1000)] int Quantity);

    466. When would you choose FluentValidation over DataAnnotations?

    FluentValidation keeps validation rules outside the DTO, supports complex conditional and cross-property rules, and is easy to unit test in isolation. It also handles async validation such as uniqueness checks against a database. DataAnnotations are lighter and zero-dependency, so they suit simple contracts.

    public class CreateOrderValidator : AbstractValidator<CreateOrderDto>
{
    public CreateOrderValidator()
    {
        RuleFor(x => x.Quantity).GreaterThan(0);
        RuleFor(x => x.Customer).NotEmpty().MaximumLength(50);
    }
}

    467. How do you integrate FluentValidation so invalid models return 400 automatically?

    Register validators with AddValidatorsFromAssemblyContaining<T>(), then add a custom IValidatorInterceptor or configure ApiBehaviorOptions.InvalidModelStateResponseFactory. With automatic validation enabled, failures are copied into ModelState and the standard ProblemDetails response is produced. This keeps controllers free of validation plumbing.

    468. What is the difference between action filters, resource filters and exception filters?

    Resource filters run before model binding and after the result, which makes them suitable for caching or short-circuiting expensive work. Action filters run around the action method after binding and are used for logging, validation or result transformation. Exception filters catch unhandled exceptions from the action and can replace the result without unwinding the whole pipeline.

    469. How do you register a filter globally versus per controller or per action?

    Global filters are added through MvcOptions.Filters; controller and action level filters are applied as attributes. Attributes are instantiated by the framework unless you use [ServiceFilter] or [TypeFilter] to resolve them from DI. Global filters apply to every action, so they must be cheap and safe.

    builder.Services.AddControllers(o =>
    o.Filters.Add<GlobalExceptionFilter>());

    470. What is ProblemDetails and which RFC defines it?

    ProblemDetails is a machine-readable error payload defined by RFC 7807 and clarified by RFC 9457, with members type, title, status, detail and instance. ASP.NET Core can produce it automatically for validation failures and status codes via AddProblemDetails(). Clients get one consistent error shape regardless of where the failure occurred.

    {
  "type": "https://example.com/errors/out-of-stock",
  "title": "Out of stock",
  "status": 409,
  "detail": "SKU 123 has 0 units available"
}

    471. How do you customize the automatic 400 response for validation errors?

    Configure ApiBehaviorOptions.InvalidModelStateResponseFactory to build the body you want, typically a ValidationProblemDetails with an errors dictionary. You can add a correlation id or change the media type there. Returning a consistent shape helps clients map field errors to forms.

    o.InvalidModelStateResponseFactory = ctx =>
    new BadRequestObjectResult(new ValidationProblemDetails(ctx.ModelState));

    472. How do you choose the correct HTTP status code for a POST that creates a resource?

    Return 201 Created with a Location header pointing at the new resource, usually through CreatedAtAction or Created. If the resource already exists and the call is effectively idempotent, 200 with the existing representation is often better. Use 202 Accepted when creation is asynchronous.

    return CreatedAtAction(nameof(GetById), new { id = order.Id }, order);

    473. Which status codes should a well-designed API use for common failures?

    400 for malformed or invalid input, 401 when credentials are missing or invalid, 403 when authenticated but not permitted, 404 for missing resources, 409 for state conflicts, 422 for semantic validation errors and 412 for failed preconditions. 500 means an unexpected server fault, and 503 signals temporary unavailability. Being consistent matters more than being exhaustive.

    474. When is 401 versus 403 the right response?

    401 means the request lacks valid authentication, so the client should authenticate and retry. 403 means the identity is known but the action is not allowed. Returning 403 for unauthenticated users hides the need to log in, and returning 401 for authorized-but-forbidden users causes pointless retry loops.

    475. How should REST resources be named and modelled?

    Use plural nouns for collections such as /orders and /orders/{id}/items for sub-resources, avoiding verbs in paths. Keep resource identity in the URL and let HTTP methods express the operation. Represent relationships by nesting only when the child cannot exist independently.

    476. Which HTTP methods are safe and which are idempotent?

    GET, HEAD and OPTIONS are safe because they do not change server state. GET, HEAD, PUT, DELETE and OPTIONS are idempotent, so repeating them yields the same state. POST and PATCH are generally neither safe nor idempotent, which is why retries need care.

    477. What is the difference between PUT, PATCH and POST?

    POST creates a subordinate resource or triggers processing and is neither safe nor idempotent. PUT replaces the full representation at a known URI and is idempotent. PATCH applies a partial modification and is only idempotent if the patch document is designed that way.

    PUT /orders/42        { "status": "shipped", "items": [...] }
PATCH /orders/42      { "op": "replace", "path": "/status", "value": "shipped" }

    478. How would you design pagination for a large collection endpoint?

    Offer page size and page number or an opaque cursor, cap the maximum page size, and return pagination metadata either in the body envelope or via Link headers. Offset pagination is simple but degrades and drifts on large or changing datasets; keyset or cursor pagination is stable and faster. Document default and maximum sizes.

    {
  "items": [ ... ],
  "page": 2,
  "pageSize": 25,
  "totalCount": 1043,
  "next": "/orders?page=3&pageSize=25"
}

    479. What is a keyset or cursor pagination scheme and why use it?

    Instead of an offset, the client sends the last seen sort key, for example an encoded (createdAt, id) tuple. The query then filters with WHERE (createdAt, id) > (@c, @i) and uses an index efficiently. This avoids deep-offset scans and does not skip or duplicate rows when data changes between pages.

    480. How do you implement filtering and sorting without creating security or performance problems?

    Expose an explicit allow-list of filterable and sortable fields rather than accepting arbitrary property names, and build the query dynamically against that list. Always parameterize values and cap the number of combined filters. Whitelisting prevents expression injection and accidental full-table scans.

    GET /orders?status=open&sort=-createdAt&createdAfter=2026-01-01

    481. What are sparse fieldsets and how do you implement them?

    Sparse fieldsets let the client request only the fields it needs, for example ?fields=id,total,status. The server validates the requested names against a whitelist and projects the response, often with a dictionary or a dynamic DTO. This reduces payload size but complicates caching and typing.

    482. Which API versioning strategies exist and how do they compare?

    URI path versioning (/v1/orders) is explicit and cache friendly; query string versioning is easy but pollutes URLs; header or media type versioning keeps URLs clean but is harder to test and cache. Choose one primary strategy and document deprecation policy. The Asp.Versioning.Mvc package supports all of them plus api-supported-versions headers.

    builder.Services.AddApiVersioning(o =>
{
    o.DefaultApiVersion = new ApiVersion(1, 0);
    o.AssumeDefaultVersionWhenUnspecified = true;
    o.ReportApiVersions = true;
});

    483. How does content negotiation work in ASP.NET Core?

    The framework inspects the Accept header and picks the first output formatter that can write the requested media type, falling back to a 406 if none matches. Formatters are configured in MvcOptions.OutputFormatters and their order determines priority. JSON is the default, with System.Text.Json as the serializer.

    484. How do you add a custom output formatter such as CSV?

    Derive from TextOutputFormatter, declare supported media types and encodings, and write to the response body in WriteResponseBodyAsync. Register it in OutputFormatters before or after the JSON formatter depending on desired priority. Restrict it with [Produces("text/csv")] where appropriate.

    public sealed class CsvFormatter : TextOutputFormatter
{
    public CsvFormatter()
    {
        SupportedMediaTypes.Add("text/csv");
        SupportedEncodings.Add(Encoding.UTF8);
    }
}

    485. How do you generate OpenAPI documents in modern ASP.NET Core?

    AddOpenApi() and MapOpenApi() build the document at runtime from endpoint metadata, replacing Swashbuckle for many scenarios. UI tooling can be layered on top, or the JSON document can feed client generators and contract tests. Document only endpoints you want published.

    builder.Services.AddOpenApi();
app.MapOpenApi();
app.MapGet("/orders/{id:int}", (int id) => ...).WithSummary("Get an order");

    486. How do you document response types and examples for OpenAPI?

    Use [ProducesResponseType] attributes or Produces<T>() on minimal APIs so status codes and schemas appear in the document. Add XML comments by enabling GenerateDocumentationFile and wiring the XML path, and enrich schemas with examples through transformers or annotations. Accurate response metadata is what makes generated clients usable.

    487. When should you prefer minimal APIs over controllers?

    Minimal APIs reduce ceremony for small, focused endpoints and start faster with less reflection. Controllers still win for large APIs that lean on filters, model binding conventions, and shared base classes. Many teams mix both in one application because they share routing and DI.

    488. How do endpoint filters work in minimal APIs?

    An endpoint filter implements IEndpointFilter and wraps the handler invocation, so it can inspect arguments, short-circuit, or modify the result. Filters are added per endpoint, per route group, or globally via AddEndpointFilter. They are the minimal API counterpart of action filters.

    app.MapPost("/orders", CreateOrder)
   .AddEndpointFilter<ValidationFilter>();

    489. What are route groups and what problem do they solve?

    MapGroup("/api/orders") creates a shared prefix and metadata scope so conventions such as authorization, tags or filters are applied once. Groups can be nested and are not themselves endpoints. This removes repetitive prefixes and attributes across related handlers.

    var orders = app.MapGroup("/api/orders").RequireAuthorization().WithTags("Orders");
orders.MapGet("/", ListOrders);
orders.MapGet("/{id:int}", GetOrder);

    490. What is IResult and why do TypedResults matter?

    IResult is the minimal API abstraction for producing an HTTP response. TypedResults returns concrete types such as Ok<T>, which makes handler signatures explicit and improves OpenAPI generation and unit testing. It replaces the untyped Results helpers in most new code.

    static Results<Ok<OrderDto>, NotFound> Get(int id, IOrderService svc) =>
    svc.Find(id) is { } o ? TypedResults.Ok(o) : TypedResults.NotFound();

    491. How does parameter binding work in minimal APIs?

    Simple types bind from the route by default, and other sources are declared explicitly with attributes such as [FromQuery], [FromHeader] or [FromBody]. Complex types default to the body, and well-known services like HttpContext, ClaimsPrincipal or CancellationToken are injected. The [AsParameters] attribute lets you group many query values into a single record.

    492. How do you validate request bodies in minimal APIs?

    Minimal APIs do not run DataAnnotations automatically before .NET 10, so teams either use an endpoint filter, a FluentValidation filter, or explicit validation in the handler. With .NET 10 you can call AddValidation() and annotate the DTO to get automatic 400 responses. Whatever the mechanism, validate at the boundary and return ProblemDetails.

    app.MapPost("/orders", (CreateOrderDto dto) => ...).AddValidation();

    493. What service lifetimes does ASP.NET Core DI support and how do they differ?

    Singleton instances live for the application, scoped instances live for one request scope, and transient instances are created per resolution. A scoped service resolved from the root provider is effectively a singleton, which is a common bug. Keep scoped lifetimes for DbContext, request-bound state and per-request caches.

    494. Why is injecting a scoped service into middleware's constructor a bug?

    Middleware is constructed once from the root provider, so a constructor-injected scoped service becomes a captive dependency shared across all requests. That can leak state, share a DbContext across threads and corrupt data. Inject the scoped service into InvokeAsync or use IMiddleware.

    495. How do you safely resolve scoped services inside a singleton background service?

    Inject IServiceScopeFactory and create a scope per unit of work, resolving scoped services from that scope and disposing it afterwards. Never hold a scoped service in a field of the singleton. This pattern is standard for hosted services and queue consumers.

    using var scope = _scopeFactory.CreateScope();
var db = scope.ServiceProvider.GetRequiredService<AppDbContext>();
await db.Orders.AddAsync(order);

    496. What is the difference between GetRequiredService and GetService?

    GetService<T>() returns null when the service is not registered, while GetRequiredService<T>() throws InvalidOperationException. Prefer the required variant for mandatory dependencies so misconfiguration fails fast rather than with a null reference. Neither should be used for control flow in application code.

    497. What are the pitfalls of using IHttpContextAccessor?

    It relies on an AsyncLocal, so the context is only valid inside the request flow and may be null in background threads or after the request completes. It hides dependencies and makes code harder to test. Prefer passing the values you need explicitly, and if you must use it, only read inside the active request.

    498. How does configuration work in ASP.NET Core and what is the precedence order?

    Configuration is a stack of providers; later providers override earlier ones, so appsettings.json, appsettings.{Environment}.json, user secrets, environment variables and command line args apply in that increasing order. Keys are flat with : separators and bind to strongly typed options. Reload-on-change is supported for file providers.

    499. What are user secrets and when should they not be used?

    User secrets store development-only values outside the repository in the user profile, keeping connection strings and API keys out of source control. They are never encrypted and are not a production secret store. In production use environment variables, a vault or a managed secret service.

    dotnet user-secrets init
dotnet user-secrets set "Smtp:Password" "dev-only"

    500. How do you bind configuration to a strongly typed options class?

    Use services.Configure<T>(configuration.GetSection("Section")) and inject IOptions<T>, IOptionsSnapshot<T> or IOptionsMonitor<T>. IOptions is a singleton, IOptionsSnapshot is scoped and recomputed per request, and IOptionsMonitor supports change notifications. Choose based on whether you need reloads.

    501. How do you validate options at startup so misconfiguration fails fast?

    Call AddOptions<T>().Bind(section).ValidateDataAnnotations().ValidateOnStart() so validation runs during startup rather than on first use. For complex rules use Validate with a predicate or implement IValidateOptions<T>. Failing at boot is far cheaper than failing on the first production request.

    builder.Services.AddOptions<SmtpOptions>()
    .Bind(builder.Configuration.GetSection("Smtp"))
    .ValidateDataAnnotations()
    .ValidateOnStart();

    502. How do logging scopes help in ASP.NET Core?

    A scope attaches key-value pairs to every log entry written within it, so correlation ids, tenant ids or order ids appear on all related messages. The framework already creates a scope per request with the request id and path. Add your own keys with ILogger.BeginScope.

    using (_logger.BeginScope(new Dictionary<string, object> { ["OrderId"] = id }))
{
    _logger.LogInformation("Processing order");
}

    503. What does structured logging mean and why avoid string interpolation in log calls?

    Structured logging stores message templates plus named properties rather than a flat string, so logs can be queried by field. Interpolated strings destroy that structure and allocate even when the level is disabled. Use message templates with placeholders and pass values as arguments.

    _logger.LogInformation("Order {OrderId} shipped to {Country}", id, country);

    504. How do health checks differ between liveness and readiness?

    Liveness answers whether the process is healthy enough to keep running, so orchestrators restart it on failure. Readiness answers whether the app can serve traffic, including dependencies such as the database, so traffic is withheld rather than the pod killed. Expose them on separate endpoints with different checks and tags.

    builder.Services.AddHealthChecks()
    .AddDbContextCheck<AppDbContext>("db", tags: new[] { "ready" });
app.MapHealthChecks("/health/live", new() { Predicate = _ => false });
app.MapHealthChecks("/health/ready", new() { Predicate = c => c.Tags.Contains("ready") });

    505. Why should health check endpoints be excluded from authentication and rate limiting?

    Probes are called frequently by infrastructure that cannot present credentials, and blocking them causes false failures and restarts. Rate limiting can also throttle probes during traffic spikes, triggering cascading outages. Map them before auth middleware or mark them AllowAnonymous and exempt from limits.

    506. How does CORS work and why is a preflight request needed?

    The browser enforces same-origin policy and asks the server, via an OPTIONS preflight, whether the origin, method and headers are allowed. The server responds with Access-Control-Allow-Origin and related headers. Non-simple requests and credentialed requests always trigger preflight, and the response must name an explicit origin when credentials are used.

    builder.Services.AddCors(o => o.AddPolicy("spa", p =>
    p.WithOrigins("https://app.example.com").AllowAnyHeader().AllowAnyMethod().AllowCredentials()));
app.UseCors("spa");

    507. What is the most common cause of CORS failures in ASP.NET Core?

    Middleware order: UseCors must run after UseRouting and before authentication and authorization so headers are added even to 401 responses. Another common cause is combining AllowAnyOrigin with AllowCredentials, which the spec forbids. Always test the actual preflight response, not just the GET.

    508. How does the built-in rate limiting middleware work?

    AddRateLimiter registers policies such as fixed window, sliding window, token bucket and concurrency limiter, and UseRateLimiter applies them. Policies can be partitioned by IP, API key, user or route. Rejected requests return 503 by default, which you should normally change to 429 with a Retry-After header.

    builder.Services.AddRateLimiter(o =>
{
    o.RejectionStatusCode = StatusCodes.Status429TooManyRequests;
    o.AddFixedWindowLimiter("api", opt =>
    {
        opt.PermitLimit = 100;
        opt.Window = TimeSpan.FromMinutes(1);
    });
});

    509. How do you partition a rate limit per API key or per user?

    Use AddPolicy with a partition key selector that reads a header, claim or IP address. Hash or truncate the key so raw secrets never become dictionary keys. Also bound the number of partitions so memory cannot grow without limit.

    o.AddPolicy("per-key", ctx =>
    RateLimitPartition.GetFixedWindowLimiter(
        ctx.Request.Headers["X-Api-Key"].ToString(),
        _ => new FixedWindowRateLimiterOptions { PermitLimit = 60, Window = TimeSpan.FromMinutes(1) }));

    510. How does response compression work and what should you not compress?

    UseResponseCompression negotiates gzip, brotli or deflate based on Accept-Encoding and compresses responses above a size threshold. Do not compress already-compressed content such as images, video or zip files, and avoid compressing responses containing secrets over unencrypted channels. HTTPS plus compression can also expose BREACH-style attacks on reflected secrets.

    511. What is the difference between response caching and output caching?

    Response caching sets HTTP cache headers so clients and intermediaries can cache, but the server still executes the request. Output caching stores the rendered response server-side and can serve it without running the endpoint, with support for tags and eviction. Output caching is the better fit for expensive read endpoints you control.

    builder.Services.AddOutputCache(o =>
    o.AddPolicy("orders", b => b.Expire(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30)).Tag("orders")));
app.UseOutputCache();

    512. Why must you be careful caching responses for authenticated requests?

    A shared cache keyed only by URL can serve one user's data to another, which is a serious data leak. Vary the cache key by user, tenant or relevant headers, or disable caching for personalized endpoints and send Cache-Control: private, no-store. Output caching ignores Authorization by default, so it must be configured explicitly.

    513. How does IDistributedCache differ from IMemoryCache?

    IMemoryCache is in-process, fast, and lost on restart, so it is inconsistent across instances. IDistributedCache targets Redis, SQL Server or similar and shares state across nodes, at the cost of serialization and network latency. Use the distributed cache for session-like data and coordination, and memory cache for local hot data.

    514. What cache stampede problem exists and how do you mitigate it?

    When a hot key expires, many concurrent requests miss and all recompute or refetch simultaneously, overloading the backend. Mitigations include per-key locking or SemaphoreSlim, stale-while-revalidate, jittered expiry and early refresh before expiry. HybridCache in .NET 9 addresses this with built-in stampede protection.

    515. What does the SameSite cookie attribute do?

    It controls whether a cookie is sent on cross-site requests: Strict never sends it, Lax sends it on top-level GET navigation, and None always sends it but requires Secure. The default in modern browsers is Lax. This is a primary defence against CSRF for cookie-based auth.

    516. How do you configure cookie security flags in ASP.NET Core?

    Set HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite, a path and a bounded lifetime on the cookie options, and consider __Host- prefixes for strict scoping. Use CookiePolicyOptions to enforce defaults globally. HttpOnly prevents JavaScript access, mitigating XSS-based token theft.

    options.Cookie.HttpOnly = true;
options.Cookie.SecurePolicy = CookieSecurePolicy.Always;
options.Cookie.SameSite = SameSiteMode.Lax;

    517. How does JWT bearer authentication work in ASP.NET Core?
