    AddAuthentication(JwtBearerDefaults.AuthenticationScheme).AddJwtBearer() configures token validation against an authority's signing keys, issuer and audience. The handler validates signature, lifetime, issuer and audience, then builds a ClaimsPrincipal from the payload. Authorization middleware then evaluates policies against those claims.

    builder.Services.AddAuthentication().AddJwtBearer(o =>
{
    o.Authority = "https://id.example.com";
    o.Audience = "orders-api";
    o.TokenValidationParameters.ValidateIssuer = true;
});

    518. Where should a browser-based SPA store access tokens?

    Local storage is readable by any script, so an XSS flaw leaks the token; an HttpOnly cookie is not readable but needs CSRF protection. A common compromise is a short-lived access token in memory plus a refresh token in a Secure, HttpOnly, SameSite cookie. Better still, use a backend-for-frontend that keeps tokens server-side.

    519. How does cookie authentication differ from bearer token authentication?

    Cookie authentication stores an encrypted ticket in a browser cookie that is sent automatically, which suits server-rendered apps but requires CSRF defence. Bearer tokens must be attached explicitly by the client and suit APIs and mobile apps. Cookies rely on the browser's ambient authority; tokens do not.

    520. How do you implement policy-based authorization?

    Register policies in AddAuthorization with requirements, then apply them with [Authorize(Policy = "...")] or RequireAuthorization. Each requirement is evaluated by one or more handlers deriving from AuthorizationHandler<TRequirement>. Policies compose claims, roles and custom logic in one named place.

    builder.Services.AddAuthorization(o =>
    o.AddPolicy("CanApprove", p => p.RequireClaim("permission", "orders.approve")));

    521. What is a claims transformation and when is it useful?

    An IClaimsTransformation runs after authentication and can add, remove or enrich claims on the principal, for example mapping an external id to internal roles. It is useful for reconciling identity provider claims with application permissions. Keep it fast and avoid database calls on every request without caching.

    522. How does resource-based authorization work?

    You call IAuthorizationService.AuthorizeAsync(User, resource, policy) from the handler after loading the resource, so the decision depends on the actual entity rather than just claims. This pattern fits "owner or admin may edit this document" rules that URL-level attributes cannot express. Return 403 or 404 consistently to avoid leaking existence.

    var result = await _auth.AuthorizeAsync(User, order, "OrderOwner");
if (!result.Succeeded) return Forbid();

    523. What does ASP.NET Core Identity provide out of the box?

    Identity supplies user and role stores, password hashing, lockout, two-factor flows, token providers and the UserManager/SignInManager APIs, backed by EF Core by default. It is a full membership system, not just a login endpoint. For API-only scenarios many teams prefer an external identity provider such as Duende or Entra ID.

    524. How does antiforgery protection work in ASP.NET Core?

    The server issues a token bound to the user session and expects it back in a header or form field on state-changing requests. [ValidateAntiForgeryToken] or the automatic validation for Razor Pages rejects requests without a matching token. Cookie-authenticated APIs need this; pure bearer-token APIs generally do not because browsers do not attach tokens automatically.

    525. What do HSTS and HTTPS redirection do and why both?

    HTTPS redirection sends a 307 to the https scheme for that request, but the first request still travels in clear text. HSTS tells the browser to use HTTPS for the domain for a period, closing that gap, and UseHsts emits the header only outside development. Use both, and never enable HSTS on a domain you cannot serve over TLS.

    app.UseHsts();
app.UseHttpsRedirection();

    526. Which security headers should an API send?

    At minimum X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff, Referrer-Policy and, for browser-facing responses, a restrictive Content-Security-Policy. Add X-Frame-Options or CSP frame-ancestors to prevent clickjacking, and remove headers that leak server details. APIs returning only JSON need fewer headers than HTML apps but should still avoid MIME sniffing.

    527. How do you handle file uploads safely in ASP.NET Core?

    Accept IFormFile or stream with MultipartReader, enforce a size limit, validate content type and extension, and never trust the client-provided filename. Store outside the web root, generate your own names, and scan for malware when accepting untrusted content. Stream to storage rather than buffering the whole file in memory.

    [HttpPost("upload")]
[RequestSizeLimit(25_000_000)]
public async Task<IActionResult> Upload(IFormFile file, CancellationToken ct)
{
    await using var stream = File.Create(Path.Combine(_root, Guid.NewGuid().ToString("N")));
    await file.CopyToAsync(stream, ct);
    return NoContent();
}

    528. How do you stream a large download without loading it into memory?

    Return a FileStreamResult or write directly to Response.Body with CopyToAsync, optionally with enableRangeProcessing for partial content. Set content type and length when known, and never build the payload in a byte array. This keeps memory flat regardless of file size.

    return File(stream, "application/octet-stream", enableRangeProcessing: true);

    529. How do you raise the request body size limit for a single endpoint?

    Use [RequestSizeLimit] on a controller action or IHttpMaxRequestBodySizeFeature in minimal APIs, and keep the global Kestrel limit low. IIS and any reverse proxy also impose their own limits that must be raised separately. Raising global limits invites memory exhaustion attacks.

    530. How do you handle large payloads you do not want to buffer?

    Stream the request body with MultipartReader or JsonSerializer.DeserializeAsyncEnumerable, and stream responses incrementally with IAsyncEnumerable. Disable request buffering where appropriate and apply backpressure. Buffering a 100 MB JSON document is a reliable way to trigger an out-of-memory condition.

    531. How do you run background work inside a web application correctly?

    Implement IHostedService or derive from BackgroundService and register it with AddHostedService. The host manages start and graceful stop through the cancellation token, and the work survives beyond individual requests. Do not capture request-scoped services or fire-and-forget from a controller.

    public sealed class OutboxWorker(IServiceScopeFactory scopes) : BackgroundService
{
    protected override async Task ExecuteAsync(CancellationToken ct)
    {
        while (!ct.IsCancellationRequested) { /* create scope, process */ }
    }
}

    532. Why is firing a background task from a controller with Task.Run dangerous?

    The task is unobserved and untracked, so it can be killed on app recycle, lose its DI scope and swallow exceptions. The request may also complete while the work is still writing to a disposed DbContext. Use a hosted service with a queue such as Channel<T>, or a durable message broker.

    533. What is SignalR and when would you use it?

    SignalR provides real-time bidirectional messaging over WebSockets with fallbacks to server-sent events and long polling. It handles connection management, groups and reconnection for you. Use it for notifications, dashboards and chat rather than trying to poll an API.

    public class OrderHub : Hub
{
    public Task Subscribe(string orderId) => Groups.AddToGroupAsync(Context.ConnectionId, orderId);
}

    534. How does SignalR scale out across multiple servers?

    Because connections are pinned to a server, you need a backplane such as Redis or Azure SignalR Service to broadcast across instances. Sticky sessions alone are insufficient for group messaging. Also consider connection limits, message size and authentication on the negotiate step.

    535. When would you choose gRPC over REST?

    gRPC is a strong fit for internal service-to-service calls with low latency, streaming and strict contracts over HTTP/2 with Protobuf. REST with JSON wins for public APIs, browser clients and broad tooling support. gRPC-Web or JSON transcoding bridges browser scenarios when needed.

    536. What are the practical constraints of gRPC in ASP.NET Core?

    It requires HTTP/2, which affects hosting behind some proxies and on older IIS setups, and browsers need gRPC-Web. Protobuf messages are not human-readable, and load balancers must support end-to-end HTTP/2 for streaming. Deadlines, cancellation and status codes are part of the contract and should be set explicitly.

    537. What is YARP and why use it instead of nginx?

    YARP is a reverse proxy library for ASP.NET Core, configured in code or appsettings with routes and clusters, and it participates in the same DI and middleware pipeline as your app. It suits scenarios needing custom routing, auth or transforms in .NET. Dedicated proxies remain better for raw throughput and features like caching and WAF.

    builder.Services.AddReverseProxy().LoadFromConfig(builder.Configuration.GetSection("ReverseProxy"));
app.MapReverseProxy();

    538. How do idempotency keys work at the API layer?

    The client sends a unique key with a mutating request, the server stores the key and the response, and repeats return the stored response instead of reprocessing. This makes retrying a POST safe after a network failure. Keys need a scope, a TTL and atomic storage to avoid races.

    POST /payments
Idempotency-Key: 9f1c2b7e-4c2a-4d1a-9b0e-2f7c1a3d5e6f

    539. How do you implement idempotency keys without race conditions?

    Insert the key into a unique-constrained store in the same transaction as the business operation, or use a conditional write that fails if the key exists. If the first request is still in flight, return 409 or 425 rather than duplicating work. Store the response body and status so retries replay identically.

    540. What makes a good error contract for a public API?

    A stable machine-readable code, a human-readable message, field-level details for validation, and a correlation id for support. Status codes carry the coarse meaning, and the body adds specifics without leaking internals. Keep the shape identical across endpoints and versions.

    {
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/validation",
  "title": "One or more validation errors occurred",
  "status": 400,
  "errors": { "quantity": ["Must be greater than 0"] },
  "traceId": "00-8f2c1a..."
}

    541. Why should you avoid returning exception messages to clients?

    Exception text can expose stack traces, SQL fragments, file paths and internal implementation details useful to an attacker. Log the details with a correlation id and return a generic ProblemDetails message. Only surface deliberately designed domain error messages.

    542. How do you configure request timeouts in ASP.NET Core?

    Use the request timeout middleware (AddRequestTimeouts and UseRequestTimeouts) for per-endpoint or global limits, and pass CancellationToken through all downstream calls. Client timeouts and proxy timeouts must be aligned, otherwise you get truncated responses. Long-running work belongs in an async job pattern.

    builder.Services.AddRequestTimeouts(o =>
    o.DefaultPolicy = new RequestTimeoutPolicy { Timeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(10) });
app.UseRequestTimeouts();

    543. How do you apply a timeout to an outbound HTTP call from an API?

    Configure a named or typed HttpClient with a Timeout, and combine it with a CancellationToken from the request so client disconnects propagate. Retries belong in a resilience pipeline with a budget, not in a naive loop. Never share a mutable HttpClient with per-request headers.

    544. Why should you avoid exposing EF Core entities directly from controllers?

    Entities carry persistence concerns such as navigation properties, lazy-loading proxies and change tracking, which produce oversized payloads, serialization cycles and tight coupling to the database schema. They also invite over-posting, because a client can set properties you never intended to be writable. Map to explicit DTOs so the contract is intentional, stable and safe.

    545. How do you read the current environment name and what are the conventional values?

    IWebHostEnvironment.EnvironmentName exposes the value, which comes from ASPNETCORE_ENVIRONMENT or DOTNET_ENVIRONMENT. The conventions are Development, Staging and Production, and IsDevelopment() checks are extension helpers. The value must never be trusted as a security boundary; it only controls configuration and diagnostics behaviour.

    if (app.Environment.IsDevelopment())
{
    app.UseDeveloperExceptionPage();
}

    546. What are the risks of environment-specific appsettings files?

    Values drift between environments and a missing override silently inherits the previous layer, which can point production at a development database. Keep only environment-specific overrides in these files and validate critical options at startup. Prefer injecting real values from environment variables or a secret store in production.

    547. How do you pick up configuration changes without restarting the application?

    File providers support reloadOnChange, and IOptionsMonitor<T>.OnChange notifies listeners with a new value. Consume the current value through CurrentValue instead of caching it in a field. Some settings, such as Kestrel endpoints or DI registrations, still require a restart.

    _monitor.OnChange(o => _logger.LogInformation("Limit changed to {Limit}", o.Limit));

    548. Should a class library depend on IConfiguration or on IOptions?

    Libraries should take a strongly typed options object or plain parameters rather than IConfiguration, so the host owns binding and the library has no file or environment assumptions. This keeps the library testable and reusable outside ASP.NET Core. Expose a binding extension such as AddMyLibrary(Action<MyOptions>).

    549. Why is injecting IOptionsSnapshot into a singleton invalid?

    IOptionsSnapshot<T> is registered as scoped, so resolving it from a singleton creates a captive dependency and throws when scope validation is enabled. Even without validation it freezes one snapshot for the lifetime of the process. Use IOptionsMonitor<T> in singletons and IOptionsSnapshot<T> in scoped services.

    550. How does a middleware short-circuit the pipeline and when is that appropriate?

    A middleware simply does not call next and writes the response itself, ending the chain. This is appropriate for authentication failures, rate limiting, cached responses, static files or health endpoints. Short-circuiting must still produce a complete, correct response with headers and status.

    551. How do you pass request-scoped data from middleware to a controller?

    Store it in HttpContext.Items, which lives for the request, or enrich the response headers. A cleaner approach is a scoped service holding the values, injected into both the middleware and the controller. Avoid static or thread-static fields, which break under async and pooling.

    context.Items["CorrelationId"] = correlationId;
// later
var id = HttpContext.Items["CorrelationId"] as string;

    552. How do you implement global exception handling with IExceptionHandler in .NET 8 and later?

    Implement IExceptionHandler with TryHandleAsync, register it with AddExceptionHandler<T>(), and call app.UseExceptionHandler(). Return true once you have written a ProblemDetails response, or false to let the next handler try. This keeps error mapping in one testable class instead of a lambda.

    public async ValueTask<bool> TryHandleAsync(HttpContext ctx, Exception ex, CancellationToken ct)
{
    ctx.Response.StatusCode = ex is NotFoundException ? 404 : 500;
    await ctx.Response.WriteAsJsonAsync(new ProblemDetails { Status = ctx.Response.StatusCode }, ct);
    return true;
}

    553. What is the difference between UseExceptionHandler and an exception filter?

    UseExceptionHandler is middleware that catches anything thrown further down the pipeline, including model binding and serialization failures. Exception filters only wrap action execution inside MVC and cannot catch middleware-level errors. Global error handling is therefore usually built on the middleware.

    554. How would you add a correlation id to every request and response?

    Read an incoming X-Correlation-Id header or generate one, store it in HttpContext.Items and the logging scope, then echo it on the response via OnStarting. Downstream HTTP calls should forward the same value. This makes logs traceable across services.

    var id = ctx.Request.Headers["X-Correlation-Id"].FirstOrDefault() ?? Guid.NewGuid().ToString("N");
ctx.Response.OnStarting(() => { ctx.Response.Headers["X-Correlation-Id"] = id; return Task.CompletedTask; });

    555. What is HttpContext.Features and can you give a practical use?

    Features are the low-level server abstraction collection, exposing things like IHttpRequestBodyDetectionFeature, IHttpResponseBodyFeature or IHttpMaxRequestBodySizeFeature. You can read or replace a feature to change server behaviour for a request. Custom features are also a clean way for middleware to hand data to later components.

    556. When must the developer exception page be disabled?

    It must never be enabled outside local development because it exposes stack traces, source snippets, configuration values and connection strings. Gate it on IsDevelopment() and ensure the production path uses the exception handler middleware with logging. A misconfigured environment name is a common cause of accidental exposure.

    557. How do you conditionally add middleware only for certain environments?

    Wrap the registration in an if on app.Environment, or use UseWhen with a predicate when the decision is per request. Keep the branching shallow because two different pipelines are hard to reason about. Prefer configuration flags over environment names when behaviour varies per deployment.

    558. How do you generate URLs for other endpoints without hardcoding paths?

    Use LinkGenerator or Url.Action with route names and values so links stay correct when templates change. Endpoint names come from [HttpGet(Name = "...")] or WithName in minimal APIs. This matters for pagination links and Location headers.

    var url = _linkGenerator.GetPathByName("GetOrder", new { id = 42 });

    559. What causes an AmbiguousMatchException in routing and how do you fix it?

    Two endpoints match the same request with equal precedence, typically because templates differ only by an optional or unconstrained segment. Routing throws rather than guessing. Fix it by adding a constraint, reordering precedence with literal segments, or merging the endpoints.

    560. What is endpoint metadata and how do you read it at runtime?

    Routing attaches metadata objects to an endpoint, such as IAuthorizeData, IAllowAnonymous, ProducesResponseTypeMetadata or custom attributes. Middleware can inspect context.GetEndpoint()?.Metadata to make per-endpoint decisions. This is how authorization and CORS policies become endpoint-aware.

    561. What are the risks of catch-all route parameters?

    A template like {**path} matches everything, so it can shadow other routes depending on precedence and swallow 404s. It also makes caching, authorization and input validation harder because the value is arbitrary. Reserve it for proxy or static-content scenarios and validate the captured path strictly.

    562. How do you bind repeated query parameters or a complex filter object?

    Bind an array or list, for example ?tag=a&tag=b into string[] tag, or accept a single complex type that the binder populates from query keys. In minimal APIs group many values with [AsParameters]. Validate the count and each value to prevent abuse.

    public IActionResult List([FromQuery] string[] tag, [FromQuery] OrderFilter filter) => ...;

    563. How do you implement cross-field validation with DataAnnotations?

    Implement IValidatableObject on the model and return ValidationResult instances from Validate, optionally naming the offending members. This covers rules like "end date must be after start date". For anything more complex or reusable, a custom ValidationAttribute is cleaner.

    public IEnumerable<ValidationResult> Validate(ValidationContext ctx)
{
    if (End <= Start) yield return new ValidationResult("End must follow Start", new[] { nameof(End) });
}

    564. How do you surface validation errors that originate in the service or domain layer?

    Throw a dedicated validation exception carrying a dictionary of field errors, catch it in the exception handler and emit a 400 ValidationProblemDetails. Alternatively return a result object the controller translates. Never return bare strings, and keep the same error shape as model-binding failures.

    565. What behaviours does the [ApiController] attribute enable?

    It enables automatic 400 responses for invalid model state, binding source inference for complex parameters, required body inference, multipart form inference, and ProblemDetails for error status codes. It also requires attribute routing. Without it, controllers behave like MVC views controllers and silently ignore ModelState errors.

    566. How do you return a clean 400 when the JSON body is malformed?

    Deserialization failures surface as JsonException or model-state errors with an input formatter error, which [ApiController] converts to a 400 ProblemDetails. If you handle the exception yourself, distinguish it from business errors and avoid echoing the raw payload. Never allow a malformed body to become a 500.

    567. How do you configure JSON serialization globally?

    Use AddControllers().AddJsonOptions or ConfigureHttpJsonOptions for minimal APIs, and set policies such as camelCase naming, enum-as-string converters, null handling and max depth. Keep the configuration identical across both stacks so mixed applications behave consistently. Also set a reasonable MaxDepth to guard against deep payloads.

    builder.Services.ConfigureHttpJsonOptions(o =>
    o.SerializerOptions.Converters.Add(new JsonStringEnumConverter()));

    568. How do you accept snake_case field names from a client?

    Set PropertyNamingPolicy = JsonNamingPolicy.SnakeCaseLower for that endpoint or globally, or annotate individual members with [JsonPropertyName]. Mixing policies across a codebase confuses clients, so prefer a single documented convention per API version. Remember that the naming policy also affects outgoing responses.

    569. How should you represent dates and times in an API contract?

    Use ISO 8601 with an explicit offset, and prefer DateTimeOffset for instants so the client cannot misinterpret the zone. Store UTC in the database and convert at the edges. Document which fields are instants versus local dates, since a date-only value should not be sent as midnight UTC.

    "createdAt": "2026-03-14T09:21:00Z",
"deliveryDate": "2026-03-20"

    570. How do you model an operation that is not CRUD, such as cancelling an order?

    Prefer a state sub-resource or a control action, for example POST /orders/42/cancel, which is explicit and easy to authorize. If the state is a simple field you may also model it as PATCH on the order. Avoid overloading DELETE when the resource still exists afterwards.

    571. How would you design a bulk create endpoint?

    Accept a collection at the collection URI with POST /orders/batch, return 200 or 207 with a per-item result array, and enforce a maximum batch size. Decide explicitly whether the operation is atomic or best-effort, and document it. For very large volumes, return 202 and process asynchronously.

    572. How do you report partial success without confusing clients?

    Return 207 Multi-Status or 200 with an explicit results array where each entry has an index, status and optional error. Keep the overall status meaningful and never hide failures behind a 200 without per-item detail. Clients must be able to correlate results with their input by position or id.

    { "results": [ { "index": 0, "status": 201 }, { "index": 1, "status": 409, "error": "duplicate" } ] }

    573. Should child collections be nested in the URL or filtered at the top level?

    Nest when the child's identity is scoped to the parent and it cannot exist independently, such as /orders/42/items. Use a top-level resource with a filter when you need to query children across parents, such as /order-items?status=backordered. Deep nesting beyond two levels makes URLs brittle.

    574. What is HATEOAS and do most APIs need it?

    HATEOAS means responses include links describing available transitions, so clients navigate by following links instead of hardcoding URLs. It improves discoverability but adds payload and complexity, and few public APIs implement it fully. Most teams get most of the benefit from a well-documented OpenAPI contract plus pagination links.

    575. How do you design a long-running operation endpoint?

    Return 202 Accepted with a Location header pointing at an operation resource that reports status, progress and the eventual result. Include Retry-After and keep the operation resource idempotent to read. This decouples the client from timeouts and server restarts.

    HTTP/1.1 202 Accepted
Location: /operations/7f3c
Retry-After: 5

    576. How do you implement conditional GET with ETag?

    Compute a stable hash or version for the representation, return it in ETag, and compare If-None-Match on later requests to reply 304 without a body. This saves bandwidth and CPU. The ETag must change whenever the representation changes, or clients will serve stale data.

    if (ctx.Request.Headers.IfNoneMatch == etag)
{
    ctx.Response.StatusCode = StatusCodes.Status304NotModified;
    return;
}

    577. How does If-Match implement optimistic concurrency at the HTTP level?

    The client sends the ETag it last saw in If-Match, and the server rejects the update with 412 Precondition Failed if the current version differs. This prevents lost updates without holding locks. Map it to a rowversion or version column in the database.

    578. When should you return 409 versus 422?

    409 Conflict means the request is well-formed but conflicts with current server state, such as a duplicate email or a version mismatch. 422 Unprocessable Content means the syntax is valid but the semantics fail business rules. Many teams use 400 for all validation and reserve 422 for semantic failures; the important thing is consistency.

    579. What should a DELETE endpoint return and is it idempotent?

    Return 204 No Content on successful deletion, 404 if the resource never existed, and consider 200 with a body if you need to return details. Deleting an already-deleted resource should not error on repetition, which is what makes DELETE idempotent in effect. Soft deletes should still hide the resource from reads.

    580. How do you deprecate an API endpoint properly?

    Announce it in documentation, add Deprecation and Sunset response headers, and keep the old version working for a published window. Monitor usage per client so you know who still calls it. Never remove an endpoint without a migration path.

    Deprecation: true
Sunset: Wed, 01 Jul 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: <https://api.example.com/v2/orders>; rel="successor-version"

    581. How do you publish separate OpenAPI documents per API version?

    With Asp.Versioning.ApiExplorer you enumerate the discovered API versions and register one document per version. Each document then contains only the endpoints for that version, which is what client generators expect. Expose them at stable paths such as /swagger/v2/swagger.json.

    582. How does media type versioning work in practice?

    The client requests a vendor-specific media type that encodes the version, and the server selects the matching formatter or controller. It keeps URLs clean and aligns with content negotiation, but it is harder to test in a browser, harder to cache and poorly supported by some proxies. Many teams still prefer explicit path versioning.

    GET /orders/42
Accept: application/vnd.example.order.v2+json

    583. How do you keep minimal API registrations organized in a large application?

    Group endpoints into static classes with an MapXxxEndpoints(this IEndpointRouteBuilder) extension method, and call them from Program.cs or from route groups. Keep handlers in the same file as their mappings so the contract is readable. This avoids a single thousand-line Program.cs.

    public static class OrderEndpoints
{
    public static IEndpointRouteBuilder MapOrderEndpoints(this IEndpointRouteBuilder app)
    {
        var g = app.MapGroup("/api/orders");
        g.MapGet("/{id:int}", GetById);
        return app;
    }
}

    584. How do you stream server-sent events or an endless response from an API?

    Return an IAsyncEnumerable<T> and set the content type, or write to Response.Body with explicit flushing. Disable response buffering and handle client disconnects through the request cancellation token. Remember that proxies and load balancers may time out idle connections.

    app.MapGet("/stream", (CancellationToken ct) =>
    TypedResults.Ok(Generate(ct)));

    585. How do you integration test a minimal API endpoint?

    Use WebApplicationFactory<Program> with a partial Program class, create a client with CreateClient() and call the endpoint over the in-memory test server. Override dependencies such as the database through ConfigureTestServices. Assert on status code, headers and the deserialized ProblemDetails body.

    var factory = new WebApplicationFactory<Program>();
var client = factory.CreateClient();
var res = await client.GetAsync("/api/orders/999");
Assert.Equal(HttpStatusCode.NotFound, res.StatusCode);

    586. What are the main limitations of minimal APIs compared with controllers?

    Minimal APIs lack built-in model-state validation before .NET 10, have no filters hierarchy, and do not support some MVC features such as view results or complex binder customization. They also encourage large handler lambdas if you are not disciplined. Controllers remain stronger for large, convention-heavy APIs.

    587. What are keyed services and when are they useful?

    Keyed services let you register several implementations of one interface under different keys and resolve a specific one with [FromKeyedServices("key")] or GetRequiredKeyedService. They replace factory delegates and service-locator patterns for choosing a strategy. Common uses are multiple payment providers or storage backends.

    builder.Services.AddKeyedSingleton<IPaymentGateway, StripeGateway>("stripe");
builder.Services.AddKeyedSingleton<IPaymentGateway, AdyenGateway>("adyen");

    588. Why should you use IHttpClientFactory instead of newing up HttpClient?

    The factory pools and rotates handlers, avoiding socket exhaustion from disposing many clients and stale DNS from a single long-lived one. It also centralizes base addresses, default headers, resilience policies and logging. Register typed clients so dependencies stay explicit and testable.

    builder.Services.AddHttpClient<CatalogClient>(c =>
    c.BaseAddress = new Uri("https://catalog.internal/"));

    589. How do you add cross-cutting behaviour to a registered service without editing it?

    Use the decorator pattern: implement the same interface, wrap the inner implementation and register the decorator with a factory that resolves the inner type. Libraries such as Scrutor provide Decorate for this. Typical uses are caching, metrics and retry around an existing service.

    builder.Services.Decorate<IPriceService, CachedPriceService>();

    590. How do you log request and response bodies without leaking secrets?

    Log only at Debug or Trace, truncate to a fixed size, and redact known sensitive fields such as passwords, tokens, card numbers and authorization headers. Disable body logging entirely for authentication and payment endpoints. Buffering bodies for logging adds memory and latency, so make it opt-in.

    591. How do you control log verbosity per namespace or category?

    Set Logging:LogLevel entries per category in configuration, for example raising Microsoft.AspNetCore to Warning while keeping your own namespaces at Information. Category names default to the fully qualified type name, so organizing types into namespaces gives you control. Filters also support rules per provider.

    {
  "Logging": {
    "LogLevel": {
      "Default": "Information",
      "Microsoft.AspNetCore": "Warning",
      "MyApp.Orders": "Debug"
    }
  }
}

    592. What does OpenTelemetry add over plain logging?

    It provides distributed traces, metrics and correlated logs with a vendor-neutral protocol, so you can see a request's full path across services. ASP.NET Core and HttpClient emit activities automatically once instrumentation is added. This makes latency bottlenecks and error propagation visible where logs alone only show isolated events.

    593. How do you publish custom application metrics from an API?

    Use System.Diagnostics.Metrics.Meter to create counters, histograms or observable gauges, and register the meter with your metrics exporter. Keep tags low-cardinality, since user ids or raw URLs explode the metric series count. Prefer histograms for latency distributions rather than averages.

    private static readonly Meter M = new("Orders");
private static readonly Counter<int> Created = M.CreateCounter<int>("orders.created");

    594. How do you write a custom health check?

    Implement IHealthCheck with a CheckHealthAsync that returns Healthy, Degraded or Unhealthy plus a description. Register it with AddCheck<T>("name", tags: ...) and keep the check fast with its own timeout. Never let a slow dependency turn health checks into a bottleneck.

    public Task<HealthCheckResult> CheckHealthAsync(HealthCheckContext ctx, CancellationToken ct)
    => Task.FromResult(HealthCheckResult.Healthy("queue reachable"));

    595. What should a readiness check avoid doing?

    It should not perform expensive queries, mutate state, or fail because an optional dependency is down, which would remove healthy instances from rotation. Avoid checking things the instance cannot fix, such as a third-party outage. Keep it cheap enough to run every few seconds.

    596. How do you invalidate output cache entries when data changes?

    Tag cached responses with Tag("orders") and call IOutputCacheStore.EvictByTagAsync("orders", ct) after a mutation. Eviction is explicit and immediate, unlike relying on expiry. Tag granularly, for example per resource id, to avoid flushing everything on a single update.

    await _cache.EvictByTagAsync($"order-{id}", ct);

    597. How do you cache data that differs per user?

    Include the user or tenant in the cache key, either explicitly or by varying on a claim or header value, and never share a key across identities. Alternatively cache only non-sensitive reference data and keep personalized data out of shared caches. Verify behaviour with two different users, since leaks here are silent.

    598. What is over-posting (mass assignment) and how do you prevent it?

    Over-posting happens when a client can set properties you never intended to expose, such as IsAdmin or Balance, because the entity is bound directly. Prevent it by binding to a request DTO that contains only writable fields and mapping explicitly. Never rely on ignoring properties at serialization time for security.

    public record UpdateUserDto(string DisplayName, string Email); // no IsAdmin

    599. How do you guard against SSRF in an endpoint that fetches a user-supplied URL?

    Validate the scheme, resolve the host and block private, loopback and link-local address ranges, then re-check after redirects. Ideally route outbound calls through an allow-listed proxy or a fixed set of domains. Also set timeouts and response size limits so the call cannot be used for resource exhaustion.

    600. What is the difference between OAuth2 scopes and roles?

    Scopes describe what an application is allowed to do on behalf of a user, such as orders.read, and are part of the access token granted to the client. Roles describe what the user is, such as Administrator, and belong to the identity. APIs typically authorize on scopes for coarse access and on roles or claims for finer business rules.

    601. How do you handle logout and token revocation with JWTs?

    Access tokens are self-contained, so you cannot revoke them before expiry without state; keep lifetimes short and revoke the refresh token instead. For immediate invalidation, check a denylist or a token version claim on each request. Refresh token rotation with reuse detection limits the damage of a stolen token.

    602. How do you design API key authentication for server-to-server clients?

    Issue long random keys, store only a hash with an identifier prefix, and look the key up by a constant-time comparison. Send them in a dedicated header, never in the query string where they end up in logs. Support rotation with overlapping validity and record last-used timestamps.

    603. What is System.Text.Json source generation and why use it?

    A source generator produces serialization code at compile time, removing reflection and startup cost, and it enables trimming and AOT. You annotate a partial JsonSerializerContext and pass its options to the serializer. The trade-off is that every type must be declared in the context.

    [JsonSerializable(typeof(OrderDto))]
public partial class ApiJsonContext : JsonSerializerContext { }

    604. How does response buffering affect streaming endpoints?

    By default Kestrel and proxies may buffer, so small writes are not sent until the buffer fills or the response completes. You must flush explicitly and disable buffering where possible to get true streaming. Also check that compression and proxies do not re-buffer the content.

    605. How do you unit test a controller action in isolation?

    Instantiate the controller with mocked dependencies, call the action, and assert on the returned result object and its value. You do not need the HTTP pipeline for pure logic, which keeps these tests fast. Still add a few integration tests, because mocking misses binding, filters and serialization.

    var sut = new OrdersController(new Mock<IOrderService>().Object);
var result = await sut.Get(1);
Assert.IsType<NotFoundResult>(result.Result);

    606. What does WebApplicationFactory give you in integration tests?

    It boots the real application in memory with its full middleware pipeline, DI container and configuration, then exposes an HttpClient that talks to the test server. You get realistic routing, filters, auth and serialization without opening a port. It also lets you override services and configuration per test class.

    607. How do you replace a real dependency in WebApplicationFactory?

    Override ConfigureWebHost and call ConfigureTestServices to remove or replace registrations; the last registration wins for a single service. Replace the database with a container or an in-memory provider, and replace outbound HTTP with a stub handler. Keep overrides minimal so tests still resemble production.

    builder.ConfigureTestServices(s =>
{
    s.RemoveAll<DbContextOptions<AppDbContext>>();
    s.AddDbContext<AppDbContext>(o => o.UseSqlite(_connection));
});

    608. How do you test authenticated endpoints without a real identity provider?

    Add a test authentication scheme that produces a ClaimsPrincipal from configurable claims, and register it as the default scheme in the test host. Then you can assert 401, 403 and success paths quickly. Do not bypass authorization middleware, since the point is to test it.

    services.AddAuthentication("Test")
    .AddScheme<AuthenticationSchemeOptions, TestAuthHandler>("Test", _ => { });

    609. How do you assert on a ProblemDetails error response in a test?

    Read the content as ProblemDetails or ValidationProblemDetails with the web defaults, then assert on status, title and the errors dictionary. Asserting only on the status code lets contract regressions slip through. Use the same serializer options the API uses.

    610. How do you test a handler that returns TypedResults?

    You can call the static handler directly and inspect the returned result type, for example Ok<OrderDto> or NotFound, which is fast and avoids the pipeline. Then add one integration test per route to cover binding and serialization. Typed results make the direct assertion type-safe without casting to IResult.

    611. How do you decide how much to unit test versus integration test in an API?

    Unit test business rules, validators and mapping where logic is dense and dependencies are easy to fake. Integration test the contracts: routing, status codes, auth, serialization and persistence, because that is where most real defects live. Avoid duplicating every unit test as an integration test; each layer should test what only it can break.

    612. When should you test against a real Kestrel server instead of TestServer?

    TestServer bypasses the network stack, so it cannot catch issues with HTTP/2, TLS, Kestrel limits or real client behaviour. Use a real host for smoke tests of transport-level concerns and for load or contract testing. Keep the fast in-memory tests for everyday feedback.

    613. Why do time-dependent tests become flaky and how do you fix them?

    Tests that call DateTime.UtcNow or wait on real delays fail around boundaries, time zones or slow machines. Inject TimeProvider and use FakeTimeProvider, and replace real HTTP with a stub HttpMessageHandler instead of sleeping. Deterministic tests document the intended behaviour far better.

    services.AddSingleton<TimeProvider>(new FakeTimeProvider(start));

    614. Should you map domain objects to DTOs by hand or with a mapping library?

    Hand-written mapping is explicit, debuggable and fast, and the compiler catches signature changes. Libraries such as AutoMapper reduce boilerplate for large, repetitive shapes but hide errors until runtime and complicate refactoring. Many teams use explicit mapping for API contracts and a library only for internal projections.

    615. How do you avoid over-fetching when building DTOs from EF Core?

    Project directly in the query with Select into the DTO so only the needed columns are read and no change tracking is involved. This also avoids loading navigation properties and triggering lazy loads. Verify the generated SQL, since projecting into methods or computed properties can defeat the translation.

    var dtos = await db.Orders
    .Where(o => o.Status == OrderStatus.Open)
    .Select(o => new OrderSummary(o.Id, o.Total, o.CreatedAt))
    .ToListAsync(ct);

    616. How do you keep request and response DTOs stable as the domain model evolves?

    Treat DTOs as a published contract: change them only deliberately, add rather than rename where possible, and version when a change is breaking. Map at the boundary so internal renames do not leak to clients. Contract tests or an OpenAPI diff in CI catch accidental breaks.

    617. Should API DTOs be records or classes?

    Records give value equality, concise syntax and immutability, which suits response contracts and request payloads. Classes are more flexible when you need mutable binding, parameterless construction or inheritance. For System.Text.Json both work, but records with primary constructors deserialize cleanly and read better in tests.

    618. How do you prevent serialization cycles caused by navigation properties?

    Do not serialize entities; project into DTOs that contain only the fields the client needs. If you must serialize a graph, configure ReferenceHandler.IgnoreCycles or use [JsonIgnore], but that usually signals a modelling problem. Cycles also inflate payloads dramatically.

    619. How should you evolve an enum that is part of a public API contract?

    Serialize as strings so added members do not change existing numeric values, and document that clients must tolerate unknown values. Never renumber or reuse an existing member. If removal is required, deprecate the member and keep accepting it for a transition period.

    [JsonStringEnumConverter]
public enum OrderStatus { Pending, Paid, Shipped, Cancelled }

    620. What is the difference between a request DTO and a command object?

    A request DTO describes the wire contract and is shaped by HTTP concerns, while a command describes an intent in the application layer and carries only domain-relevant data. Mapping between them keeps transport changes from leaking into business logic. In small apps they can be the same type, but separating them pays off as the API grows.

    Advanced 377 questions

    EF Core and data access, performance and diagnostics, distributed systems, messaging, testing, observability, security, modern .NET and architecture.

    621. What is the recommended lifetime for a DbContext in a web request, and why should it not be a singleton?

    A DbContext is designed to be short-lived and scoped per unit of work, which in ASP.NET Core means one instance per HTTP request. It is not thread-safe, so sharing a singleton across concurrent requests causes change-tracker corruption and unrelated exception types such as "A second operation started on this context". A singleton also accumulates tracked entities forever, producing unbounded memory growth and stale first-level cache reads. Use AddDbContext with the scoped default, or IDbContextFactory<T> when you genuinely need short-lived contexts outside a request scope.

    services.AddDbContext<AppDb>(o => o.UseSqlServer(cs));
services.AddDbContextFactory<AppDb>(o => o.UseSqlServer(cs));

    622. How does DbContext pooling work and when does it hurt more than it helps?

    AddDbContextPool keeps fully initialised context instances in a pool so the internal service provider, model and query caches are reused without re-allocation per request. On reset the pool clears the change tracker and other per-unit state, so the semantics stay equivalent to a fresh context. The benefit is real only for high-throughput services where construction cost is measurable; pooling is incompatible with constructor state that differs per request or with interceptors registered as instance state. Also avoid pooling if you keep references to tracked entities after the context is disposed, because the instance will be reused by another request.

    623. Why is a long-lived DbContext considered a bug even in a desktop application?

    Long lifetimes mean the change tracker keeps growing and every SaveChanges call scans an ever larger graph, so save performance degrades superlinearly. Identity resolution guarantees that a later query returns the cached instance instead of fresh database values, which silently serves stale data. Memory also grows because tracked entities and their relationship fixup snapshots are rooted. The fix is a context per logical operation, with explicit loading or projection for the data the operation needs.

    624. How does the change tracker decide whether an entity is Added, Modified or Unchanged?

    For entities returned by a query the tracker creates an entry marked Unchanged and stores original values; later mutation of a property is detected by comparing current against original snapshots during DetectChanges. Entities attached manually via Add become Added, while Attach marks them Unchanged and Update marks them Modified (or Added when the key is default). Disconnected graphs need care because Update marks every property modified, generating wider UPDATE statements than necessary.

    db.Entry(order).Property(o => o.Status).IsModified = true;
db.Entry(order).Property(o => o.RowVersion).OriginalValue = dto.RowVersion;

    625. When is DetectChanges invoked, and how can it cost you performance?

    DetectChanges walks every tracked entity, compares scalar properties with their snapshots and performs relationship fixup, so its cost scales with graph size. It runs implicitly on most public APIs such as SaveChanges, ChangeTracker.Entries() and many query executions. In loops that add thousands of entities, the repeated scans become the dominant cost. Mitigations are batching work, calling ChangeTracker.AutoDetectChangesEnabled = false for bulk load sections and calling DetectChanges once before saving.

    626. What is the difference between tracking, no-tracking and identity resolution?

    Tracking registers entities in the change tracker so modifications are persisted; no-tracking materialises detached instances and skips that bookkeeping entirely. Identity resolution is the guarantee that a single tracked query graph contains exactly one instance per key, which matters for navigation fixup and reference equality. AsNoTracking disables both tracking and identity resolution, while AsNoTrackingWithIdentityResolution keeps one instance per key without tracking it. Use the latter for read-only queries that build object graphs and rely on reference equality.

    627. Why can AsNoTracking cause duplicate objects in an include-heavy query?

    Without identity resolution each row is materialised independently, so a parent joined to several children is instantiated once per row. The result is a list containing multiple distinct objects with the same key, which breaks naïve de-duplication and inflates memory. Switching to AsNoTrackingWithIdentityResolution restores a single instance per key at the cost of a temporary identity map during materialisation. Alternatively project into a DTO shape so the database returns exactly the rows you need.

    628. How do you read fresh values when an entity is already tracked with stale data?

    Query results are not applied over tracked entities, so the tracked instance keeps its old values. Entry(entity).Reload() re-queries that single row and refreshes both current and original values. Entry(entity).GetDatabaseValues() fetches a detached copy you can inspect without overwriting local changes. If you truly need database truth, consider detaching the entity first so the next query materialises a new instance.

    629. What is the correct workflow for adding a migration in a CI pipeline?

    Migrations should be generated during development with the design-time factory or startup project configured, then reviewed and committed as source code. In CI you apply them with dotnet ef migrations script --idempotent or a migration bundle rather than executing database update against production. Generating migrations at deploy time is discouraged because it hides schema drift and makes the artifact non-deterministic. Always diff the generated SQL and run it against a staging copy first.

    dotnet ef migrations add AddOrderIndex
dotnet ef migrations script --idempotent -o deploy.sql

    630. Why is applying migrations automatically at application startup dangerous in production?

    Multiple instances starting concurrently will attempt the same DDL and either deadlock or fail, and a long-running migration can outlive the startup probe and get the pod killed mid-way. Startup migration also couples schema change to code deploy, so a rollback of the app does not roll back the schema. Prefer a separate, single-run migration step in the release pipeline with a lock and a timeout. Keep migrations backward compatible for at least one release so old and new code can run side by side.

    631. How do you make a column rename safe with zero downtime?

    Use the expand and contract pattern: add the new column, dual-write from the application, backfill in batches, switch reads, then drop the old column in a later release. Each step is a separate deploy with its own rollback path, so no single moment breaks running code. Renaming in place is a breaking change because old instances still reference the previous name. The same reasoning applies to type changes and nullability tightening.

    632. How do you handle a migration that must transform data, not just schema?

    Put pure schema changes in migrations and keep heavy data movement in a separate, resumable job. If the transform must ship with the migration, use raw SQL in Up with batched updates to avoid a huge transaction and log growth. Always implement a genuine Down or explicitly document that the migration is irreversible. Test the migration against a production-sized copy, since duration and locking behaviour are the real risks.

    633. What does the delete behaviour of a relationship control, and what are the options?

    Delete behaviour decides what happens to dependents when a principal is deleted. Cascade deletes children, Restrict blocks the delete, NoAction leaves it to the database, SetNull nulls the foreign key and ClientSetNull only nulls tracked entities in memory. EF Core defaults required relationships to Cascade and optional ones to ClientSetNull. Choose explicitly per relationship, because the default can silently delete business data.

    modelBuilder.Entity<Order>()
    .HasMany(o => o.Lines)
    .WithOne(l => l.Order)
    .OnDelete(DeleteBehavior.Cascade);

    634. What is the difference between ClientSetNull and SetNull?

    SetNull creates an ON DELETE SET NULL constraint, so the database nulls the foreign key even for rows the context does not track. ClientSetNull creates no database action and only fixes up loaded entities in memory, so untracked dependents keep a dangling key. The latter is the default for optional relationships and often surprises developers when orphan rows remain. For integrity, prefer Restrict plus explicit soft delete in most business domains.

    635. Why is soft delete usually better than cascade delete in a business system?

    Cascades destroy audit history and can silently remove large subtrees through chains of relationships. Soft delete keeps rows for reporting, compliance and undo, and makes deletion a normal update that fits optimistic concurrency. Implement it with a global query filter plus an interceptor that converts deletes into updates. Remember that filters hide data from queries but not from unique indexes, so plan for filtered indexes on the deleted flag.

    modelBuilder.Entity<Order>().HasQueryFilter(o => !o.IsDeleted);

    636. What is an owned entity type and when should you model a value object that way?

    Owned types are mapped as part of the owner's table rather than as separate entities with their own identity, which fits value objects like Money or Address. They are configured with OwnsOne or OwnsMany, and the owner's key propagates into the owned table. Because they have no identity of their own, they cannot be shared between owners and nullability is controlled by the containing column set. Use a separate entity once the concept needs its own lifecycle, queries or references.

    modelBuilder.Entity<Customer>().OwnsOne(c => c.Address, a =>
{
    a.Property(x => x.City).HasMaxLength(80);
});

    637. What are the practical limitations of owned types in EF Core?

    Owned collections require a separate table keyed by owner plus a synthetic discriminator, and they cannot be queried independently of the owner. Nullable owned references are awkward because all columns are inline, and EF cannot distinguish "no address" from "address with null fields" without an extra flag. Changing an owned instance is tracked as modification of the owner rather than as its own state machine. For anything with real behaviour and identity, a normal entity is simpler.

    638. How do you map a JSON column and query inside it efficiently?

    Map to a JSON column with OwnsOne(...).ToJson() for a document shape, or use HasColumnType("jsonb") with a value converter for a raw string. EF Core translates member access on owned JSON to JSON path expressions, so predicates run server-side without deserialising the whole document. For PostgreSQL, add a GIN index on the jsonb column; on SQL Server use computed columns plus indexes, since indexing inside JSON is limited. Aggregating or sorting on arbitrary JSON paths usually forces a scan.

    CREATE INDEX ix_order_meta ON orders USING gin (metadata jsonb_path_ops);

    639. When would you choose JSON columns over a normalised child table?

    JSON suits sparse, schema-flexible payloads that are always read and written as a whole, such as webhook bodies, feature flags or third-party snapshots. Normalised tables win when you filter, join, aggregate or enforce referential integrity on the individual fields. The decisive question is whether the database must reason about the inner values or merely store and return them. Mixed approaches are common: promote the hot fields to real columns and keep the remainder as JSON.

    640. What are temporal tables and how does EF Core support them?

    SQL Server system-versioned temporal tables keep a full history of row versions in a companion history table, queried with FOR SYSTEM_TIME. EF Core can create them via IsTemporal() and exposes TemporalAsOf, TemporalAll and related operators for point-in-time queries. They give free audit history without triggers, at the cost of extra storage and write overhead. Schema changes on temporal tables are restricted, so plan migrations carefully.

    modelBuilder.Entity<Order>().ToTable("Orders", t => t.IsTemporal());
var past = db.Orders.TemporalAsOf(instant).ToList();

    641. How do eager, explicit and lazy loading differ, and why is lazy loading risky?

    Eager loading pulls related data in the same query via Include, explicit loading issues a separate query on demand via Load, and lazy loading transparently queries when a navigation property is first accessed. Lazy loading is risky because it hides round-trips inside property access, so a loop over a collection produces the classic N+1 pattern and latency you cannot see in the query log. It also requires virtual navigations and proxies, which interfere with serialisation and detached scenarios. In server code prefer explicit projection or eager loading.

    642. How do you fix an N+1 problem you discovered in a list endpoint?

    Replace the per-item lookup with a single projection that joins the needed data, or batch the ids and issue one query with an IN clause. Include works for straightforward graphs, but projection into a DTO is usually cheaper because it selects only required columns. Verify with query logging that the count of round-trips is constant regardless of page size. Adding a covering index often removes the remaining cost.

    var page = await db.Orders.AsNoTracking()
    .Where(o => o.CustomerId == id)
    .Select(o => new OrderDto(o.Id, o.Total, o.Lines.Count))
    .ToListAsync();

    643. How can you detect N+1 queries in production rather than in tests?

    Instrument the data layer with a diagnostic listener or interceptor that counts commands per logical operation and logs the correlation id with the count. In OpenTelemetry, EF Core emits activity spans per command, so you can alert on services whose span count per request spikes. APM tools such as Application Insights show the dependency call histogram, which reveals the repeated pattern. A cheap safeguard is a debug-only assertion that fails when more than N commands execute in one request scope.

    644. What is the cartesian explosion problem with split queries?

    Including two or more collections in one SELECT produces the cross product of their rows, so a parent with 10 orders and 10 lines returns 100 rows and duplicates payload. AsSplitQuery issues one query per collection, trading the cross product for multiple round-trips. Split queries are consistent only inside a transaction or snapshot, otherwise concurrent writes can produce an inconsistent graph. Choose per query: use split for multiple large collections, single query for one collection or small data.

    var orders = await db.Orders
    .Include(o => o.Lines)
    .Include(o => o.Payments)
    .AsSplitQuery()
    .ToListAsync();

    645. What is a compiled query and when does it actually help?

    A compiled query caches the translation of a LINQ expression into SQL, skipping the model-dependent query pipeline on subsequent executions. It helps only for hot paths executed at high frequency with the same shape, where translation shows up in profiles. EF Core's internal query cache already covers most cases, so gains are modest and mostly in very tight loops. With EF.CompileAsyncQuery the delegate is static, so it cannot capture instance state.

    static readonly Func<AppDb, int, Task<Order?>> ById =
    EF.CompileAsyncQuery((AppDb db, int id) => db.Orders.FirstOrDefault(o => o.Id == id));

    646. How do you safely execute raw SQL with parameters in EF Core?

    Use FromSqlInterpolated or FromSql with DbParameter objects so values are sent as parameters rather than concatenated into the text. Interpolated strings passed to FromSqlInterpolated are converted to parameters, which makes them safe by construction. Only use FromSqlRaw with a parameter array, never with string concatenation of user input. Note that raw SQL composing with Include requires the query to be composable, meaning it must start with SELECT and not end with a semicolon.

    var orders = await db.Orders
    .FromSqlInterpolated($"SELECT * FROM Orders WHERE CustomerId = {id}")
    .ToListAsync();

    647. When is Dapper a better choice than EF Core for a specific query?

    Dapper is a thin mapper over ADO.NET, so it adds almost no overhead and gives exact control over the SQL and result shape. It suits reporting queries, hand-tuned joins, bulk reads and legacy schemas that do not map cleanly to entities. The trade-off is losing change tracking, migrations, LINQ composition and model validation. Many teams mix both: EF Core for writes and aggregates, Dapper for read models.

    648. How do savepoints work with EF Core transactions?

    Calling BeginTransaction starts a database transaction, and SaveChanges inside it may create a savepoint automatically when multiple batches are sent. You can create and roll back to a savepoint explicitly via CreateSavepoint and RollbackToSavepoint on the transaction. Savepoints let you discard part of the work without aborting everything, which is useful for optional side effects. Not all providers support them, so verify against your database.

    await using var tx = await db.Database.BeginTransactionAsync();
await tx.CreateSavepointAsync("beforeAudit");
await tx.RollbackToSavepointAsync("beforeAudit");
await tx.CommitAsync();

    649. What is an ambient transaction and how does TransactionScope interact with EF Core?

    An ambient transaction is one stored in Transaction.Current so any enlisted resource participates without passing it explicitly. TransactionScope can span multiple DbContexts or a DbContext plus raw ADO.NET, which is useful for cross-context atomicity. It escalates to a distributed transaction when more than one resource enlists, which is slow and often blocked in cloud environments. Prefer a single context with an explicit BeginTransaction whenever possible.

    650. How do you implement optimistic concurrency with a rowversion column?

    Mark a byte[] property as IsRowVersion so EF includes the original value in the WHERE clause of UPDATE and DELETE. If no row matches, EF throws DbUpdateConcurrencyException and exposes the affected entries. Handle it by reloading database values, re-applying the user's intended changes and retrying, or by surfacing a conflict to the caller. Expose the token to clients so they can send it back as an ETag.

    catch (DbUpdateConcurrencyException ex)
{
    var entry = ex.Entries.Single();
    await entry.ReloadAsync();
}

    651. How do you implement pessimistic locking without breaking connection pooling?

    Pessimistic locking requires a held transaction, so open a transaction, issue a locking read such as SELECT ... WITH (UPDLOCK, ROWLOCK) or FOR UPDATE, do the work and commit quickly. Keep the lock window small, because locks block other sessions and long holds cause timeouts and deadlocks. Never hold a lock across an HTTP call or user think time; use a queue or state machine instead. Order lock acquisition consistently to reduce deadlock probability.

    SELECT * FROM Inventory WITH (UPDLOCK, ROWLOCK) WHERE Sku = @sku;

    652. What does EnableRetryOnFailure change about your code?

    The execution strategy automatically retries transient failures such as deadlocks, connection drops and throttling, wrapping the whole unit of work. Because a retry may re-execute the delegate, your code must be idempotent, especially for operations with external side effects. Explicit transactions must be started through Database.CreateExecutionStrategy().ExecuteAsync, otherwise EF throws that the configured strategy does not support user-initiated transactions. Do not retry non-transient errors like constraint violations.

    var strategy = db.Database.CreateExecutionStrategy();
await strategy.ExecuteAsync(async () =>
{
    await using var tx = await db.Database.BeginTransactionAsync();
    // work
    await tx.CommitAsync();
});

    653. Why are bulk inserts slow with plain SaveChanges and what are the alternatives?

    SaveChanges sends parameterised INSERTs, and even with batching the per-row overhead in the change tracker dominates for tens of thousands of rows. Options are provider bulk copy such as SqlBulkCopy or Npgsql binary import, third-party libraries like EFCore.BulkExtensions, or staging tables plus a set-based MERGE. Disabling AutoDetectChanges and using AddRange gives a modest improvement but is not a substitute for bulk copy. Always measure throughput per second rather than assuming.

    654. How does MaxBatchSize affect insert performance and failure behaviour?

    EF Core groups statements into batches limited by MaxBatchSize and provider parameter limits, which reduces round-trips dramatically. Larger batches improve throughput but widen the transaction and increase memory and lock duration. When one statement in a batch fails, the whole batch fails and EF must work out which entries succeeded, so error handling becomes coarser. Tune the value against real payload sizes rather than leaving large defaults unexamined.

    655. How do you decide which indexes to create for a slow query?

    Read the actual execution plan, find the operator consuming the most cost, and check whether scans are caused by missing or non-sargable predicates. Design composite indexes with equality columns first and the range or sort column last, and include covering columns to avoid key lookups. Verify with the plan that the index is actually used, then measure logical reads before and after. Every index adds write cost and storage, so remove unused ones.

    CREATE INDEX ix_orders_customer_created
ON Orders (CustomerId, CreatedAt DESC) INCLUDE (Total);

    656. Why can a query be slow even when the correct index exists?

    Common causes are parameter sniffing with a plan built for atypical values, stale statistics, implicit conversions that make the predicate non-sargable, and functions applied to the indexed column. Skewed data distribution can also make one cached plan wrong for most executions. Investigate with the actual plan and sys.dm_exec_query_stats rather than guessing. Fixes include updating statistics, rewriting the predicate, or using OPTION (RECOMPILE) for genuinely variable queries.

    657. How does keyset pagination work and why is it better than OFFSET at scale?

    Keyset pagination filters on the last seen sort key, for example WHERE (CreatedAt, Id) < (@ts, @id) ORDER BY CreatedAt DESC, Id DESC, so the engine seeks directly to the position. OFFSET must generate and discard all preceding rows, so cost grows linearly with page depth. Keyset requires a stable, unique tiebreaker and cannot jump to an arbitrary page number. It also fits infinite scroll far better than a page counter.

    SELECT Id, CreatedAt FROM Orders
WHERE (CreatedAt, Id) < (@ts, @id)
ORDER BY CreatedAt DESC, Id DESC
LIMIT 50;

    658. What makes a pagination query unstable and how do you prevent it?

    Sorting on a non-unique column means rows with equal values can appear on multiple pages or be skipped entirely. Always add a unique tiebreaker such as the primary key to the ORDER BY and to the keyset predicate. Also beware of concurrent inserts shifting offsets, which OFFSET pagination cannot handle. If the UI needs a total count, compute it separately and accept eventual consistency.

    659. How does connection pooling behave when a connection is not disposed?

    The pool has a maximum size; leaked connections are never returned, so the pool exhausts and new requests block until the connection timeout fires. Symptoms are timeouts with a healthy database and rising active session counts. Always dispose connections and contexts with using or await using, and check for async-over-sync calls that hold connections longer than needed. Enable pool exhaustion metrics and log a stack trace when acquisition exceeds a threshold.

    660. What are the important connection string settings for pooling in high-throughput services?

    Max Pool Size caps concurrency to the database, Min Pool Size avoids cold starts, and Connection Lifetime helps with failover by recycling old connections. Connection Idle Timeout trims idle connections, which matters for serverless and bursty workloads. Typically the right pool size is far smaller than the thread count, because the database is the bottleneck. Pair these with command timeouts so a stuck query cannot pin a connection forever.

    661. How would you design a repository layer over EF Core without hiding its power?

    A useful repository exposes intent-level operations for an aggregate, such as GetById, Add and a couple of purpose-built queries, rather than a generic CRUD interface over every entity. Keep IQueryable out of the public surface, because leaking it lets callers build arbitrary queries and defeats the abstraction. Allow read models to be queried directly with projections from the composition root. The point is testability and a clear boundary, not shielding developers from SQL.

    662. What are the trade-offs of the specification pattern with EF Core?

    Specifications package reusable query criteria and can be composed into expressions, which helps keep query logic out of controllers and services. The cost is indirection: generated SQL becomes harder to predict and includes and projections may not compose cleanly. Overuse leads to a parallel expression language that duplicates LINQ. Use them for genuinely reused, domain-meaningful filters, and keep complex reporting queries explicit.

    663. How do you define an aggregate boundary when using EF Core?

    An aggregate is a consistency boundary: everything inside is loaded and saved together, and external references go through the root's identity only. Keep aggregates small, because large graphs cause concurrency conflicts, heavy queries and lock contention. Reference other aggregates by id rather than navigation, and use eventual consistency between them. EF Core mapping should follow the model, not dictate it, so avoid the temptation to map every relationship for convenience.

    664. What are the semantics of a unit of work with EF Core, and where do people get it wrong?

    The DbContext is the unit of work: it tracks changes and flushes them atomically on SaveChanges, which itself wraps the batch in a transaction. Mistakes include calling SaveChanges inside loops, spreading one business operation over several contexts, and committing before external side effects succeed. A correct unit of work has one commit point per use case and emits integration events only after that commit. Outbox patterns bridge the gap when messaging must be atomic with the database.

    665. How do memory, distributed and HTTP caching differ in an application architecture?

    In-memory caching is per process and fastest but inconsistent across instances; distributed caching like Redis is shared and survives restarts but adds a network hop. HTTP caching with ETag and Cache-Control delegates freshness decisions to clients and proxies, which removes load entirely but requires careful invalidation. Layer them: HTTP for public GETs, distributed for cross-instance shared data, memory for tiny hot lookups with short TTLs. Every layer needs an explicit invalidation strategy.

    666. How do you invalidate a distributed cache safely?

    Prefer versioned keys or short TTLs over broadcast deletes, because delete messages can be lost or reordered. Write-through with a version stamp means readers compute the key from the current version and old entries expire naturally. If you must delete, publish an invalidation event through a reliable bus and accept a brief window of staleness. Never delete before the database commit, or readers will repopulate with stale data.

    667. What Redis data structures would you use for a leaderboard and a rate limiter?

    A sorted set gives O(log n) ranking with ZADD and range queries for leaderboards and pagination by rank. A rate limiter maps naturally to a counter with expiry, or to a sorted set of timestamps for sliding windows. Use Lua scripts to make check-and-set atomic, since separate commands can interleave under concurrency. Set TTLs on every key to avoid unbounded growth.

    local c = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])
if c == 1 then redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1]) end
return c

    668. What are the common Redis caching anti-patterns in .NET services?

    Storing large object graphs as one blob causes serialisation cost and thundering-herd rebuilds on expiry. Using KEYS in production blocks the server, so use SCAN instead. Ignoring connection multiplexing by creating a client per request exhausts sockets. Also avoid caching per-user data under shared keys, which leaks data across users.

    669. When is BenchmarkDotNet the right tool and what does it protect you from?

    BenchmarkDotNet runs warmed-up, statistically analysed iterations across processes, so it removes JIT, tiering and noise artifacts that make manual stopwatch measurements misleading. Use it to compare implementations of a hot path, not to measure end-to-end service latency. Always validate results with the reported error margins and run in Release. Beware of dead-code elimination, which requires consuming results, typically via a return value or Consumer.

    [Benchmark]
public int Parse() => int.Parse("12345");

[GlobalSetup] public void Setup() { }

    670. How do you use dotnet-counters to confirm a suspicion about GC pressure?

    Attach with dotnet-counters monitor and watch allocation rate, gen0 and gen2 collection counts, heap size, GC pause percentage and thread pool queue length. A high allocation rate with frequent gen2 collections points to large object allocations or long-lived caches. Rising thread pool queue length with low CPU indicates blocking or starvation. Counters tell you where to look; they do not explain why.

    dotnet-counters monitor -p 1234 --counters System.Runtime

    671. When would you reach for dotnet-trace instead of dotnet-counters?

    Counters give cheap aggregate signals, while traces capture events over time so you can see call stacks, allocation sites and contention. Use dotnet-trace collect with the CPU sampling or GC provider when you need to know which method burns CPU or allocates. The resulting nettrace file opens in Visual Studio or PerfView for analysis. Keep collection windows short and targeted, since tracing adds overhead.

    672. How do you approach a memory leak investigation with dotnet-dump?

    Capture two dumps separated by time under similar load, then compare the heap using dumpheap -stat and gcroot to find what grew and who roots it. Look for unexpected counts of a type rather than only total size, because a leak is often many small objects. gcroot traces the path to a static, event handler or thread that keeps the object alive. Confirm the hypothesis in code before changing anything.

    dotnet-dump collect -p 1234 -o leak.dmp
dotnet-dump analyze leak.dmp
> dumpheap -stat

    673. What are typical managed memory leak causes in long-running .NET services?

    Event handler subscriptions never unsubscribed, static collections used as caches without eviction, and captured closures in long-lived timers or background tasks. Unbounded channels and concurrent dictionaries keyed by tenant or request id are frequent culprits. Async state machines can also root objects when a task never completes. Use weak references or explicit disposal, and add memory ceilings with metrics and alerts.

    674. How do workstation and server GC differ, and how do you choose?

    Workstation GC uses one heap and one GC thread and suits client apps with small heaps and low core counts. Server GC creates a heap per core with parallel collection, favouring throughput on multi-core servers at the cost of higher memory. Container deployments should set DOTNET_GCConserveMemory or use System.GC.HeapCount to avoid one heap per host core. Measure pause times as well as throughput before switching.

    <ServerGarbageCollection>true</ServerGarbageCollection>
<ConcurrentGarbageCollection>false</ConcurrentGarbageCollection>

    675. What is the Large Object Heap and what problems does it cause?

    Objects of 85000 bytes or more go to the LOH, which is collected only in gen2 and is not compacted by default. Repeated large allocations fragment the heap, raising memory usage and pause times. Buffer pools and ArrayPool avoid churn, and GCSettings.LargeObjectHeapCompactionMode can compact once when necessary. Streaming APIs are better than buffering entire payloads.

    676. How do you diagnose thread pool starvation?

    The signature is high latency with low CPU, a growing thread pool queue and injection of new threads at roughly one per second. Causes are sync-over-async calls such as .Result or .Wait(), blocking locks held during I/O, and long-running synchronous work on pool threads. Capture a trace and look for threads blocked in Monitor.Wait or waiting on tasks. Fix by removing blocking calls and using async all the way, not by raising MinThreads as a permanent remedy.

    677. Which allocations would you target first when optimising a hot path?

    Start with the highest-volume small allocations: boxing of value types, string concatenation in loops, closures capturing variables, and LINQ iterators in tight loops. Then check for large temporary arrays and buffered reads that could stream instead. Use the allocation profiler to rank by bytes and by count, since high-count small objects drive GC frequency. Optimise only code proven hot by measurement.

    678. How does boxing show up in performance profiles and how do you avoid it?

    Boxing allocates a heap object for a value type whenever it is converted to object or an interface, so profiles show allocations attributed to the call site rather than the type. Common sources are non-generic collections, string.Format with value arguments, and interface-typed parameters. Generic APIs and Span<T> avoid it. Watch for implicit conversions in logging and dictionary lookups.

    // boxing
