object o = 42;
// no boxing
void Log<T>(T value) { }

    679. What string handling practices matter most for throughput?

    Prefer StringBuilder or interpolation handlers over repeated concatenation, and use string.Create for allocation-free formatting. Compare with StringComparison.Ordinal for non-linguistic data, which is much faster and avoids culture surprises. ReadOnlySpan<char> slicing avoids substrings entirely. For high-volume parsing, avoid Split, which allocates arrays and strings.

    680. How do Span and Memory enable allocation-free parsing?

    Span<T> is a stack-only view over contiguous memory, so slicing a buffer creates no allocation. Memory<T> is the heap-friendly counterpart that can be stored in fields and used across awaits. Together with Utf8Parser and SequenceReader they let you parse bytes in place. The restriction is that Span cannot cross an await boundary.

    ReadOnlySpan<byte> span = buffer.AsSpan(0, read);
Utf8Parser.TryParse(span, out int value, out int consumed);

    681. When are System.IO.Pipelines a better abstraction than a Stream?

    Pipelines separate reading from processing, so the transport thread can fill buffers while your parser works on them, which suits protocol servers and high-throughput ingestion. They handle partial frames naturally through ReadOnlySequence and back-pressure via flow control. Streams are simpler for file and sequential I/O and integrate with existing APIs. Reach for pipelines when you own the protocol parsing and need to minimise copies.

    682. How does ObjectPool help and where does it introduce risk?

    Object pooling reuses expensive instances such as large buffers or serialisers, cutting GC pressure and allocation cost. The risk is state leakage: a pooled object returned dirty will corrupt the next user, so always reset on return. Pools also retain memory, so size them and consider trimming. For plain byte arrays, ArrayPool<byte> is usually enough.

    var buffer = ArrayPool<byte>.Shared.Rent(4096);
try { /* use */ }
finally { ArrayPool<byte>.Shared.Return(buffer, clearArray: true); }

    683. How do source generators improve .NET application performance?

    Source generators run at compile time and emit code, so work that would otherwise happen via reflection or runtime expression compilation disappears from startup and steady state. Examples are System.Text.Json source generation, regex generators, and logging generators that avoid boxing and parsing. The benefit is measurable in startup time, AOT compatibility and allocations. The cost is build time and harder debugging of generated code.

    [JsonSerializable(typeof(OrderDto))]
internal partial class AppJsonContext : JsonSerializerContext { }

    684. What breaks when you enable trimming or Native AOT?

    Reflection, dynamic assembly loading, Activator.CreateInstance on unannotated types, and serialisers that discover members at runtime all fail once unused code is removed. Libraries must be trim-annotated; otherwise you get warnings and runtime MissingMethodException. Configuration binding, DI scanning and some ORM features are common casualties. Test the published trimmed build, not just the debug build.

    <PublishTrimmed>true</PublishTrimmed>
<TrimMode>partial</TrimMode>

    685. What are the practical limits of Native AOT for a typical web API?

    AOT gives fast startup, low memory and small containers, but it forbids runtime code generation, so dynamic LINQ, some serialisers and reflection-heavy ORMs are out. EF Core has limited AOT support, and compiled queries must be pre-generated. Build times and cross-compilation constraints also affect the pipeline. Evaluate AOT for small, latency-sensitive services rather than large line-of-business apps.

    686. How do you reduce application startup time?

    Measure first with the startup hook or dotnet-trace, since most time is usually in JIT, assembly loading, DI graph construction and configuration or network calls. ReadyToRun images cut JIT cost, and reducing the number of assemblies and reflection-based registrations helps further. Move non-critical initialisation off the startup path into background services. Avoid synchronous calls to external systems during startup.

    dotnet publish -c Release -p:PublishReadyToRun=true -r linux-x64

    687. What is ReadyToRun and what does it not do?

    ReadyToRun precompiles IL to native code at publish time, reducing JIT work and improving cold start while remaining portable across platforms when published per RID. It does not eliminate the runtime or enable full AOT, and the native code may still be less optimised than tiered JIT output. Binaries are larger. Combine it with tiered compilation settings tuned for your workload.

    688. How do you set and defend a performance budget for a service?

    Express budgets as user-visible outcomes: p95 latency, error rate, throughput per instance, memory ceiling and startup time. Derive them from product requirements and traffic forecasts, then allocate the budget across layers so no single dependency consumes it all. Enforce them in CI with load tests and fail the build on regression beyond a threshold. Revisit after each major architectural change.

    689. How should you design a load test to be meaningful?

    Model the traffic shape from production: realistic read and write ratios, payload sizes, think times and ramp-up rather than a flat hammer. Separate load testing from stress testing and soak testing, because each answers a different question. Include downstream dependencies or faithful stubs, since a mocked database hides the real bottleneck. Record p50, p95, p99 and error rate together, and keep a baseline run for comparison.

    690. What is the difference between p95 and average latency, and why does it matter?

    The average smooths over the tail, so a fast majority hides the slow requests that users actually complain about. p95 means 5 percent of requests are slower than that value, which is a better proxy for perceived performance. Report several percentiles plus the max within a window, and keep the sample count visible. Never compare p95 across systems with very different traffic volumes.

    691. How do you profile database round-trips in a distributed request?

    Correlate the request id across application logs, EF Core command logs and database-side activity to reconstruct the timeline. OpenTelemetry spans for each command show duration and SQL text, and the trace waterfall exposes calls that were hidden inside loops. Server-side tools such as Query Store or pg_stat_statements add real execution time independent of the client. Compare the two to separate network latency from database work.

    692. How would you capacity plan for a service that must sustain a traffic peak?

    Measure throughput per instance under a realistic load test, then add headroom for failover, GC pauses and rolling deploys. Identify the binding constraint first, which is usually the database or a downstream dependency rather than the app tier. Model peak-to-average ratios from historical data and plan for the failure of one availability zone. Validate with a game day that removes an instance during peak.

    693. What is query plan caching and how can it produce unpredictable latency?

    The database caches a compiled plan per statement shape, so the same query text reuses a plan built for the first parameter values it saw. With skewed data that plan can be optimal for one tenant and catastrophic for another. Symptoms are queries that are fast in isolation but slow in production for specific inputs. Solutions include plan guides, OPTION (RECOMPILE), or splitting hot and cold code paths.

    694. How do you keep an EF Core query sargable?

    Keep the indexed column alone on one side of the comparison and avoid functions, arithmetic or casts on it. Convert computed predicates into range predicates where possible, for example CreatedAt >= start && CreatedAt < end instead of truncating the date. Ensure parameter types match the column types so no implicit conversion occurs. Verify by checking that the plan shows a seek rather than a scan.

    // non-sargable
.Where(o => o.CreatedAt.Year == 2026)
// sargable
.Where(o => o.CreatedAt >= new DateTime(2026,1,1) && o.CreatedAt < new DateTime(2027,1,1))

    695. Which LINQ constructs silently fall back to client evaluation in EF Core?

    Methods EF cannot translate, custom C# functions, some string overloads without comparison arguments and complex projections can force client evaluation or throw. Modern EF Core throws for the final projection in most cases, but a partially client-evaluated subquery may still pull excessive rows. Read the warnings logged at Warning level during development. Keep predicates translatable and do post-processing in memory explicitly.

    696. How do you avoid loading an entire table when only a few columns are needed?

    Project into a DTO or anonymous type so the generated SQL selects only the required columns. This also avoids tracking and bypasses the identity map, which is faster and safer for read paths. Use AsNoTracking when the full entity is genuinely needed. Check the generated SQL, because a projection that references navigation properties can still join broadly.

    697. Why can Include make a query slower than separate queries?

    Each Include adds joins and duplicate parent columns, so wide rows and cartesian products inflate network traffic and materialisation cost. Multiple collection includes are the worst case, and they may also break pagination semantics. Split queries or explicit projection are often cheaper. Measure the actual SQL and row counts rather than assuming Include is free.

    698. How does EF Core handle transactions when SaveChanges spans multiple batches?

    When more than one batch is required, EF Core creates a transaction automatically so the whole save is atomic. If an ambient transaction already exists, it participates in that instead. Providers that support savepoints use them so a failed batch can be rolled back without discarding earlier work. Disabling automatic transactions is possible but rarely wise.

    699. What is the risk of calling SaveChanges inside a loop?

    Each call opens a transaction, flushes a batch and re-runs change detection, so cost multiplies with the number of iterations. Partial failure also leaves the data half-written unless you wrap the loop in an explicit transaction. Prefer mutating the graph and saving once at the unit-of-work boundary. If you need streaming progress, commit in explicit chunks.

    700. How do interceptors help with cross-cutting data concerns?

    EF Core interceptors let you hook command execution, connection opening, SaveChanges and transaction boundaries without polluting domain code. Typical uses are auditing timestamps, soft delete, slow query logging and injecting tenant filters. Register them through AddInterceptors and keep them fast, because they run on every operation. Prefer them over overriding SaveChanges when the behaviour must also apply to raw SQL.

    public override ValueTask<InterceptionResult<int>> SavingChangesAsync(
    DbContextEventData e, InterceptionResult<int> r, CancellationToken ct)
    => base.SavingChangesAsync(e, r, ct);

    701. How would you implement multi-tenancy at the data layer?

    For a shared schema, add a tenant id column, apply a global query filter from the current tenant context and enforce it in the database with row-level security if available. For stronger isolation use a database or schema per tenant with a tenant-aware connection resolver. Whichever you choose, resolve the tenant from an authenticated claim, never from a client-supplied header alone. Add tests that prove cross-tenant reads are impossible.

    702. Why is a global query filter not a security boundary on its own?

    The filter applies to LINQ queries but not to raw SQL, IgnoreQueryFilters calls or direct database access. Anyone with connection credentials can bypass it, and a single developer mistake removes the filter. Treat it as defence in depth and enforce real authorisation in the database or at the API layer. Also remember filters affect migrations and seed data.

    703. How do you model a many-to-many relationship with payload in EF Core?

    Use an explicit join entity when the link carries data such as quantity or timestamps; EF Core then maps it as two one-to-many relationships. Skip navigations with an implicit join table are fine only for pure associations. Query through the join entity for the payload and keep indexes on both foreign keys. Watch out for cascade paths when both sides are required.

    704. What causes the "multiple cascade paths" error and how do you resolve it?

    SQL Server refuses a schema where a row can be deleted through two different cascade chains, because the outcome is ambiguous. It appears when an entity is reachable from a principal by more than one required relationship. Resolve it by making one path Restrict or NoAction and handling deletion explicitly in code. Another option is to break the relationship into an optional one.

    705. How do you handle concurrency conflicts across aggregates without long locks?

    Keep transactions short, use optimistic concurrency tokens and design operations to be idempotent so retries are safe. Where contention is inherent, serialise through a queue or partition the work by key so conflicts are rare. Escalate to pessimistic locking only for short critical sections. Measure conflict rates rather than guessing.

    706. What is the difference between a deadlock and a lock timeout, and how do you respond to each?

    A deadlock is detected by the database, which kills one victim transaction immediately; a lock timeout occurs when a waiter exceeds the configured threshold. Deadlocks usually indicate inconsistent access order or missing indexes causing scans. Timeouts usually mean long-running transactions or excessive blocking. Both should be retried only when the operation is idempotent.

    707. How do you make a retry policy safe for operations with side effects?

    Make the operation idempotent using a natural key, an idempotency token or a deduplication table, so a replay produces the same outcome. Record intent before performing the side effect, then confirm completion afterwards, which is the basis of the outbox and inbox patterns. Never retry blindly on an ambiguous timeout without checking state. Bound retries with exponential backoff and jitter.

    708. How do you decide between a database transaction and a saga?

    Use a local transaction when all state lives in one database and can commit atomically. When a business operation spans services or external systems, use a saga with compensating actions, because distributed transactions are fragile and often unsupported. Sagas require explicit design for partial failure and intermediate states visible to users. Keep the number of steps small and each step idempotent.

    709. What does the outbox pattern solve and how is it implemented?

    It makes database changes and message publication atomic by writing the message into an outbox table inside the same transaction, then publishing asynchronously. A background relay reads unpublished rows, sends them, and marks them done, with at-least-once semantics requiring consumer idempotency. This avoids the dual-write problem where the commit succeeds but the publish fails. Use ordering keys and a retention policy for the outbox table.

    710. How would you detect and fix a slow first request after deployment?

    Cold start comes from JIT, assembly loading, model building and connection pool warm-up. Fix by publishing ReadyToRun images, pre-warming the EF model, and running a warm-up probe that hits critical paths before the instance receives traffic. Keep startup dependencies minimal and lazy-load optional features. Measure with traces that separate startup from first-request work.

    711. How do you reduce EF Core model-building cost at startup?

    Model building happens once per context type per service provider, so the main cost is a very large model or many context types. Compile the model ahead of time with a generated IModel and register it via UseModel. Reduce the number of distinct context types and avoid heavy conventions. Measure model build time in traces before optimising.

    optionsBuilder.UseModel(AppDbContextModel.Instance);

    712. What are the trade-offs of splitting a large DbContext into several smaller ones?

    Smaller contexts build faster, have clearer boundaries and reduce the chance of accidental cross-aggregate navigation. The cost is more registrations, more model builds and the loss of easy joins across the split. Cross-context operations need explicit transactions or eventual consistency. Split along aggregate or module boundaries, not arbitrarily.

    713. How do you test data access code without making tests slow and flaky?

    Use integration tests against the real provider, typically a containerised database, because in-memory providers do not reproduce SQL semantics, constraints or concurrency. Keep a small number of fast unit tests for domain logic and a broader suite for repositories. Reset state between tests with transactions rolled back or a fresh schema. Seed with builders so tests read clearly.

    714. Why is the EF Core in-memory provider unsuitable for meaningful tests?

    It is a LINQ-to-objects store, so it does not enforce relational constraints, does not translate to SQL and accepts queries the real database would reject. Tests pass locally and fail in production on constraint violations or unsupported translations. SQLite in-memory is a better approximation if you need speed. Use the production provider for anything that must be trusted.

    715. How do you keep read models consistent when using CQRS with separate stores?

    Publish domain events transactionally through an outbox, then project them into the read store with idempotent handlers and a checkpoint per consumer. Accept eventual consistency and expose it in the API through versioning or "read your writes" tokens where needed. Monitor projection lag as a first-class metric. Be able to rebuild projections from the event log.

    716. When is a document database a better fit than a relational one?

    Document stores fit aggregate-shaped data read and written as a unit, with rapidly evolving schemas and horizontal scale-out requirements. Relational databases win when you need ad-hoc joins, strong constraints and mature transactional guarantees. The deciding factor is the query and consistency model, not developer preference. Many systems use both, with relational as the source of truth.

    717. How do you approach a query that got slower after a data volume increase?

    Compare the current plan with the historical one, because the optimiser may have switched from seek to scan once statistics changed. Check for missing indexes on new access patterns, key lookups caused by non-covering indexes and parameter sniffing. Update statistics and consider filtered indexes for skewed subsets. Then verify with logical reads, not just elapsed time.

    718. How do you handle large exports without exhausting memory?

    Stream results with AsAsyncEnumerable or a data reader rather than materialising a list, and write the output incrementally to the response or storage. Disable tracking and avoid buffering middleware for that endpoint. Consider keyset batching so each chunk is a bounded query. Always apply a timeout and cancellation token.

    await foreach (var row in db.Orders.AsNoTracking().AsAsyncEnumerable().WithCancellation(ct))
{
    await writer.WriteAsync(row.ToCsvLine());
}

    719. What are the risks of streaming a response while holding a database connection?

    The connection stays checked out for the whole response, so slow clients occupy pool slots and can starve other requests. Network interruptions surface as exceptions mid-stream, after headers were sent, so the client sees a truncated payload. Mitigate with bounded chunk sizes, timeouts and a connection-per-chunk strategy. Monitor connection pool saturation during large exports.

    720. How do you choose between a surrogate key and a natural key in a relational model?

    Surrogate keys are stable, narrow and immune to business rule changes, which makes them better for clustering and foreign keys. Natural keys carry meaning and can avoid an extra lookup but often change, which cascades through the schema. A common compromise is a surrogate primary key with a unique constraint on the natural key. Keep keys narrow because every non-clustered index carries them.

    721. Why does key width matter for index performance?

    Every non-clustered index stores the clustering key, so a wide key inflates all indexes and the buffer pool footprint. Wide keys also slow comparisons and increase fragmentation. Prefer narrow integer or sequential GUID types and avoid GUID primary keys with random distribution on clustered indexes. If GUIDs are required, use sequential generation.

    722. What problems do random GUID primary keys cause in SQL Server?

    Random values scatter inserts across pages, causing page splits, fragmentation and poor cache locality. The clustered index grows in all directions, and index maintenance becomes heavier. Sequential GUIDs or a separate identity column with a unique GUID column solve it. The same concern applies to any non-monotonic clustered key.

    723. How do you safely deploy a schema change that rewrites a large table?

    Avoid long-held locks by making the change online where the engine supports it, or by using a shadow table and batched copy. Batch the backfill with small transactions and throttling so replication and locking stay healthy. Deploy in expand and contract steps rather than one atomic rewrite. Have a rollback plan that does not require restoring the whole database.

    724. How do you add a NOT NULL column to a large existing table?

    Add it as nullable with a default, backfill in batches, then tighten to NOT NULL in a later migration once all rows are populated. Adding NOT NULL with a default on a large table can rewrite every row and hold a lock. For engines that support metadata-only default constraints the operation is cheap, but do not assume it. Verify behaviour on a production-sized copy.

    725. What is the role of statistics in query performance?

    Statistics describe value distribution, and the optimiser uses them to estimate cardinality and pick join strategies and index usage. Stale statistics after bulk loads cause bad estimates, which produce nested loops over millions of rows or hash joins over ten. Update statistics after large changes and consider sampled versus full scans. Ascending key columns often need special attention because new values fall outside the histogram.

    726. How do you read an execution plan to find the real bottleneck?

    Start with the operator that has the largest cost relative to actual rows, then compare estimated versus actual row counts to detect bad cardinality estimates. Look for scans where a seek is expected, key lookups, sort and hash spills to tempdb, and implicit conversions. Read the plan right to left but judge by actual rows, not the percentage cost. Always use the actual plan for diagnosis.

    727. What does a key lookup in a plan tell you?

    It means the index satisfied the predicate but not the selected columns, so the engine had to fetch rows from the clustered index. A few lookups are harmless, but thousands indicate a non-covering index that should include the requested columns. Alternatively the query selects too many columns and should be projected narrower. Lookups are especially costly when they cause random I/O.

    728. How do you decide whether to denormalise for read performance?

    Denormalise when a measured read workload cannot meet its budget through indexing or caching, and the duplication has a clear owner. Accept the cost of keeping copies consistent, usually through the same transaction or an outbox-driven projection. Prefer materialised read models over scattering duplicate columns. Document the invariant that keeps the copies aligned.

    729. What is the difference between a covering index and an included column?

    Key columns define order and are usable for seeking, sorting and filtering; included columns exist only in the leaf level to satisfy the projection. Including a column avoids a key lookup without widening the seek structure. Include wide columns rather than making them keys. Keep the index narrow enough that maintenance stays cheap.

    730. How does the buffer pool influence apparent query performance?

    Queries reading cached pages avoid disk I/O, so the same query can be fast in a warm system and slow after a restart. Memory pressure from other queries or a large scan can evict hot pages and cause sudden latency spikes. Monitor page life expectancy and cache hit ratio alongside logical and physical reads. Do not tune based on a single cold run.

    731. How would you investigate intermittent latency spikes that do not correlate with traffic?

    Look for background causes: GC pauses, index or statistics maintenance, backup and log growth, autovacuum, container CPU throttling and thread pool injection after a burst. Correlate spikes with infrastructure metrics and GC counters on the same timeline. Check for connection pool churn causing new physical connections. Sustained investigation needs continuous profiling rather than a one-off capture.

    732. What is CPU throttling in containers and how does it appear to .NET?

    When a container exceeds its CPU quota within a scheduling period it is paused, so wall-clock latency rises while CPU usage looks moderate. .NET sees time jumps, and thread pool and timer behaviour degrade because the process is simply not scheduled. Symptoms are latency spikes with low average CPU and high throttling counters. Fix by raising limits, reducing bursty allocations or lowering thread counts.

    733. How does .NET behave when a container has a CPU limit?

    The runtime reads cgroup limits and sizes the thread pool and server GC heaps accordingly, so the process does not assume all host cores are available. This is why container-aware configuration matters and why older runtimes over-allocated threads and memory. Verify by inspecting the reported processor count at startup. Never override the detected count without measuring.

    734. What is tiered compilation and how does it affect benchmark results?

    Tiered compilation starts with quickly generated code and recompiles hot methods with optimising JIT, so performance improves over the first seconds or minutes. Benchmarks must therefore run long enough after warm-up, which BenchmarkDotNet handles automatically. In short-lived processes the fast tier may dominate and hide the steady-state behaviour. Disable tiering only when you have a specific reason.

    735. What is PGO and when would you enable it?

    Dynamic PGO lets the runtime collect type and branch profiles and recompile hot code with better devirtualisation and inlining. It is enabled by default in modern .NET and generally helps, but can add startup cost and code size. Measure with and without for your workload before changing defaults. Static PGO via ReadyToRun is a different mechanism aimed at cold start.

    736. How do you reduce allocation in an ASP.NET Core request pipeline?

    Minimise per-request services, avoid logging with string interpolation on hot paths and use the generated LoggerMessage pattern. Reuse buffers with pooling, and avoid reading entire bodies into strings. Keep middleware lightweight and do not allocate closures in filters. Measure allocations per request with a profiler rather than guessing.

    737. How do you cache serialised responses without serving stale or private data?

    Separate public caches from authenticated responses, and set Cache-Control with private or no-store accordingly. Use ETags for conditional requests so clients revalidate cheaply. Invalidate by versioning the cache key when underlying data changes. Never cache a response keyed only by URL if it varies by user or tenant.

    Response.Headers.CacheControl = "public,max-age=60";
Response.Headers.ETag = etag;

    738. What is a thundering herd on cache expiry and how do you prevent it?

    Many requests miss the same key simultaneously and all recompute the value, saturating the database. Use a per-key lock or a "stale while revalidate" pattern so one caller refreshes while others serve the old value. Add jitter to TTLs to spread expiries. For expensive aggregates, precompute on a schedule instead of on demand.

    739. How do you choose a cache TTL for data that changes unpredictably?

    Derive the TTL from the business tolerance for staleness rather than from a guess about change frequency. Add explicit invalidation for events you can observe, and keep TTLs as a safety net. Shorter TTLs raise load, longer ones raise staleness risk. Instrument cache hit ratio and staleness complaints to tune empirically.

    740. What is cache stampede protection at the distributed layer?

    Distributed locks or single-flight primitives ensure only one node recomputes a missing value while others wait or serve a default. Redis SET NX with a short expiry is a common implementation. Always release locks safely, ideally with a token check, so a slow node cannot delete another's lock. Bound wait times so a lock outage degrades gracefully.

    SET lock:orders:42 <token> NX PX 5000

    741. Why is measuring only the application tier misleading when diagnosing latency?

    Total latency includes DNS, TLS, load balancers, queueing at the proxy, network hops and database time, none of which appear in application spans unless instrumented. A service can show 20 ms internally while users see 400 ms. Instrument at the edge and propagate trace context so the whole path is visible. Compare server-side and client-side timings to locate the gap.

    742. How do you use distributed tracing to find a slow dependency?

    Follow the trace waterfall and find the span that dominates the critical path, not just the largest single duration. Repeated sibling spans reveal N+1 behaviour, while gaps between spans indicate queueing or scheduling delays. Compare percentile traces for the same endpoint to distinguish systematic from sporadic issues. Sampling must retain slow traces, so use tail-based sampling.

    743. What is tail-based sampling and why does it matter for diagnosing tail latency?

    Head-based sampling decides at trace start, so it may discard exactly the slow or failed requests you need. Tail-based sampling buffers a trace and keeps it based on outcome, such as errors or duration above a threshold. This makes p99 investigations feasible without storing everything. It requires more memory and a collector capable of buffering.

    744. How do you correlate slow requests with database activity in production?

    Emit the trace id as a comment or application name on database commands where supported, and log it alongside command duration. On SQL Server, Query Store and extended events can capture the statement text and duration for the same window. Join on timestamp and query hash to find the offending statement. Without correlation you can only guess which query belongs to which request.

    745. What is a reasonable strategy for query timeouts and command cancellation?

    Set a command timeout shorter than the caller's timeout so the database call fails fast with a clear error instead of cascading. Always pass cancellation tokens so client disconnects stop work and release connections. Distinguish timeout errors from connection failures in retry policies. Log the statement and parameters on timeout, but never log secrets.

    746. How do you avoid unbounded result sets from a query API?

    Require pagination with a server-enforced maximum page size and reject or clamp larger requests. Return a continuation token rather than trusting client-supplied offsets. Add a hard cap and a timeout as a backstop. Document limits so clients can design around them.

    747. How do you keep an EF Core query stable when the underlying schema evolves?

    Query through projections into DTOs so schema changes are absorbed in one mapping place, and keep integration tests that assert result shapes. Avoid SELECT * semantics from raw SQL because added columns change the result set. Review generated SQL when upgrading EF Core versions, since translation changes can alter performance. Pin provider versions and test upgrades deliberately.

    748. What changes in query translation should you watch for when upgrading EF Core?

    New versions may translate the same LINQ differently, producing different SQL and performance, and some previously client-evaluated expressions may start throwing. Include and split-query defaults have changed between versions, and JSON and primitive collection support expanded. Read the breaking changes and run the query suite against a staging database. Keep regression tests that assert SQL shape for critical queries.

    749. How do you decide between a single large service and splitting by bounded context?

    Split when teams, deployment cadence or scaling needs genuinely diverge, because each boundary adds operational and consistency cost. Keep a modular monolith when the domain is still being discovered and transactions cross the would-be boundaries frequently. Boundaries should follow business capabilities, not technical layers. Extract services incrementally once the seams are proven.

    750. What is the risk of sharing a database between services?

    A shared schema becomes a de facto public contract, so any migration is a coordinated release across teams and hidden coupling grows. Services also contend for connections, locks and resources. Prefer one schema or database per service, with data exchange through APIs or events. If sharing is unavoidable, treat the tables as read-only contracts owned by one service.

    751. How do you implement idempotency keys for a payment endpoint in ASP.NET Core?

    Require the client to send a unique key in a header, persist it together with a hash of the request payload and the resulting response in the same transaction as the business effect, and reject reuse of the same key with a different payload. On a duplicate request, return the stored response instead of re-executing the operation, which makes retries safe. Use a unique index plus optimistic insert to close the race between concurrent retries, and give keys a bounded lifetime.

    CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ux_idem ON IdempotencyKeys(TenantId, Key);

    752. What is the difference between an idempotency key and a natural idempotency of an operation?

    Operations such as "set status to Shipped" are naturally idempotent because repeating them has no additional effect, while "increment balance" or "append order line" are not. Idempotency keys add artificial idempotency to non-idempotent operations by deduplicating client intent. Prefer designing operations that are naturally idempotent and fall back to keys only where the business action cannot be expressed that way.

    753. How do you deduplicate messages in a consumer that receives at-least-once deliveries?

    Store a processed-message identifier per consumer group in a durable store and check-before-process inside the same transaction that applies the business change. A unique constraint on (consumer, messageId) turns duplicate detection into a single atomic insert, so a crash before commit simply causes reprocessing. Keep the dedup window long enough to cover the broker's maximum redelivery horizon, then purge aggressively to bound storage.

    754. Why is the transactional outbox pattern needed when publishing events from a database transaction?

    Writing to the database and publishing to a broker are two separate systems, so a crash between them either loses the event or produces an event for a rolled-back change. The outbox writes the event into a table in the same local transaction as the state change, and a relay publishes it afterwards. This yields atomicity without distributed transactions at the cost of a small publication delay.

    BEGIN;
UPDATE Orders SET Status = 'Paid' WHERE Id = @id;
INSERT INTO Outbox(Id, Type, Payload) VALUES (@eid, 'OrderPaid', @json);
COMMIT;

    755. How do you publish outbox messages reliably and in order?

    Run a relay that reads undelivered rows ordered by a monotonic sequence per aggregate, publishes them, and marks them sent; use row locking with SKIP LOCKED so multiple relay instances do not collide. Ordering per aggregate is preserved by publishing sequentially per partition key, not globally. Alternatively use CDC such as Debezium to stream the outbox table, which avoids polling but requires operational maturity.

    756. What problem does the inbox pattern solve?

    The inbox records inbound messages that have already been handled so that redelivery does not reapply side effects. It is the consumer-side counterpart of the outbox and is usually implemented as an insert into a processed-messages table in the same transaction as the handler's writes. Together, outbox and inbox approximate exactly-once processing over at-least-once transport.

    757. Explain the saga pattern and when you would choose orchestration over choreography.

    A saga coordinates a long-running business transaction as a sequence of local transactions with compensating actions for failures. Orchestration centralizes the flow in a coordinator, which is easier to reason about, test and observe for complex conditional workflows. Choreography uses events between participants and couples services more loosely but makes end-to-end flow and failure handling harder to trace.

    758. What makes a compensating action correct in a saga?

    Compensation must be semantically meaningful rather than a literal undo, for example issuing a refund instead of deleting a payment. It must be idempotent and retryable because it can itself fail or be delivered twice, and it must be persisted so that a crashed orchestrator can resume. Some steps are not compensable, such as sending an email, so they should be deferred to the end of the saga.

    759. How do you handle a saga step that never completes?

    Give each step an explicit timeout and treat expiry as a failure that triggers compensation or a manual intervention state. Record the saga state durably so a recovery worker can resume after process restarts, and expose stuck sagas as an operational metric. Avoid infinite retries that block the whole flow; escalate to a dead-letter or human queue instead.

    760. What does eventual consistency mean for the user experience, and how do you design for it?

    Reads may briefly return stale data after a write, so the UI must not assume read-your-writes across service boundaries. Design for it with optimistic UI, explicit pending states, polling or push notifications, and by routing read-after-write traffic to the same replica that accepted the write where possible. Also make the convergence time an explicit SLO rather than an accident.

    761. How can you provide read-your-writes consistency in an eventually consistent system?

    Track a version or watermark per write and have the client pass it on subsequent reads, then wait or redirect until a replica has caught up. Alternatively pin the session to the primary for a short window after a write, or return the authoritative result directly from the write response so the client does not need to read it back.

    762. Compare delivery semantics of Kafka, RabbitMQ and Azure Service Bus.

    Kafka offers at-least-once by default and effectively-once within its stream-processing primitives via idempotent producers and transactions; consumers commit offsets and can replay. RabbitMQ gives at-least-once with publisher confirms and manual acks, and redelivers unacked messages after channel or connection loss. Azure Service Bus provides at-least-once with peek-lock and complete/abandon, plus duplicate detection and sessions for ordered processing.

    763. What is the practical reality of exactly-once delivery?

    Exactly-once delivery over an unreliable network is impossible in general, so systems achieve exactly-once effects through idempotent processing or transactional coordination between broker and consumer state. Kafka transactions can make read-process-write atomic when the sink supports them, but once a side effect leaves the transaction boundary, such as an HTTP call, you are back to at-least-once plus deduplication.

    764. How does Kafka guarantee ordering, and what breaks it?

    Kafka preserves order within a partition, so ordering is only as strong as your partitioning key. Increasing partition count, retries without idempotence enabled, or processing with multiple in-flight requests per partition can reorder records. To preserve per-entity order, key by entity id and ensure max.in.flight.requests.per.connection is 1 or idempotence is on.

    765. How do you choose a partition key for an event stream?

    Choose a key that matches the ordering and locality requirement, typically the aggregate or tenant id, and verify that the key distribution avoids hot partitions. Avoid keys with skewed cardinality such as country codes for global traffic, and consider composite keys or salting when a single entity is too hot. Changing the key later is a breaking change because it breaks ordering guarantees for existing data.

    766. What is a poison message and how do you handle it?

    A poison message is one that always fails processing, for example due to malformed payload or a business rule violation, so retrying forever blocks progress. Track a delivery count and after a threshold move the message to a dead-letter queue with failure metadata. Alert on DLQ depth and provide a controlled replay path after the defect is fixed.

    767. What should a dead-letter queue strategy include operationally?

    DLQs need ownership, alerting on non-zero depth, a retention policy and a documented replay procedure that is idempotent. Store the original payload, headers, exception details and attempt count so triage does not require guesswork. Without a replay path a DLQ becomes a silent data-loss bucket, which defeats its purpose.

    768. How do competing consumers interact with ordering?

    Competing consumers scale throughput but destroy global ordering because multiple instances process messages concurrently. Ordering can still be preserved by partitioning and assigning each partition to exactly one consumer, as Kafka consumer groups do. On queues without partitions, use sessions or a single consumer per ordered stream.

    769. How do you scale consumers without losing per-tenant ordering?

    Partition by tenant so all messages for a tenant land on one partition or session, then scale by adding consumers up to the partition count. Beyond that point, increase partitions or split hot tenants into sub-streams with explicit ordering boundaries. Accept that per-tenant throughput is capped by single-partition processing speed.

    770. How do you implement a distributed lock with Redis, and what are its limits?

    Use SET key token NX PX ttl to acquire and release only if the stored token matches, ideally via a Lua script for atomic compare-and-delete. The fundamental limit is that a lock cannot guarantee safety under pauses longer than the TTL, because the holder may continue working after expiry. Redlock reduces but does not eliminate this; prefer fencing tokens or idempotent operations where correctness matters.

    SET lock:order:42 <token> NX PX 10000

    771. What is a fencing token and why does it matter for distributed locks?

    A fencing token is a monotonically increasing value issued with the lock; every write to the protected resource includes it and the resource rejects tokens lower than the highest seen. This prevents a paused lock holder from committing stale work after its lease expired. Without fencing, a lease only provides mutual exclusion under the assumption that process pauses are bounded.

    772. How do you implement leader election with a lease in .NET?

    Use a blob lease in Azure Storage or a lease object in Service Bus that must be renewed before expiry, and only the current lease owner performs the leader work. On renewal failure, stop work immediately and let another instance acquire the lease. Combine with fencing tokens or generation numbers for writes that must not be applied by a stale leader.

    773. Why is retrying without jitter dangerous?

    Simultaneous retries from many clients synchronize into bursts that repeatedly overload a recovering dependency, often called a thundering herd. Full jitter, where the delay is randomized between zero and the exponential cap, spreads retries and dramatically improves recovery. Also cap total attempts and only retry transient, idempotent operations.

    var delay = TimeSpan.FromMilliseconds(Random.Shared.Next(0, (int)capMs));

    774. How does a circuit breaker differ from retries and timeouts?

    A timeout bounds a single call, retries re-attempt a failed call, and a circuit breaker stops calling a failing dependency altogether for a period to allow recovery and fail fast. Used together, the breaker prevents retry storms from consuming threads and connections. Half-open probing with a small number of trial requests determines when to close again.

    775. What is the bulkhead pattern in a .NET service?

    A bulkhead isolates resources so failure in one dependency or tenant cannot exhaust capacity for others, for example separate connection pools, thread pools or concurrency limiters per downstream. It converts a global outage into a partial degradation. It is often implemented with a rate limiter or a semaphore per dependency.

    776. What is request hedging and when is it appropriate?

    Hedging issues a second request to another replica if the first is slow beyond a percentile threshold, using whichever responds first and cancelling the other. It reduces tail latency but increases load, so it only makes sense for idempotent reads with spare capacity. Never hedge writes or calls to a dependency already saturated.

    777. How do you apply backpressure in a message-driven .NET service?

    Limit prefetch and concurrent handlers so the broker does not push more work than the process can absorb, and let unacked messages stay queued. Combine with bounded internal channels that block or shed load when full. Without backpressure the service buffers unboundedly, then fails with memory pressure and loses in-flight work.

    778. How does consumer prefetch affect throughput and fairness in RabbitMQ?

    A higher prefetch increases throughput by keeping the consumer busy but reduces fairness across consumers and increases memory and redelivery volume on crash. Set prefetch from measured per-message latency and concurrency rather than from a default. Very high prefetch also delays detection of slow consumers because their local buffer hides the problem.

    779. What are the core responsibilities of an API gateway?

    A gateway terminates TLS, authenticates and authorizes, applies rate limits and quotas, routes and version-translates, and centralizes cross-cutting concerns such as correlation ids and response compression. It should not become a hidden business logic layer, because that creates a distributed monolith and a scaling bottleneck. Keep transformation minimal and push domain policy into services.

    780. How do you avoid the API gateway becoming a single point of failure?

    Deploy multiple gateway instances across zones, keep the data plane stateless, and use health-checked load balancing with automatic failover. Configure timeouts, circuit breakers and concurrency limits at the gateway so a slow backend cannot exhaust its connections. Treat gateway configuration as code with staged rollouts.

    781. Compare client-side and server-side service discovery in .NET.

    With client-side discovery the caller queries a registry or uses platform DNS plus a load-balancing policy such as gRPC's round-robin resolver, which removes a network hop and allows fine-grained balancing. With server-side discovery the caller hits a stable virtual address and a load balancer or sidecar chooses the instance, which keeps clients simple. Kubernetes service DNS plus kube-proxy is effectively server-side, while headless services plus a client library give client-side behavior.

    782. How does health checking interact with service discovery and load balancing?

    A liveness probe answers whether the process should be restarted, while a readiness probe answers whether it should receive traffic, and conflating them causes restart loops or traffic to unready pods. On graceful shutdown, mark the instance not ready first, then drain in-flight requests before exiting. Discovery should stop routing to instances that fail readiness within a bounded detection window.

    783. When would you choose gRPC over REST for internal service communication?

    gRPC gives strongly typed contracts, compact binary payloads over HTTP/2, streaming and low overhead, which suits high-throughput internal calls and polyglot contracts. It is weaker for browser clients, human-debuggable APIs and public consumption without a proxy. The generated-code workflow also means contract changes require coordinated client regeneration.

    784. What are the main trade-offs between REST and GraphQL?

    GraphQL lets clients request exactly the fields they need in one round trip, which helps diverse front-ends but complicates caching, rate limiting and query cost control. REST is simpler to cache and secure per endpoint, with clearer semantics for HTTP infrastructure. GraphQL shifts complexity to the server, where depth limits, persisted queries and per-field authorization are essential.

    785. What is contract testing and how does it differ from integration testing?

    Contract testing verifies that a consumer's expectations and a provider's actual responses agree, without running both together in a full environment. Consumer-driven contracts such as Pact publish expectations that the provider verifies in its own pipeline, catching breaking changes early. Integration tests exercise real collaboration and catch more, but are slower and flakier, so contracts give fast feedback across service boundaries.

    786. How do you evolve an event schema without breaking consumers?

    Prefer backward-compatible additive changes such as optional fields with defaults, and never repurpose or remove an existing field. Use a schema registry with compatibility checks and a versioned envelope carrying event type and version. When a breaking change is unavoidable, publish a new event type and run both in parallel until consumers migrate.

    787. What is the difference between backward and forward compatibility for messages?

    Backward compatibility means new readers can read old data; forward compatibility means old readers can read new data, typically by ignoring unknown fields. At-least-once systems usually need both during rolling upgrades, since producers and consumers are never on the same version. Tolerant readers that skip unknown fields and treat missing optional fields as defaults are the practical mechanism.

    788. How do you version a public HTTP API in ASP.NET Core?

    Choose one strategy such as URL segment, header or media type, apply it consistently, and document deprecation timelines with sunset headers. Add the new version alongside the old, keep both running, and instrument usage per version so you know when it is safe to retire. Avoid breaking changes inside a version even when they seem harmless, such as tightening validation.

    789. What are the three pillars of OpenTelemetry and how do they relate?

    Traces model a request's path across services as a tree of spans, metrics are aggregated numeric time series, and logs are discrete events. Trace and span ids in logs and exemplars on metrics let you pivot between them. In .NET the OpenTelemetry SDK unifies instrumentation for ASP.NET Core, HttpClient, EF Core and runtime metrics.

    790. How does distributed trace context propagation work across service boundaries?

    The W3C Trace Context standard carries traceparent and tracestate headers, which ASP.NET Core and HttpClient instrument automatically when propagation is configured. Each hop creates a child span with the same trace id and a new span id, preserving causal relationships. Message brokers require manual propagation by copying the headers into message properties, since there is no HTTP request to carry them.

    using var activity = ActivitySource.StartActivity("ProcessOrder");

    791. How do you propagate correlation across a message queue?

    Inject the trace context and a business correlation id into message headers on publish, then extract and start a consumer span as a child of that context on receive. Without this, each consumer message becomes an orphan root span and the end-to-end flow is invisible. Store the correlation id in logs and in any outbox row so retries and manual replays keep the same identifier.

    792. What is structured logging and why does it matter at scale?

    Structured logging emits named properties rather than interpolated strings so entries remain machine-queryable and indexable. It enables filtering by tenant, order id or trace id instead of fragile text search. Message templates should stay constant with varying parameters, which also keeps cardinality and aggregation predictable.

    logger.LogInformation("Order {OrderId} shipped to {Region}", id, region);

    793. How do you control logging cost in a high-volume .NET service?

    Set per-category levels, sample verbose paths such as health checks and successful reads, and never log full payloads by default. Route high-volume diagnostic logs to cheap storage with short retention and reserve indexed storage for errors and audit events. Monitor ingestion volume as an SLO-adjacent metric, because log cost often grows faster than traffic.

    794. How do you avoid high-cardinality metrics that break your monitoring backend?

    Never use unbounded values such as user id, order id or raw URL as metric dimensions; aggregate those into bounded buckets or keep them in traces and logs instead. Keep label sets small and reviewed, since each unique combination is a separate time series. Cardinality explosions are a common cause of monitoring outages during incidents, exactly when you need it most.

    795. What is the difference between an SLI, an SLO and an error budget?

    An SLI is a measured ratio such as successful requests over total requests; an SLO is the target for that SLI over a window, for example 99.9 percent over 30 days. The error budget is the complement, the allowed failure volume, and it governs release velocity. When the budget is exhausted, teams freeze risky changes and prioritize reliability work.

    796. Why should alerts fire on symptoms rather than causes?

    Users experience symptoms such as errors or latency, while causes like high CPU may be normal or harmless, which produces alert fatigue. Alert on SLO burn rate so pages correspond to real user impact and are actionable. Keep cause-based signals as dashboards or tickets for diagnosis rather than paging triggers.

    797. What is multi-window multi-burn-rate alerting?

    It combines a short window to detect fast burns with a long window to confirm significance, reducing both false positives and slow detection. For example page when a 1-hour burn rate exceeds 14x and a 5-minute window confirms it is still ongoing. This approach ties alert urgency directly to how much error budget is being consumed.

    798. What is chaos engineering and what safeguards make it responsible?

    Chaos engineering deliberately injects failures such as latency, instance kills or dependency errors to validate resilience assumptions before they fail in production. Responsible practice starts in staging with a hypothesis, a bounded blast radius, an abort switch and stakeholder awareness. Measure the outcome against an SLI so experiments produce knowledge rather than just incidents.

    799. How would you test that a circuit breaker actually fails fast?

    Inject a dependency that fails a configurable number of times, drive enough calls to open the breaker, then assert that subsequent calls return the fallback or throw immediately without invoking the dependency. Measure elapsed time and the dependency's invocation count, not just the exception type. Also verify the half-open transition after the break duration by allowing one probe to succeed and confirming the breaker closes.

    800. Compare the unit, integration and end-to-end levels of the test pyramid.

    Unit tests exercise a single component in isolation with fast, deterministic execution and form the broad base. Integration tests cross real boundaries such as a database or broker and catch wiring and serialization defects. End-to-end tests validate critical user journeys through the whole system and should be few because they are slow and brittle.

    801. Why is the test pyramid often replaced by a testing trophy in service architectures?

    In distributed systems much of the risk lives at boundaries, so integration tests deliver more value per test than pure unit tests of trivial logic. The trophy shape keeps a solid base of unit tests but emphasizes integration testing and a thin E2E layer. The principle is to invest where defects actually occur rather than to hit a ratio.

    802. What factors should drive your choice between xUnit, NUnit and MSTest?

    All three are capable; xUnit is the common default in modern .NET with strong parallelization and fixture-based dependency injection, NUnit offers richer parameterization and attributes, and MSTest integrates tightly with Microsoft tooling. Choose based on team familiarity and ecosystem rather than raw features, and stay consistent inside a solution. Migration cost between them is rarely worth it without a concrete unmet need.

    803. How does xUnit handle test isolation and shared context?

    xUnit creates a new instance of the test class per test, so instance state is naturally isolated. Shared setup is expressed through IClassFixture for per-class state or ICollectionFixture for state shared across a collection, with collections also controlling parallelization. This differs from NUnit's set-up-once model, which requires more discipline to keep tests independent.

    804. When should you not mock a dependency?

    Do not mock types you do not own, value objects, or anything whose real behavior is cheap and deterministic, because mocks encode your assumptions rather than reality. Avoid mocking to test interactions that are not part of the contract, which produces tests that break on refactoring. Prefer real implementations or in-memory fakes for infrastructure, and reserve mocks for external, slow or non-deterministic collaborators.

    805. What are the pitfalls of mocking with Moq or NSubstitute?

    Over-specified interaction tests break when implementation details change, and loose mocks silently return default values, hiding missing setup. Mocking virtual or interface members only means sealed or static dependencies force awkward wrappers. Prefer asserting observable outcomes and use strict behavior only where a call is genuinely required.

    var repo = Substitute.For<IOrderRepository>();
repo.GetAsync(42).Returns(order);

    806. How does WebApplicationFactory improve integration testing in ASP.NET Core?

    It boots the real application pipeline in-process with TestServer, so routing, filters, model binding, serialization and DI are exercised without network overhead. You can override registrations in ConfigureTestServices to substitute infrastructure. This catches defects that controller-level unit tests miss, such as missing DI registrations or incorrect middleware order.

    var client = factory.WithWebHostBuilder(b =>
    b.ConfigureTestServices(s => s.AddSingleton<IClock, FakeClock>())).CreateClient();

    807. What are the risks of testing against in-memory providers instead of real infrastructure?

    EF Core's in-memory or SQLite provider does not enforce relational constraints, transaction semantics, concurrency tokens or provider-specific SQL, so tests pass where production fails. It also encourages lazy-loading and query patterns that behave differently on a real server. Use a real database via Testcontainers for anything touching persistence behavior.

    808. How does Testcontainers change your integration testing strategy?

    Testcontainers starts real Dockerized dependencies such as SQL Server, PostgreSQL, Redis or Kafka per test run, giving production-faithful behavior with disposable state. It removes the shared-environment problem where tests interfere with each other. The cost is Docker availability in CI and slower startup, mitigated with collection fixtures and reusable containers.

    await new ContainerBuilder().WithImage("postgres:16").WithPortBinding(5432, true).StartAsync();

    809. How do you manage database schema and migrations in automated tests?

    Apply the same migrations used in production rather than EnsureCreated, so migration defects surface in CI. Reset state between tests with transaction rollback where possible, or recreate the schema per collection when isolation demands it. Keep seed data minimal and deterministic, and never depend on data left by another test.

    810. How do you diagnose and eliminate flaky tests?

    Treat flakiness as a defect: capture the seed, timings, logs and machine state, and quarantine the test so it stops eroding trust. Common causes are time and timezone dependencies, unawaited tasks, shared mutable state, port collisions and reliance on ordering. Inject IClock, avoid Thread.Sleep in favor of polling with timeouts, and isolate external resources per test.

    811. What does mutation testing tell you that code coverage does not?

    Coverage measures execution, while mutation testing modifies the code, such as flipping a comparison, and checks whether a test fails. Surviving mutants reveal assertions that are missing or too weak even on fully covered lines. It is computationally expensive, so run it on critical assemblies or incrementally rather than across the whole solution.

    812. How do you design a meaningful performance test for a .NET API?

    Define the question first, such as capacity at a latency SLO, then model a realistic workload mix with think time and payload sizes. Warm up the JIT and connection pools, run against production-like infrastructure, and measure p95 and p99 rather than averages. Report saturation signals like CPU, GC pauses and queue depth alongside throughput so results are explainable.

    813. What is broken object level authorization and why is it the top API risk?

    BOLA occurs when an endpoint accepts an object identifier and returns it without verifying that the caller owns or may access that object. It tops the OWASP API Security Top 10 because it is easy to introduce, hard to detect with scanners, and exposes other tenants' data directly. Mitigate with per-request authorization that binds the object to the authenticated subject and tenant, enforced in the data layer rather than the UI.

    var order = await db.Orders.FirstOrDefaultAsync(o => o.Id == id && o.TenantId == tenantId);

    814. How do you defend against mass assignment in ASP.NET Core?

    Never bind request payloads directly to persistence entities, because clients can then set fields such as IsAdmin or Balance. Use explicit request DTOs containing only client-settable properties and map them to domain objects deliberately. The [Bind] attribute and BindNever are weaker and easy to forget, so separate models are the reliable approach.

    815. What is broken function level authorization?

    It is the failure to restrict administrative or privileged operations to the roles that should perform them, often because authorization is applied at the controller level but not per action. Enumerate every endpoint and require an explicit policy rather than relying on default authenticated access. Test with low-privilege tokens as part of your security regression suite.

    816. How do you prevent excessive data exposure in API responses?

    Return purpose-built response DTOs instead of serializing domain entities, so new internal fields never leak automatically. Apply allow-lists in serialization and review payloads for sensitive fields such as internal ids, emails or tokens. The client filtering data it does not need does not protect anything, since the raw response is visible on the wire.

    817. What is unrestricted resource consumption and how do you mitigate it?

    Attackers can exhaust CPU, memory or downstream quotas by requesting expensive operations repeatedly or with pathological inputs. Mitigate with request size limits, pagination caps, query complexity limits, per-client rate limits and timeouts on expensive work. Also bound concurrency per endpoint so a single client cannot monopolize the service.

    818. When is the client credentials flow appropriate in OAuth 2.0?

    It is for machine-to-machine calls where no user is involved and the client can keep a secret confidential, such as a background service calling an API. The token represents the application itself, so scopes should be minimal and the secret stored in a vault rather than configuration files. It must never be used from a browser or mobile app, where the secret cannot be protected.

    819. How does authorization code flow with PKCE protect public clients?

    The client generates a random code verifier, sends its hash as code_challenge, and must present the original verifier when redeeming the code. An attacker who intercepts the authorization code on a mobile device cannot redeem it without the verifier. PKCE also protects confidential clients against code injection, so it is recommended for all authorization code flows.

    challenge = Base64Url(SHA256(verifier))

    820. Why is the implicit flow deprecated?

    The implicit flow returns access tokens directly in the URL fragment without a back-channel exchange, exposing them to browser history, logs and referrer leakage with no client authentication. Authorization code with PKCE plus a token endpoint exchange now works in browsers without a client secret. Use the current best practice, which also enables refresh tokens and sender-constrained tokens.

    821. How should access token and refresh token lifetimes be chosen?

    Keep access tokens short-lived, commonly five to fifteen minutes, to bound the damage of theft, and use refresh tokens with longer lifetimes plus rotation and reuse detection. Sliding sessions with absolute maximums balance user convenience against risk. Never treat a revoked user as logged out until the access token expires unless you add introspection or a revocation check.

    822. What is refresh token rotation and why does reuse detection matter?

    Each refresh returns a new refresh token and invalidates the previous one, so a stolen token becomes useless once the legitimate client refreshes. If an already-used token is presented, the authorization server assumes compromise and revokes the whole token family. This converts silent long-term access into a detectable security event.

    823. What is the difference between ID token and access token?

    The ID token is a signed assertion of authentication for the client, describing who the user is and when they authenticated, and must not be sent to APIs as authorization. The access token authorizes API calls and is intended for the resource server, which validates it independently. Mixing them up leads to confused-deputy vulnerabilities and broken authorization.

    824. Which JWT validation checks must a .NET API perform?

    Validate the signature against a trusted key set, the issuer, the audience, the lifetime with a small clock skew, and the token type or algorithm allow-list. Set RequireSignedTokens and RequireExpirationTime, and disable all claim-type mapping surprises explicitly. Skipping audience validation allows a token issued for another API to be replayed against yours.

    options.TokenValidationParameters.ValidAudience = "orders-api";
options.TokenValidationParameters.ClockSkew = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30);

    825. Why is accepting the alg header at face value dangerous?

    If the validator trusts the token's alg field, an attacker can present an unsigned token with alg none or switch from RS256 to HS256 and sign with the public key as an HMAC secret. Always configure an explicit algorithm allow-list on the validation parameters so the header cannot select the verification method. The vulnerability is a validator configuration flaw, not a JWT format flaw.

    826. How should the kid header be used during validation?

    kid selects which key from the issuer's JWKS should verify the signature, which enables rotation without invalidating live tokens. The validator must only consider keys fetched from the trusted metadata endpoint, never a key embedded in the token. If kid is missing or unknown, fail closed and refresh the key set once before rejecting.

    827. How do you handle signing key rotation and JWKS caching?

    Fetch JWKS over HTTPS from the issuer's metadata, cache it with a modest TTL, and refresh on an unknown kid with rate limiting to avoid a fetch storm. Publish new keys before signing with them and keep old keys for at least the maximum token lifetime. In .NET, configuration managers handle this automatically when Authority metadata is used.

    828. How do you choose between Azure Key Vault, user secrets and environment variables?

    User secrets are for local development only and are never a deployment mechanism. Environment variables are convenient for containers but visible to process inspection and often end up in logs or crash dumps. Key Vault or an equivalent vault provides access control, auditing and rotation, and should be reached through managed identity rather than stored credentials.

    829. How does managed identity remove secrets from a .NET application?

    The platform injects a federated identity token to the workload, and the app authenticates to Key Vault or storage without any stored credential. DefaultAzureCredential chains local developer credentials with the deployed identity so the same code works in both environments. This eliminates the rotation and leakage problem for the credentials that would otherwise protect everything else.

    builder.Configuration.AddAzureKeyVault(new Uri(vaultUri), new DefaultAzureCredential());

    830. Why should secrets never be placed in appsettings.json or source control?

    Configuration files are copied to build artifacts, container images and log outputs, and git history makes removal effectively impossible. A leaked secret must be treated as compromised and rotated, not just deleted. Use vault references so configuration holds only a pointer, and enable secret scanning with push protection in the repository.

    831. What is the difference between encryption at rest and in transit, and what does each protect against?

    Encryption in transit protects data on the network from interception and tampering, typically TLS with strong cipher suites and certificate validation. Encryption at rest protects stored data from disk or backup theft, but not from a compromised application that has the keys. Neither protects against an attacker who can query the API legitimately, which is why authorization and auditing remain necessary.

    832. How does the ASP.NET Core Data Protection API work and what must you configure in a farm?

    Data Protection encrypts payloads such as cookies and antiforgery tokens with a key ring, and by default keys are stored locally and protected with DPAPI or the machine key. In a multi-instance deployment, all instances must share the same key ring and a stable application name, otherwise cookies issued by one node fail on another. Persist keys to a shared store and protect them with a certificate or vault.

    services.AddDataProtection().PersistKeysToAzureBlobStorage(uri).ProtectKeysWithAzureKeyVault(keyId, cred);

    833. Why is input validation alone insufficient as a security control?

    Validation reduces the attack surface but cannot anticipate every dangerous context, so it must be combined with encoding, parameterization and authorization. Allow-list validation with length, range and format constraints is far more robust than block-listing dangerous characters. Validate on the server always, and treat client-side validation as a usability feature only.

    834. How do you prevent SQL injection in a .NET application?

    Use parameterized queries or an ORM with parameter binding and never concatenate user input into SQL text. Dynamic identifiers such as sort columns cannot be parameterized, so map them through a strict allow-list. Stored procedures do not help if they build SQL dynamically inside.

    await db.Database.ExecuteSqlInterpolatedAsync($"UPDATE Orders SET Status = {status} WHERE Id = {id}");

    835. What is the difference between HTML encoding, attribute encoding and JavaScript encoding?

    Each output context has different escaping rules, and using the wrong one still allows injection. Razor encodes by default for HTML text and attributes, but data placed inside script blocks or URLs needs context-specific encoding. Prefer avoiding inline script with user data entirely, and use JSON serialization APIs when embedding values in JavaScript.

    836. How do you protect an ASP.NET Core API against CSRF?

    Cookie-authenticated endpoints need antiforgery protection because browsers attach cookies automatically to cross-site requests. Use the antiforgery token plus SameSite cookies, and require a custom header for state-changing calls from JavaScript clients. Purely bearer-token APIs are not vulnerable in the same way, because the browser does not attach the token automatically.

    837. What are the risks of a permissive CORS policy?

    Allowing any origin with credentials lets a malicious site read authenticated responses on a victim's behalf, effectively bypassing the same-origin policy. Specify an explicit origin allow-list, never combine wildcard origins with AllowCredentials, and restrict methods and headers. Remember that CORS is a browser control and does not protect the API from non-browser clients.

    838. What is server-side request forgery and how do you prevent it in .NET?

    SSRF tricks the server into making requests to attacker-chosen destinations, reaching internal metadata endpoints or services behind the firewall. Prevent it by allow-listing hosts and schemes, resolving and validating the IP after DNS resolution, and blocking link-local, loopback and private ranges. Follow redirects only within the allow-list and consider egress proxies for any user-supplied URL fetch.

    839. Why must XML parsers be configured securely against XXE?

    External entity expansion lets an attacker read local files, perform SSRF or cause denial of service through entity blowup. Disable DTD processing and external entities explicitly, since defaults differ across parser versions. Use XmlReaderSettings with DtdProcessing set to Prohibit and avoid XmlDocument with default settings.

    new XmlReaderSettings { DtdProcessing = DtdProcessing.Prohibit, XmlResolver = null }

    840. What makes insecure deserialization so dangerous?

    Deserializers that can instantiate arbitrary types or invoke setters during construction give an attacker control over object graphs, which can lead to remote code execution or authorization bypass. Avoid BinaryFormatter and other type-driven serializers entirely, and use System.Text.Json with explicit DTOs. If polymorphic payloads are required, use a strict type allow-list rather than the type name in the payload.

    841. How do you secure a file upload endpoint?

    Enforce size and count limits, validate content by inspecting magic bytes rather than trusting the extension or content type, and generate a new server-side filename. Store uploads outside the web root or in object storage with no execute permission, and serve them through a handler that sets a safe content type and Content-Disposition. Scan for malware asynchronously and re-encode images to strip embedded payloads.

    842. How do you implement rate limiting in ASP.NET Core?

    Use the built-in rate limiter middleware with fixed window, sliding window, token bucket or concurrency limiters per endpoint and per client identity. Partition by API key or user id rather than IP alone, because IP is shared behind NAT and spoofable in some paths. Return 429 with Retry-After and apply limits at the gateway too so direct-to-service traffic cannot bypass them.

    builder.Services.AddRateLimiter(o => o.AddFixedWindowLimiter("api", w => { w.PermitLimit = 100; w.Window = TimeSpan.FromMinutes(1); }));

    843. What should an audit log contain, and how does it differ from application logs?

    Audit logs record who did what to which resource, when, from where, and with what outcome, and they must be tamper-evident and retained per policy. Application logs are diagnostic and sampled, while audit records are complete for the audited actions and must never be dropped. Write them append-only to a separate sink and treat them as sensitive because they often contain personal data.

    844. How do you minimize PII in logs and telemetry?

    Log identifiers rather than payloads, hash or mask sensitive fields at the logging boundary, and review telemetry pipelines for accidental capture of bodies, query strings and headers. Redact authorization headers and cookies before enrichment, and never log full tokens or card data. Apply the same retention and access controls to logs as to databases, since they often escape data-protection review.

    845. How do you enforce multi-tenant data isolation in EF Core?

    Carry the tenant id in the authenticated principal, never in the request body, and apply a global query filter keyed to that value so reads cannot cross tenants. Populate TenantId on insert in SaveChanges and add a composite index and foreign keys including it. For higher assurance, use database-level row security or separate databases per tenant.

    modelBuilder.Entity<Order>().HasQueryFilter(o => o.TenantId == _tenant.TenantId);

    846. What are the weaknesses of application-level tenant filters?

    They can be bypassed with IgnoreQueryFilters, raw SQL, or code paths that do not go through the filtered context, and they silently fail open if the tenant claim is missing. Add a strict check that throws when no tenant is resolved, cover queries with tests, and supplement with database row-level security for defense in depth.

    847. How do you implement a GDPR erasure request in a distributed .NET system?
