    Maintain a data map of which services and stores hold personal data, then fan out a deletion or anonymization command that is idempotent and tracked to completion. Distinguish hard deletion from legal retention obligations, and handle backups and derived stores such as search indexes and analytics with documented timelines. Record the request, the scope and the completion evidence for accountability.

    848. How do you reconcile deletion with event-sourced or immutable logs?

    You cannot rewrite an append-only log cheaply, so use crypto-shredding where the payload is encrypted per subject and deleting the key renders it unreadable. Alternatively tombstone the subject id and keep only non-personal aggregates. Design this before launch, because retrofitting erasure into immutable storage is extremely expensive.

    849. How does the CAP theorem guide real architecture decisions in .NET systems?

    During a network partition you must choose between consistency and availability, and since partitions are inevitable the real decision is which operations sacrifice availability. Most systems are not purely CP or AP but mix per-operation choices, for example strong consistency for payments and availability for the product catalog. State the choice explicitly per data set rather than claiming the system is CA.

    850. Why is two-phase commit rarely used across microservices?

    Two-phase commit requires all participants to hold locks from prepare until commit, so a single slow or failed participant blocks everyone and can leave resources locked indefinitely. It also requires compatible transaction managers across heterogeneous stores and brokers, which is operationally fragile. Sagas with compensation achieve business consistency without distributed locking, at the cost of accepting intermediate states.

    851. What happens during a Kafka consumer group rebalance and how do you reduce its impact?

    Partitions are reassigned among consumers, and during a stop-the-world rebalance all consumption pauses, with duplicates likely for records processed but not yet committed. Cooperative sticky assignment rebalances incrementally instead of revoking everything, and static membership avoids rebalances on rolling restarts. Keep poll intervals short relative to max.poll.interval.ms and commit after processing.

    852. When should you commit Kafka offsets, and what are the consequences of each choice?

    Committing after processing gives at-least-once semantics with possible duplicates on crash, while committing before processing risks message loss. Async commits improve throughput but can reorder or drop commits on failure, so use them with idempotent processing and monotonic offset tracking. Store offsets together with derived state when you need atomic read-process-write.

    853. How do Azure Service Bus sessions provide ordered processing?

    Sessions bind messages with the same SessionId to a single consumer at a time, preserving order and enabling per-entity state on the receiver. This gives ordered, exclusive processing per session while still allowing many sessions to be processed in parallel. It is the natural fit for per-tenant or per-order workflows where global ordering is unnecessary.

    var session = await client.AcceptNextSessionAsync(queueName);

    854. How does Service Bus duplicate detection differ from application-level idempotency?

    Duplicate detection suppresses messages with the same MessageId inside a configured time window, which handles short-term producer retries. It does not protect against duplicates arriving after the window or against reprocessing after a successful send followed by a crash. Application-level deduplication in the handler remains necessary for correctness.

    855. Why is PUT considered idempotent while POST is not?

    PUT replaces the resource at a client-known URI, so repeating it produces the same state, whereas POST asks the server to create or process something and may create a new resource each time. This is a semantic contract that infrastructure and clients rely on for safe retries. If you need retryable creation with POST, use an idempotency key and return the original result.

    856. What are the trade-offs between polling an outbox table and using CDC?

    Polling is simple, portable and easy to reason about, but adds latency and database load and needs careful locking to avoid double publication. CDC such as Debezium reads the transaction log with low latency and no query load, but couples you to database-specific tooling and operational complexity. Many teams start with polling and move to CDC when latency or load becomes a constraint.

    857. How do you safely replay messages from a dead-letter queue?

    Fix the root cause first, then replay through a controlled tool that re-injects with the original payload and headers plus a replay marker, ideally into a staging queue or with a low rate limit. Ensure handlers are idempotent so partial prior success does not duplicate effects. Track replayed messages separately so a second failure does not silently loop.

    858. Compare head-based and tail-based trace sampling.

    Head-based sampling decides at the start of a trace, which is cheap and consistent across services but cannot know whether the request will fail or be slow. Tail-based sampling buffers spans and decides after seeing the whole trace, so it can retain all errors and outliers at a fraction of the volume. Tail sampling requires a collector topology that sees all spans for a trace, typically routed by trace id.

    859. What are RED and USE metrics and when do you use each?

    RED describes request-driven services through rate, errors and duration, which maps naturally to SLOs. USE describes resources through utilization, saturation and errors, which is better for diagnosing hosts, queues and connection pools. Use RED for the service view and USE for the underlying resource view so you can see both symptom and cause.

    860. Why are histograms better than averages for latency monitoring?

    Averages hide the tail, and latency distributions are skewed, so a good mean can coexist with a terrible p99. Histograms preserve the distribution and allow percentile aggregation across instances, which averages of averages cannot do. Percentiles also cannot be averaged after the fact, so record histograms rather than precomputed percentiles per instance.

    861. How do OpenTelemetry resource attributes and semantic conventions help?

    Resource attributes describe the emitting entity, such as service name, version and deployment environment, and semantic conventions standardize attribute names across libraries. This makes traces and metrics queryable and comparable across services and vendors without custom mapping. Set service.name and service.version deliberately, since they drive dashboards and deployment correlation.

    builder.Services.ConfigureOpenTelemetryMeterProvider(b =>
    b.ConfigureResource(r => r.AddService("orders-api", serviceVersion: "1.4.2")));

    862. What is the difference between health checks and synthetic monitoring?

    Health checks run inside the process and report dependency and readiness state for orchestration decisions, while synthetic monitors exercise the system from outside as a user would. Health checks can pass while real traffic fails, for example when routing or DNS is broken. Use both, and keep liveness checks shallow so they do not restart healthy pods during a dependency outage.

    863. Distinguish load, stress and soak testing.

    Load testing validates behavior at expected peak traffic against latency and error SLOs. Stress testing pushes beyond capacity to find the breaking point and confirm graceful degradation rather than collapse. Soak testing runs at sustained moderate load for hours to expose leaks, fragmentation, growing queues and connection exhaustion.

    864. How do approval or golden-file tests help protect wire contracts?

    They serialize a representative object graph and compare it against a reviewed snapshot, so any change to field names, casing, nullability or ordering appears as a diff in review. This catches accidental breaking changes that compile fine and pass behavioral tests. Keep snapshots small and commit them as reviewed artifacts.

    865. How do SAST, DAST and dependency scanning complement each other?

    SAST analyzes source for dangerous patterns early, DAST probes the running application for exploitable behavior, and dependency scanning flags vulnerable or unmaintained packages. None is sufficient alone, and all produce noise that needs triage and ownership. Add an SBOM so you can answer quickly which services ship a newly disclosed component.

    dotnet list package --vulnerable --include-transitive

    866. When would you use mTLS instead of OAuth for service-to-service authentication?

    mTLS authenticates workloads by certificate, which suits a service mesh or zero-trust network where identity is bound to the workload rather than the request. OAuth carries user or client context and fine-grained scopes that mTLS cannot express. Many systems use both: mTLS for transport-level workload identity and tokens for authorization decisions.

    867. What problem do sender-constrained tokens such as DPoP solve?

    Bearer tokens are usable by anyone who steals them, so DPoP binds the token to a client-held key by requiring a proof-of-possession JWT on each request. A stolen access token is then useless without the private key. This reduces the impact of token leakage from logs, proxies or compromised storage.

    868. What is the on-behalf-of flow and when is it required?

    An API that received a user token exchanges it for a downstream token representing the same user, so the next service sees the original identity rather than the calling service's identity. It is required when downstream authorization must reflect the end user, and it preserves scopes and consent boundaries. Without it, services either impersonate the user with excessive permissions or lose the user context entirely.

    869. Compare API keys and OAuth client credentials for partner integrations.

    API keys are simple to issue and identify a caller but carry no scopes, expiry or standard rotation story, and they are often pasted into insecure places. Client credentials give scoped, expiring tokens with auditable issuance and revocation. Prefer OAuth for anything sensitive, and if keys are required, scope and rotate them and never treat them as user identity.

    870. How do policy-based and resource-based authorization differ in ASP.NET Core?

    Policy-based authorization evaluates requirements against the user and optional resource up front, which suits coarse rules such as role plus tenant membership. Resource-based authorization calls IAuthorizationService against the loaded entity, which is necessary when the decision depends on the resource's state, such as ownership or draft status. Use resource-based checks for object-level access to avoid BOLA.

    var result = await authorizationService.AuthorizeAsync(User, order, "OrderOwner");

    871. How do you rotate application secrets with zero downtime?

    Support overlapping validity so the old and new secret both work during a transition window, deploy consumers with both configured, switch producers to the new secret, then retire the old one. Automate rotation in the vault and reload configuration without a restart where supported. Monitor authentication failures during the window so a missed consumer is detected immediately.

    872. What is envelope encryption and why is it used?

    Data is encrypted with a per-object data key, and that key is itself encrypted by a long-lived master key in a vault or HSM. This limits how much data one key protects, avoids sending bulk data to the KMS, and makes rotation cheap because only key wrappings change. It also enables crypto-shredding by destroying a single data key.

    873. Distinguish pseudonymization from anonymization.

    Pseudonymization replaces identifiers with tokens that can be reversed with additional data, so the data remains personal under GDPR but lowers risk. Anonymization must be irreversible and prevent re-identification, at which point the data falls outside personal-data rules. Hashing an email without a secret salt is pseudonymization at best, since it is reversible by enumeration.

    874. How do you implement a data subject access request efficiently?

    Build a per-service export endpoint that collects data for a subject from each store, including logs and object storage where feasible, and returns a machine-readable bundle. Verify the requester's identity before releasing anything, and apply the statutory response window from receipt. Automate discovery with a maintained data map so the request does not depend on tribal knowledge.

    875. How do you enforce data retention automatically?

    Classify data by retention category at write time, then delete or archive with scheduled jobs that are idempotent and observable. Use storage-native lifecycle policies for blobs and TTL indexes for documents, and include backups and derived copies in the policy. Track deletion counts and failures, since silent retention failures are a compliance risk.

    876. How do you prevent a noisy tenant from degrading service for others?

    Apply per-tenant quotas at the gateway and per-tenant concurrency or rate limits inside the service, and isolate expensive workloads into separate queues or worker pools. Track per-tenant latency and error metrics so skew is visible rather than hidden in aggregates. For the largest tenants, consider dedicated infrastructure or sharding by tenant tier.

    877. What is event upcasting and why is it preferable to rewriting history?

    Upcasting transforms older event versions into the current shape when they are read, so consumers stay simple while stored events remain immutable. Rewriting history requires migrating the log and risks corrupting the audit trail. Keep upcasters small, versioned and covered by tests, and add a new upcaster rather than editing an existing one.

    878. How do schema registry compatibility modes constrain event evolution?

    Modes such as backward, forward, full and transitive define which changes the registry will accept, for example adding an optional field is backward compatible but removing one is not. Enforcing a mode in CI prevents a producer from publishing a schema that breaks consumers. Choose the strictest mode your consumers can tolerate, and design changes to fit it.

    879. What is deadline propagation and why does it matter in a call chain?

    Each request carries a remaining-time budget that shrinks at every hop, so downstream calls fail fast instead of working on a request the caller has abandoned. gRPC propagates deadlines natively, while HTTP requires a header or a custom convention. Without it, timeouts multiply along the chain and resources stay busy for work nobody is waiting for.

    var deadline = DateTime.UtcNow.AddMilliseconds(remaining);

    880. How do canary releases and feature flags reduce the risk of a distributed deployment?

    A canary routes a small share of traffic to the new version and compares SLOs before a full rollout, while a feature flag decouples deployment from activation and allows instant rollback without redeploying. Together they limit blast radius and shorten time to mitigation. Instrument both by version and flag state so a regression is attributable, and remove stale flags to avoid combinatorial debt.

    881. What is the difference between .NET LTS and STS releases, and how does that affect your upgrade planning?

    LTS releases ship every two years and are supported for three years; STS releases ship in the intermediate year and are supported for eighteen months. LTS is the default target for production workloads because it reduces forced migrations, while STS is appropriate when you need newer runtime features or want to stay close to the platform's direction. A pragmatic plan keeps production on LTS and validates the next LTS on a non-critical service during the STS cycle.

    882. How would you describe the .NET release cadence introduced with .NET 5 and later?

    Microsoft ships a new major .NET version every November, alternating between LTS and STS, and patches are delivered monthly. Applications should be re-targeted at least once a year to stay inside the support window. Teams that skip multiple versions accumulate migration debt and lose security patches once the runtime goes out of support.

    883. What do collection expressions add to C# 12, and where do they fall short?

    Collection expressions provide a unified, target-typed syntax using square brackets for arrays, spans, and any type with a collection builder. Spread elements let you inline an existing sequence, which removes a lot of boilerplate concatenation code. They do not replace LINQ and can allocate when the target type has no efficient builder, so hot paths still need measurement.

    int[] a = [1, 2, 3];
List<string> names = ["ana", "bo"];
Span<int> all = [..a, 4, 5];

    884. How do primary constructors for classes differ from records, and what is the common misuse?

    Primary constructor parameters on a class are captured only if used, and they do not become public properties automatically, unlike positional records. The common misuse is capturing a mutable dependency in many members so the compiler generates a hidden field that outlives the intended scope. Prefer explicit fields or assignment when the value is used across the type.

    885. What problem do required members solve, and what are their runtime limitations?

    The required modifier forces object initializers to set a property, which makes immutable-style initialization safe without constructor overloads. Enforcement is compile-time only, so reflection, serializers, and Activator.CreateInstance can bypass it unless the type is annotated or validated. Types with required members also need SetsRequiredMembers on constructors that set them to avoid false errors.

    public sealed class Options
{
    public required string Endpoint { get; init; }
    public int Retries { get; init; } = 3;
}

    886. When are raw string literals preferable to verbatim strings?

    Raw string literals start and end with three or more quotes, need no escaping for quotes, backslashes, or braces, and strip leading indentation based on the closing delimiter. They are the cleanest option for JSON, SQL, and regex payloads. Verbatim strings are still fine for simple paths but cannot express embedded quotes without doubling.

    var json = """
    { "id": 1, "path": "C:\\temp" }
    """;

    887. What does the alias-any-type feature enable in C# 12?

    Using aliases can now target tuples, arrays, pointers, and other non-named types, and generic aliases can be defined with the using directive. This lets a team give semantic names to structural types, which improves readability in domain-heavy code. Aliases are file or global scoped and disappear at compile time, so they do not affect the public API surface.

    using Point = (int X, int Y);
using StringMap = System.Collections.Generic.Dictionary<string, string>;

    888. How do default lambda parameters change the way you write small handler code?

    Lambda expressions can declare default parameter values, so a delegate can be invoked with fewer arguments. This is convenient for expression-tree-free code such as local handlers or minimal API filters, but the default is baked into the delegate signature rather than being optional metadata. Callers must still use the delegate type, not reflection, to get the default behaviour.

    var greet = (string name = "guest") => $"hi {name}";

    889. When is keyed dependency injection the right choice, and what are its pitfalls?

    Keyed services let you register multiple implementations of one interface and resolve them by key, which removes factory switches for things like multiple payment providers. The pitfall is hiding strategy selection inside the container, which makes dependencies implicit and harder to test. Prefer keys at composition boundaries and keep the selection logic explicit where the key is known.

    builder.Services.AddKeyedSingleton<IPayment, StripePayment>("stripe");
app.MapPost("/pay", ([FromKeyedServices("stripe")] IPayment p) => p.Charge());

    890. Why should production code depend on TimeProvider instead of DateTime.UtcNow?

    TimeProvider abstracts the clock and timers, so tests can use FakeTimeProvider and advance time deterministically instead of sleeping. It also centralizes time zone and timestamp semantics, which matters for auditing and scheduling logic. The main discipline is injecting it once and never mixing it with static time calls in the same component.

    public sealed class Expiry(TimeProvider clock)
{
    public bool IsExpired(DateTimeOffset at) => at < clock.GetUtcNow();
}

    891. What do frozen collections give you compared with a regular dictionary?

    Frozen collections are optimized for read-heavy, build-once workloads: creation is more expensive, but lookups are faster and thread-safe with lower memory overhead. They suit static lookup tables such as status codes or allow-lists created at startup. Do not use them for data that changes, since every mutation means rebuilding the frozen instance.

    private static readonly FrozenDictionary<int, string> Codes =
    new Dictionary<int, string> { [200] = "OK", [404] = "NotFound" }.ToFrozenDictionary();

    892. How does SearchValues improve high-throughput string scanning?

    SearchValues precomputes an optimized matcher for a fixed set of characters or strings, allowing the runtime to use vectorized paths in IndexOfAny and similar APIs. It is created once and cached, which avoids rebuilding per-call search state. The win is largest on hot parsing loops such as tokenizers or log scrubbers.

    private static readonly SearchValues<char> Separators = SearchValues.Create(",;|");
int i = span.IndexOfAny(Separators);

    893. What are the trade-offs of using System.Text.Json source generation?

    Source generation removes reflection at runtime, which improves startup, reduces allocations, and is required for Native AOT trimming scenarios. The cost is extra generated code, slower builds, and constraints on polymorphic or dynamic shapes that the generator cannot model. It is usually worth enabling for DTO-heavy services and mandatory for AOT-published APIs.

    [JsonSerializable(typeof(OrderDto))]
internal partial class ApiJsonContext : JsonSerializerContext { }

    894. What do you lose when you enable Native AOT for an ASP.NET Core service?

    Native AOT gives fast startup, small memory footprint, and no JIT, which is ideal for scale-to-zero and CLI tools. In exchange you lose dynamic assembly loading, most reflection-based serialization, runtime code generation, and some libraries that depend on them. Profiling and debugging are also less familiar, so AOT is a deliberate architectural choice rather than a checkbox.

    895. How do trimming and AOT differ in risk profile?

    Trimming removes unreachable IL but still runs on the JIT, so failures appear as missing members at runtime. AOT goes further and compiles ahead of time, so unsupported patterns fail at publish time or require annotations. Trimming is easier to adopt incrementally, while AOT demands a dependency audit up front.

    896. Which minimal API improvements matter most for a production service?

    Route groups, typed results, endpoint filters, and validation integration remove most reasons to fall back to controllers for small services. TypedResults gives compile-time checking and better OpenAPI metadata than returning IResult. Groups also let you apply authentication, rate limiting, and conventions once instead of per endpoint.

    var api = app.MapGroup("/orders").RequireAuthorization();
api.MapGet("/{id:int}", (int id, IOrderService svc) =>
    svc.Find(id) is { } o ? TypedResults.Ok(o) : Results.NotFound());

    897. How do Blazor render modes change the way you design a page?

    Render modes decide where a component executes: static SSR on the server, interactive server over a circuit, interactive WebAssembly in the browser, or auto which starts on the server and switches to WASM. Choosing per component means you must reason about state ownership, latency, and whether the component can run in the browser. Data access belongs behind an API when a component may become interactive WebAssembly.

    898. When is static SSR the better Blazor choice than interactive server mode?

    Static SSR renders HTML per request with no persistent circuit, so it scales cheaply and behaves like classic server-rendered pages. It suits content, forms with progressive enhancement, and public pages where interactivity is limited. Interactive server is better when you need rich client state and can afford long-lived connections.

    899. What does HybridCache add over IMemoryCache plus IDistributedCache?

    HybridCache combines a local in-memory layer with a distributed layer and handles stampede protection, serialization, and tag-based invalidation in one API. It removes the custom two-level cache code most teams write and get subtly wrong. The key caveat is that local entries can be stale across instances until invalidation propagates, so choose expiration values with that in mind.

    var value = await cache.GetOrCreateAsync($"order:{id}",
    async ct => await repo.LoadAsync(id, ct),
    options: new() { Expiration = TimeSpan.FromMinutes(5) });

    900. Which OpenTelemetry signals does .NET emit out of the box, and what still needs work?

    The runtime and ASP.NET Core emit metrics for requests, GC, thread pool, and Kestrel, and HttpClient and EF Core provide traces and metrics with instrumentation packages. What still needs deliberate effort is business-level telemetry, correlation across async boundaries you own, and consistent resource attributes. Cardinality control is the usual production failure point.

    901. How would you plan an upgrade from .NET Framework 4.8 to modern .NET?

    Start with a dependency and API inventory: unsupported libraries, WCF, AppDomain, remoting, and Windows-only assumptions drive the estimate. Then choose a path, either a side-by-side rewrite of the web layer behind a proxy or an incremental migration using the compatibility shim for libraries. Running the test suite and load tests on the new runtime early exposes behavioural differences before the cutover.

    902. What is your strategy for upgrading a large solution across several .NET versions?

    Upgrade in dependency order, starting with leaf libraries and shared packages, and pin the SDK with a global.json so builds are reproducible. Use analyzers and the upgrade assistant to surface API changes, then run the full test suite per project rather than per solution. Keep the change mechanical and separate from feature work so reviews stay reviewable.

    { "sdk": { "version": "9.0.100", "rollForward": "latestFeature" } }

    903. How do you decide whether a new language feature is safe to adopt in a shared codebase?

    Adopt features that reduce ambiguity or boilerplate and that every target framework in the repo can compile, since multi-targeting constrains syntax. Check tooling support, including analyzers, formatters, and the debugging experience in production. Introduce one feature at a time and document the convention so the codebase does not become a mix of styles.

    904. What does a good multi-stage Dockerfile for a .NET service look like?

    Build and publish in an SDK stage, then copy only the published output into a runtime stage so the final image has no compilers or source. Use cache-friendly layer ordering by restoring against the project file before copying sources. Set a non-root user and explicit entrypoint in the final stage.

    FROM mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/sdk:9.0 AS build
WORKDIR /src
COPY *.csproj ./
RUN dotnet restore
COPY . ./
RUN dotnet publish -c Release -o /app

FROM mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/aspnet:9.0-noble-chiseled
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=build /app ./
USER $APP_UID
ENTRYPOINT ["dotnet", "Api.dll"]

    905. What are chiseled and distroless images, and why do they matter for security?

    Chiseled images contain only the runtime, a minimal set of native libraries, and no shell or package manager, which shrinks the attack surface and image size. Distroless follows the same idea outside the Microsoft-published set. The trade-off is no shell for debugging, so you rely on exec into the app process, sidecar diagnostics, or an ephemeral debug container.

    906. How do you reduce container image size and startup time for a .NET API?

    Publish in Release with trimming or ReadyToRun where it helps, use the ASP.NET chiseled runtime image, and avoid copying test or source artifacts. Enabling composite or partial compilation of Razor views and precompiling serializers removes first-request work. Measure with a cold start on the target platform, since registry pull time often dominates the real deployment latency.

    907. Which Kubernetes probes should a .NET service expose, and how do they differ?

    Liveness answers whether the process should be restarted, readiness whether it should receive traffic, and startup whether the app has finished booting. Liveness must not check dependencies, or a database blip will restart every pod. Use the health checks package with separate tags and map them to distinct endpoints.

    builder.Services.AddHealthChecks()
    .AddCheck("self", () => HealthCheckResult.Healthy(), tags: ["live"])
    .AddDbContextCheck<AppDb>(tags: ["ready"]);
app.MapHealthChecks("/health/live", new() { Predicate = r => r.Tags.Contains("live") });
app.MapHealthChecks("/health/ready", new() { Predicate = r => r.Tags.Contains("ready") });

    908. How do you set resource requests and limits for a .NET pod without guessing?

    Load test the service in a realistic environment and observe steady-state working set and CPU, then set requests near the observed baseline and limits with headroom. Memory limits below the real working set cause OOM kills, and .NET responds to container limits through the GC configuration. Revisit the numbers whenever the workload or runtime version changes.

    909. When should configuration come from a ConfigMap and when from a Secret?

    ConfigMaps hold non-sensitive settings such as feature toggles and endpoint URLs; Secrets hold credentials and keys, ideally sourced from an external secret store. Both should be mounted or projected rather than baked into images, so the same image runs in every environment. Prefer the configuration provider pattern with reload support so changes do not require a rebuild.

    910. How does graceful shutdown work in ASP.NET Core on Kubernetes?

    On SIGTERM, the host stops accepting new connections and waits for in-flight requests up to the shutdown timeout, while readiness should start failing so the endpoint is removed from service. The pod's termination grace period must exceed the application's shutdown timeout, otherwise requests are killed mid-flight. Long-running background work needs its own cancellation handling through IHostedService.StopAsync.

    builder.Services.Configure<HostOptions>(o =>
    o.ShutdownTimeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(25));

    911. Why is a health endpoint that checks every dependency a production risk?

    A readiness probe that fails when a non-critical dependency is slow will remove all instances at once, turning a partial degradation into an outage. Health checks should reflect the service's ability to serve traffic, not the health of the whole system. Separate critical dependencies from informational checks and alert on the latter instead of failing readiness.

    912. How do you mitigate cold starts in Azure Functions?

    Use the isolated worker model with the in-process alternatives, keep the deployment package small, avoid heavy static initialization, and prefer Premium or dedicated plans with pre-warmed instances for latency-sensitive paths. For HTTP APIs behind strict latency targets, a minimum instance count removes the problem at a cost. Measure the real cold start distribution rather than the best case.

    913. How do you choose between Azure App Service, Container Apps, and AKS for a .NET workload?

    App Service is the lowest-operations option for a single web app and handles scaling, slots, and certificates well. Container Apps adds container portability, revisions, and scale-to-zero with KEDA-style triggers without exposing Kubernetes. AKS is justified when you need the Kubernetes ecosystem, custom operators, or fine-grained networking and scheduling control, and you accept the platform ownership cost.

    914. How does managed identity change the way you handle secrets in Azure?

    A system-assigned or user-assigned identity lets the workload obtain tokens from Microsoft Entra ID without storing credentials, and DefaultAzureCredential picks it up locally and in the cloud. The remaining configuration is only the resource endpoint and the role assignment. This removes connection-string secrets from configuration and rotation from your runbooks.

    var client = new SecretClient(new Uri(vaultUri), new DefaultAzureCredential());
var secret = await client.GetSecretAsync("db-password");

    915. What is the difference between build-once-deploy-many and rebuilding per environment?

    Build once produces a single immutable artifact promoted through environments, so what you tested is exactly what runs in production. Rebuilding per environment reintroduces risk from dependency drift and makes provenance hard to prove. Environment differences belong in configuration and secrets, not in the build.

    916. How do you version container images and build artifacts reliably?

    Tag images with the commit SHA as the immutable identifier and add a human-readable semver or build number as a moving convenience tag. Never deploy the latest tag to production, since it cannot be traced back to a source revision. Also record the artifact digest in the deployment record for auditability.

    917. What does a solid GitHub Actions pipeline for a .NET service include?

    Separate build, test, and deploy jobs with dependency caching, a build once into a container or artifact, and environment-scoped approvals for production. Use OIDC federation to the cloud instead of stored credentials, and fail the build on vulnerable packages and analyzers. Keep the workflow itself reviewed and versioned like code.

    - uses: actions/setup-dotnet@v4
  with: { dotnet-version: '9.0.x' }
- run: dotnet test --configuration Release --logger trx

    918. How do Azure DevOps pipelines differ from GitHub Actions in ways that matter for .NET teams?

    Azure DevOps offers environments with approvals, checks, and gates that suit regulated release processes, plus tighter integration with Boards and test plans. GitHub Actions has a larger marketplace and simpler YAML reuse through composite actions. The decision is usually organizational rather than technical, so standardize on one to avoid dual maintenance.

    919. How do feature flags support progressive delivery without redeploying?

    Flags separate deployment from release, so code can ship dark and be enabled for a cohort later. Use a flag service with targeting rules and an audit trail, and keep flag evaluation cheap and cached. Every temporary flag needs an owner and an expiry, otherwise the codebase fills with dead branches.

    920. How does a canary deployment differ from blue-green?

    Blue-green runs two complete environments and switches traffic in one step, giving instant rollback but doubling infrastructure. Canary sends a small percentage of traffic to the new version and increases it while watching error rates and latency. Canary is better for uncertain behaviour changes, blue-green for fast atomic cutovers with cheap rollback.

    921. How should database migrations be executed in a CI/CD pipeline?

    Migrations should run as an explicit, idempotent step before the new application version takes traffic, with a lock so concurrent instances do not race. They must be backward compatible: add nullable columns and new tables first, backfill, then remove old structures in a later release. Never let application startup apply migrations implicitly in a multi-instance deployment.

    dotnet ef migrations bundle -o migrate -r linux-x64
./migrate --connection "$CONN"

    922. What makes a database change safe for zero-downtime deployment?

    The expand-migrate-contract pattern keeps old and new code working simultaneously: expand the schema, migrate data in batches, then contract once no instance uses the old shape. Avoid long locks on large tables, and add indexes concurrently where the engine supports it. Every change should be reversible or have a documented forward-fix plan.

    923. What belongs in infrastructure as code, and what does not?

    Networking, compute, identity, and data services should be declared in code with remote state, review, and drift detection. Application configuration that changes frequently and secrets belong in a configuration store referenced by the IaC, not hard-coded. Environment-specific values should be parameterized rather than forked into separate templates.

    924. What does environment parity actually require, and where do teams over-invest?

    Parity means the same runtime version, container image, and deployment mechanism across environments, with differences limited to scale, data, and secrets. It does not require identical data volumes or identical instance counts, which are expensive and rarely the source of failures. Most parity bugs come from differing configuration and dependency versions, not from size.

    925. What makes a production dashboard useful rather than decorative?

    A good dashboard answers a small set of questions: are users succeeding, is latency within SLO, what changed recently. Put SLO burn rate and error budget at the top, then dependency health, then saturation signals. Every panel should link to a runbook or query so an on-call engineer can act without guessing.

    926. What should a runbook contain for a .NET service?

    It should list symptoms, the queries and dashboards to confirm them, the immediate mitigation steps such as scaling out or rolling back, and the escalation path. Include known failure modes with their signatures and the commands or links to execute them. Runbooks decay, so assign ownership and review them after every incident.

    927. How do you approach cost awareness in a cloud-hosted .NET estate?

    Right-size based on measured utilization, use autoscaling with sensible minimums, and shut down non-production outside working hours. Tag resources by owner and environment so cost can be attributed, and review the largest line items monthly rather than chasing small ones. Watch for idle premium tiers, over-provisioned databases, and log retention that nobody reads.

    928. Why is autoscaling often ineffective without the right metrics?

    Scaling on CPU alone misses I/O-bound or queue-driven workloads, so the service stays slow while CPU looks idle. Choose signals that correlate with load, such as request concurrency, queue depth, or custom business metrics. Also verify that scale-out latency is shorter than the traffic spike, otherwise the autoscaler always reacts too late.

    929. How does a layered architecture differ from clean or hexagonal architecture?

    Layering organizes code by technical concern, and dependencies typically flow downward with the data layer often leaking into business logic. Clean and hexagonal put the domain at the center and make infrastructure depend on abstractions owned by the domain. The practical difference is dependency direction and testability, not the number of folders.

    930. What problem does the vertical slice architecture solve?

    Vertical slices organize code by feature so a request's endpoint, handler, validation, and persistence live together, which reduces cross-cutting churn. It trades reuse for locality and makes features easy to delete or extract later. Shared infrastructure still belongs in a common layer, but domain concepts stay inside the slice.

    931. When is a modular monolith a better choice than microservices?

    A modular monolith gives strong module boundaries inside one deployable, avoiding distributed transactions, network failures, and operational overhead while the domain is still being understood. It suits teams without platform capacity or with unclear service boundaries. Because modules communicate in process, you can extract a service later once the boundary is proven.

    932. What are the real costs of microservices that teams underestimate?

    Distributed debugging, eventual consistency, contract versioning, and per-service operational tooling usually cost more than the code. Each service multiplies deployment pipelines, dashboards, on-call surface, and data ownership decisions. Microservices pay off only when independent scaling and autonomous team delivery genuinely matter.

    933. What are the trade-offs of CQRS, and when does it hurt?

    CQRS separates write models from read models, allowing each to be optimized and scaled independently. It hurts when the domain is simple, because you duplicate models and introduce synchronization and eventual consistency for no benefit. Apply it to specific bounded contexts with asymmetric read and write loads, not as a global default.

    934. When is event sourcing justified over state-stored persistence?

    Event sourcing is justified when you need a complete audit trail, temporal queries, or the ability to rebuild projections for new read models. The costs are event schema evolution, snapshotting, replay time, and a steeper learning curve for the team. Most systems need an audit log, not an event-sourced domain model.

    935. Which DDD tactical patterns do you actually use in a .NET codebase?

    Aggregates with invariants enforced in the root, value objects for concepts without identity, and domain events for cross-aggregate reactions cover most needs. Repositories are useful at aggregate boundaries but often unnecessary over EF Core's DbContext. Factories and specifications are worth adding only when the complexity they remove is real.

    936. What is an anti-corruption layer and where does it sit?

    It is a translation boundary that protects your domain model from a foreign model, such as a legacy system or third-party API. It maps external contracts and semantics into your own types and isolates change. It usually sits in infrastructure, exposing a domain-owned interface to the application layer.

    937. How does the strangler fig pattern work for migrating a monolith?

    You put a facade or router in front of the monolith and route selected capabilities to new services, growing the new system until the old one can be retired. Each step is small, reversible, and shippable, which keeps risk low. The facade is temporary and must be designed to disappear, not to become a permanent integration hub.

    938. Why use the transactional outbox pattern with EF Core?

    The outbox writes domain changes and outgoing messages in the same database transaction, then a background publisher forwards them, so you never lose a message after a commit. It converts dual-write inconsistency into at-least-once delivery, which consumers must handle idempotently. A relay or CDC process and a cleanup job are required pieces, not optional extras.

    await using var tx = await db.Database.BeginTransactionAsync(ct);
db.Orders.Add(order);
db.Outbox.Add(new OutboxMessage(order.Id, OrderCreated));
await db.SaveChangesAsync(ct);
await tx.CommitAsync(ct);

    939. What are the failure modes of a transactional outbox implementation?

    Duplicate delivery is guaranteed at-least-once, so consumers need deduplication keys. Ordering can break if the relay publishes in parallel, and a backlog grows silently if the publisher stops. Monitor outbox lag and dead-letter poison messages, and cap retries with an alert.

    940. When does an API gateway or BFF make sense?

    A BFF gives each client type a tailored aggregation layer, avoiding chatty calls and over-fetching from mobile or web clients. An API gateway centralizes cross-cutting concerns such as authentication, rate limiting, and routing. Both risk becoming a shared bottleneck where every team's changes collide, so keep ownership clear.

    941. How do you handle partial failures in API composition?

    Decide per call whether the response is degraded-but-usable or must fail, and apply timeouts, circuit breakers, and fallbacks accordingly. Return partial results with explicit metadata rather than silently omitting data. Cache the expensive composed pieces so a single slow dependency does not take down the whole response.

    942. What does caching at the edge change about your architecture?

    Edge caching removes load and latency for cacheable content, but it makes invalidation the hard problem and complicates per-user responses. You need explicit cache keys, correct Vary headers, and short TTLs for anything personalized. It also means origin outages become partially invisible, so observability must distinguish edge hits from origin errors.

    943. What is different about designing for multi-region active-active?

    Active-active requires conflict-free data models or a single writer per partition, because latency makes synchronous cross-region consistency impractical. Routing needs to consider data residency and failover semantics, not just proximity. Many teams are better served by active-passive with a tested failover than by true active-active.

    944. How do data residency requirements constrain your cloud design?

    Data must stay in permitted geographies, which affects region selection, replication, backup storage, and even support access. It can force per-region data stores and careful handling of caches, logs, and telemetry that may carry personal data. Document the data flow per component, since incidental copies are the usual compliance gap.

    945. How do RPO and RTO drive your disaster recovery design?

    RPO defines acceptable data loss and drives backup frequency and replication mode; RTO defines acceptable downtime and drives standby capacity and automation. A warm standby with automated failover costs more but meets tight targets, while backup-and-restore is cheap and slow. Whatever the design, the only proof is a rehearsed failover with measured timings.

    946. How do you keep technical debt visible and managed rather than rhetorical?

    Record debt items with impact, effort, and the risk of deferral, then allocate a fixed capacity share each iteration. Tie items to incidents or delivery friction so prioritization is evidence-based. Track trends such as change failure rate and lead time to show whether the debt work is paying off.

    947. What does an effective code review focus on in a .NET team?

    Review for correctness, boundary and contract design, error handling, security, and test coverage of the change's intent. Style and formatting belong to automated tooling so human attention goes to design. Keep changes small and review within a day, since large late reviews produce shallow feedback.

    948. What makes an architecture decision record useful years later?

    An ADR captures the context, the decision, the alternatives considered, and the consequences, including what you are now constrained from doing. It should be short, dated, immutable, and superseded rather than edited when things change. The value comes from recording why, since the what is visible in the code.

    949. How do you incrementally extract a service from a monolith without a big-bang rewrite?

    Start with a capability that has clear boundaries and low coupling, put a router in front, and move reads before writes to reduce risk. Keep the data ownership transition explicit, often with dual writes or a change data capture bridge during the transition. Ship each slice to production and measure before starting the next.

    950. Which .NET anti-patterns do you most often find in code review?

    Captive dependencies from resolving scoped services in singletons, async over sync with blocking calls, and service locator usage are the classics. Others include anemic domain models with logic in controllers, catch-all exception handling, and static mutable state. Most are detectable with analyzers, DI validation on build, and a strict code review checklist.

    951. How do you detect a captive dependency in ASP.NET Core before production?

    Enable scope validation in the development environment and build the service provider at startup with ValidateOnBuild and ValidateScopes. Then resolve each root service once so the container walks the graph. Integration tests that boot the real host catch most of these earlier than production traffic.

    builder.Host.UseDefaultServiceProvider(o =>
{
    o.ValidateScopes = true;
    o.ValidateOnBuild = true;
});

    952. Why is async void dangerous outside event handlers?

    An async void method cannot be awaited, so exceptions escape to the synchronization context and can crash the process. Callers also cannot observe completion or apply backpressure. Use async Task everywhere except framework event handlers, and wrap those bodies in try/catch.

    953. What is the practical difference between IHttpClientFactory and a singleton HttpClient?

    A singleton HttpClient does not respect DNS changes and can accumulate stale connections, while creating one per call exhausts sockets. IHttpClientFactory pools handlers, rotates them, and integrates with Polly-style resilience pipelines. Use a named or typed client and configure timeouts and resilience at registration.

    954. How do you add resilience to outgoing HTTP calls in modern .NET?

    Use the Microsoft.Extensions.Http.Resilience package to add retries with jitter, circuit breaking, timeouts, and hedging through a standard pipeline. Retries must be limited to idempotent operations and combined with a total timeout so they cannot amplify an outage. Emit telemetry for each strategy so you can see when the breaker opens.

    builder.Services.AddHttpClient<CatalogClient>()
    .AddStandardResilienceHandler();

    955. How do you prevent a retry storm during a dependency outage?

    Combine a circuit breaker with bounded retries, exponential backoff, and jitter, and cap the total time budget per request. Queue-based work should back off at the consumer level rather than retrying in a tight loop. Alert on breaker state transitions so the team sees the outage before users do.

    956. What does idempotency require at the API level for POST operations?

    The client supplies an idempotency key, and the server stores the key with the resulting response so retries return the original outcome instead of creating duplicates. The key store needs a TTL and must be written in the same transaction as the business change. Without this, at-least-once messaging and client retries will eventually duplicate work.

    957. How do you choose between synchronous and asynchronous service-to-service communication?

    Synchronous calls are simpler to reason about and give immediate feedback, but they couple availability and latency across services. Asynchronous messaging decouples them at the cost of eventual consistency, ordering, and idempotency work. Use synchronous for queries the user is waiting on and asynchronous for state changes that can complete later.

    958. What is the difference between choreography and orchestration in a distributed workflow?

    Choreography lets services react to events with no central coordinator, which is loosely coupled but hard to trace end to end. Orchestration uses a saga or workflow engine that explicitly drives steps and compensations. Complex multi-step business processes with strict timeouts usually benefit from orchestration, while simple fan-out reactions fit choreography.

    959. How do you implement a saga and its compensations in .NET?

    Model each step with a forward action and a compensating action, persist saga state, and make every step idempotent so retries are safe. Use a durable store or workflow engine so a process restart does not lose progress. Compensation is business logic, not a rollback, so it must be designed with domain experts.

    960. What belongs in a domain event versus an integration event?

    Domain events are in-process facts raised inside a bounded context, while integration events cross process boundaries and form a public contract. Integration events need versioning, stable schemas, and a publish mechanism such as an outbox. Do not publish domain types directly, since internal refactoring would break consumers.

    961. How do you version a public HTTP API without breaking clients?

    Prefer additive change: new optional fields, new endpoints, and tolerant readers that ignore unknown members. When a breaking change is unavoidable, use explicit versioning in the path or header and run both versions concurrently with a deprecation timeline. Track client usage per version so you know when it is safe to retire one.

    962. How do you design a message schema that can evolve safely?

    Treat the schema as a contract: add optional fields, never rename or repurpose existing ones, and keep consumers tolerant of unknown properties. Use a schema registry with compatibility checks in CI when the broker supports it. Include a version and correlation identifiers in the envelope rather than the payload.

    963. What are poison messages and how do you handle them?

    A poison message fails repeatedly and blocks progress if retried forever. Use a bounded retry count with dead-lettering, then inspect and replay from the dead-letter queue after a fix. Alert on dead-letter depth, since silent accumulation is usually the first sign of a contract mismatch.

    964. How do you achieve exactly-once effects on top of at-least-once delivery?

    You cannot get exactly-once delivery end to end, so make handlers idempotent using a deduplication store keyed by message id or a natural business key. Combine that with an inbox table written in the same transaction as the business change. The result is effectively-once processing, which is what the requirement actually means.

    965. How do you handle distributed transactions now that MSDTC is rarely an option?

    Replace them with sagas, outbox patterns, and idempotent compensating actions, accepting eventual consistency. Where strong consistency is essential, keep the data inside one service and one database boundary. Designing boundaries so transactions stay local is cheaper than making distributed transactions reliable.

    966. What is your approach to connection pooling and DbContext lifetime in a web API?

    Register DbContext as scoped so each request gets a clean unit of work, and let the provider's connection pool handle physical connections. Avoid keeping a DbContext alive across background work or parallel tasks, since it is not thread-safe. For high-throughput read paths, consider compiled queries or Dapper rather than fighting the change tracker.

    967. How do you diagnose high memory usage in a .NET service in production?

    Start with runtime metrics and GC counters to distinguish a leak from expected generation growth, then capture a dump or use dotnet-counters and dotnet-gcdump on a suspect instance. Analyze retention paths to find what is holding objects alive, often event handler subscriptions, caches, or static collections. Fix the root cause rather than raising the container limit.

    968. Which signals tell you a service needs to be refactored rather than tuned?

    Rising change failure rate, tests that break for unrelated reasons, and features that require touching many modules point to structural problems. Performance tuning rarely fixes growing coordination cost. Use lead time and defect clustering per module as evidence when proposing refactoring work.

    969. How do you structure configuration for multiple environments in ASP.NET Core?

    Layer configuration sources so the environment-specific provider overrides appsettings defaults, and bind strongly typed options with validation at startup. Fail fast on invalid configuration rather than discovering it at first request. Keep secrets out of source and inject them through the environment or a secret store.

    builder.Services.AddOptions<PaymentsOptions>()
    .Bind(builder.Configuration.GetSection("Payments"))
    .ValidateDataAnnotations()
    .ValidateOnStart();

    970. Why is validating options at startup better than lazy validation?

    ValidateOnStart turns a misconfiguration into a failed deployment instead of a runtime error under specific traffic. It also makes the failure visible in the orchestrator's startup logs and health checks. Lazy validation is only reasonable for optional features that are not always exercised.

    971. How do you keep a background service from blocking application shutdown?

    Implement BackgroundService with a proper cancellation token loop and respect the stopping token in all awaited calls. Long-running work should checkpoint so it can resume rather than restart from scratch. Never swallow the cancellation exception or the host will hang until the grace period expires.

    protected override async Task ExecuteAsync(CancellationToken ct)
{
    while (!ct.IsCancellationRequested)
    {
        await _worker.RunOnceAsync(ct);
        await Task.Delay(_interval, ct);
    }
}

    972. When should work move out of the request pipeline into a queue?

    Move work out when it is slow, retryable, or not needed for the response, such as emails, document generation, or third-party sync. This shortens response times and isolates failures. The cost is a queue to operate, plus the need for idempotency and status reporting to the user.

    973. How do you implement rate limiting fairly across tenants?

    Use the built-in rate limiter with partition keys derived from tenant identity rather than IP, and choose a policy such as sliding window or token bucket per tier. Return 429 with Retry-After so clients can back off correctly. Distributed enforcement needs a shared store, since per-instance limits multiply with scale.

    builder.Services.AddRateLimiter(o => o.AddPolicy("tenant", ctx =>
    RateLimitPartition.GetTokenBucketLimiter(ctx.User.FindFirst("tid")!.Value, _ => new()
    {
        TokenLimit = 100, ReplenishmentPeriod = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(1), TokensPerPeriod = 10
    })));

    974. What is the correct way to store passwords and other secrets in a .NET application?

    Passwords are hashed with a slow, salted algorithm such as Argon2id or PBKDF2 with a current work factor, never encrypted reversibly. Service credentials belong in a managed secret store accessed through managed identity. Application code should never contain keys, and configuration files should not be the source of truth for production secrets.

    975. How do you approach authentication and authorization for internal service calls?

    Use OAuth 2 client credentials or workload identity so each service has its own identity and token audience. Authorize on scopes or application roles rather than network location, since network trust fails silently. Token caching and clock skew handling are small details that cause large outages if ignored.

    976. What is the difference between authentication middleware order mistakes and authorization bugs?

    Middleware order determines whether identity is established before authorization or endpoint execution, and getting it wrong yields 401s or anonymous access. UseRouting must precede UseAuthorization, and authentication must precede both. Add a startup test that asserts a protected endpoint returns 401 without a token.

    977. How do you test a service that depends on time, randomness, and external HTTP?

    Inject TimeProvider for time, an abstraction for randomness or ID generation, and a stubbed HttpMessageHandler or WireMock for HTTP. This keeps tests deterministic and fast without network access. Reserve full integration tests with real infrastructure for a small number of critical paths.

    978. What is the difference between a unit test and a component test in a .NET solution?

    A unit test exercises a small piece in isolation with collaborators replaced, while a component test boots the real host with its real DI graph and often a real database. Component tests catch configuration and wiring errors that unit tests never see. Both are needed, but the pyramid should still be bottom-heavy.

    979. How do you test EF Core queries meaningfully?

    Test against the real provider, since the in-memory provider does not enforce relational semantics such as constraints, translations, or cascades. Use a disposable database, ideally in a container, and reset state between tests. Assert on the resulting data rather than on generated SQL, except where a performance regression matters.

    980. How do you keep integration tests fast in a busy CI pipeline?

    Share a containerized database across the test run with per-test schema isolation or transactions, and run independent collections in parallel. Cache images and package restores between runs. Split slow end-to-end tests into a separate stage so fast feedback stays under a few minutes.

    981. How do you introduce contract testing between a producer and its consumers?

    Consumers publish expectations as contracts, and the producer's build verifies them so breaking changes fail before deployment. This replaces a large end-to-end environment with fast, targeted checks. Start with the highest-traffic consumer paths and keep contracts versioned alongside the code.

    982. What is your approach to load testing before a major release?

    983. How do you decide the right caching layer for a given read path?

    Start with the consistency requirement and the tolerance for staleness, then choose memory, distributed cache, HTTP caching, or a CDN accordingly. Every cache needs an invalidation story and a bound on size. Measure the hit rate before adding complexity, since the fastest cache is often a better query.

    984. What are the risks of caching per-request data in a singleton service?

    It leaks data across users and grows without bound, and it often hides a scoping bug that will surface as a security incident. HttpContext and scoped services must never be captured in singletons. Use IMemoryCache with explicit keys that include the tenant or user, plus size limits and expiration.

    985. How do you design pagination for a large, frequently changing dataset?

    Prefer keyset pagination on an indexed, stable sort key, since offset pagination degrades and duplicates rows when data shifts. Return an opaque cursor so the ordering can evolve without breaking clients. Cap page sizes and document the ordering guarantee.

    var page = await db.Orders
    .Where(o => o.Id > cursor)
    .OrderBy(o => o.Id)
    .Take(50)
    .ToListAsync();

    986. How do you handle large file uploads and downloads in an API?

    Stream directly to blob storage using pre-signed URLs or streaming endpoints rather than buffering in memory, and enforce size limits and content validation. Serve downloads from the storage or CDN edge to keep the app tier out of the data path. Add virus scanning and authorization checks as asynchronous steps.

    987. What does a well-designed error response look like?

    Use consistent problem details with a machine-readable code, a human-readable message, and a correlation id for support. Never leak stack traces or internal identifiers. Distinguish client errors from server errors so clients can decide whether to retry.

    return TypedResults.Problem(
    statusCode: 409, title: "Duplicate order",
    extensions: new Dictionary<string, object?> { ["code"] = "ORDER_EXISTS" });

    988. How do you correlate logs across services without leaking sensitive data?

    Propagate a trace context through W3C headers and include trace and span ids in structured logs. Log identifiers rather than payloads, and scrub personal data at the sink. Set sampling so volume stays manageable while errors are always retained.

    989. Why is structured logging preferable to string interpolation in production?

    Structured logging keeps properties queryable, so you can filter by tenant, order id, or duration instead of regex-matching text. It also lets sinks redact fields consistently. Message templates avoid the allocation cost of building strings that may never be written.

    logger.LogInformation("Order {OrderId} shipped in {ElapsedMs} ms", orderId, elapsed);

    990. How do you keep log volume and cost under control?

    Set levels per namespace, sample high-frequency informational logs, and drop health check noise at the sink. Retain debug logs briefly and errors longer, based on actual investigation needs. Review the top log producers monthly, since one verbose component often dominates the bill.

    991. What is the role of SLOs and error budgets in delivery decisions?

    An SLO sets a measurable reliability target, and the error budget quantifies allowed failure within a period. When the budget is exhausted, feature work pauses in favour of reliability work, which removes the argument. SLOs should be based on user-visible signals rather than internal CPU metrics.

    992. How do you run a useful post-incident review?

    Focus on contributing factors and detection gaps rather than individual blame, and produce a small number of owned, dated actions. Include the timeline, the customer impact, and what made recovery slow. Track the actions to completion, otherwise the same incident recurs.

    993. What is the difference between horizontal and vertical scaling limits for a .NET API?

    Vertical scaling adds resources to one instance and hits a hard ceiling with rising cost, while horizontal scaling adds instances and requires statelessness. Anything held in process memory, such as sessions or local caches, breaks horizontal scaling. Data-tier scaling is usually the real bottleneck.

    994. How do you migrate session state out of a single server?

    Move to a distributed cache or, better, a stateless design using tokens or signed cookies so no server holds session data. Sticky sessions are a stopgap that complicates deployments and failover. Encrypt and keep session payloads small, since every request pays for the round trip.

    995. How do you decide whether to use a monorepo or multiple repositories?

    A monorepo simplifies atomic cross-service changes and shared tooling, but needs build graph optimization to stay fast. Multiple repos enforce boundaries and independent release cadence at the cost of coordinated versioning. The deciding factor is usually how often changes span boundaries, not repository size.

    996. How do you manage shared library versioning across many services?

    Keep shared libraries small and focused on cross-cutting concerns, version them semantically, and publish packages from CI. Consumers should upgrade deliberately rather than float on a wildcard version. Too much shared code turns into a distributed monolith with lockstep releases.

    997. What is the danger of a shared internal framework package?

    It centralizes decisions that teams should own, and a breaking change forces every service to upgrade together. It also tends to accumulate unrelated helpers until no one understands it. Prefer small composable packages or templates over a single internal framework.

    Building blocks 14 questions

    Copy-usable .NET recipes: controller and service skeleton, minimal API, DbContext configuration, DI registration, middleware, exception handling, JWT, policies, OpenAPI, keyset pagination, health checks, HttpClient, background service, integration test.

    998. How do I keep a controller thin by separating services and DTOs from the API layer?

    Keep the controller responsible only for HTTP concerns: read the request, call an application service, and return a result. Map entities to DTOs inside the service so persistence models never leak to the client.

    public record ProductDto(int Id, string Name, decimal Price);

public interface IProductService
{
    Task<ProductDto?> GetAsync(int id, CancellationToken ct);
}

public sealed class ProductService(AppDbContext db) : IProductService
{
    public async Task<ProductDto?> GetAsync(int id, CancellationToken ct) =>
        await db.Products.AsNoTracking()
            .Where(p => p.Id == id)
            .Select(p => new ProductDto(p.Id, p.Name, p.Price))
            .FirstOrDefaultAsync(ct);
}

[ApiController]
[Route("api/products")]
public sealed class ProductsController(IProductService service) : ControllerBase
{
    [HttpGet("{id:int}")]
    public async Task<ActionResult<ProductDto>> Get(int id, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        var dto = await service.GetAsync(id, ct);
        return dto is null ? NotFound() : Ok(dto);
    }
}

    999. How do I build a minimal API endpoint with validation and TypedResults?

    Validate the model explicitly and return TypedResults so the handler has a strongly typed result and accurate OpenAPI metadata. Map validation failures to ValidationProblem instead of throwing.

    public record CreateOrderRequest(string Customer, int Quantity);

public static class OrderEndpoints
{
    public static RouteGroupBuilder MapOrders(this IEndpointRouteBuilder app)
    {
        var group = app.MapGroup("/orders");

        group.MapPost("/", async (CreateOrderRequest request, IOrderService service, CancellationToken ct) =>
        {
            if (string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(request.Customer) || request.Quantity <= 0)
            {
                return Results.ValidationProblem(new Dictionary<string, string[]>
                {
                    ["customer"] = ["Customer is required."],
                    ["quantity"] = ["Quantity must be greater than zero."]
                });
            }

            var id = await service.CreateAsync(request, ct);
            return TypedResults.Created($"/orders/{id}", id);
        });

        return group;
    }
}

    1000. How do I configure EF Core entities with IEntityTypeConfiguration instead of a bloated DbContext?

    Move fluent configuration into one class per entity and let ApplyConfigurationsFromAssembly discover them. This keeps OnModelCreating to a single line and makes each mapping independently testable.

    public sealed class OrderConfiguration : IEntityTypeConfiguration<Order>
{
    public void Configure(EntityTypeBuilder<Order> builder)
    {
        builder.ToTable("Orders");
        builder.HasKey(o => o.Id);
        builder.Property(o => o.Customer).HasMaxLength(200).IsRequired();
        builder.Property(o => o.Total).HasPrecision(18, 2);
        builder.HasIndex(o => new { o.Customer, o.CreatedAt });
        builder.HasMany(o => o.Lines)
               .WithOne()
               .HasForeignKey(l => l.OrderId)
               .OnDelete(DeleteBehavior.Cascade);
    }
}

public sealed class AppDbContext(DbContextOptions<AppDbContext> options) : DbContext(options)
{
    public DbSet<Order> Orders => Set<Order>();

    protected override void OnModelCreating(ModelBuilder modelBuilder) =>
        modelBuilder.ApplyConfigurationsFromAssembly(typeof(AppDbContext).Assembly);
}

    1001. How do I split dependency injection registration into reusable extension methods?

    Group registrations by feature in static extension methods on IServiceCollection and compose them from Program. This keeps startup code readable and lets tests register the same slices.

    public static class OrderModule
{
    public static IServiceCollection AddOrderModule(this IServiceCollection services, IConfiguration config)
    {
        services.AddScoped<IOrderService, OrderService>();
        services.AddScoped<IOrderRepository, EfOrderRepository>();
        services.AddOptions<OrderOptions>()
                .Bind(config.GetSection("Orders"))
                .ValidateDataAnnotations()
                .ValidateOnStart();
        return services;
    }
}

public static class InfrastructureModule
{
    public static IServiceCollection AddInfrastructure(this IServiceCollection services, IConfiguration config)
    {
        services.AddDbContext<AppDbContext>(o => o.UseSqlServer(config.GetConnectionString("Default")));
        return services;
    }
}

// Program.cs
builder.Services.AddInfrastructure(builder.Configuration);
builder.Services.AddOrderModule(builder.Configuration);

    1002. How do I add a correlation id to every request with custom middleware?

    Read an incoming correlation header or generate a new id, store it in HttpContext.Items, and echo it back on the response. Register the middleware early so downstream logging picks the value up.

    public sealed class CorrelationIdMiddleware(RequestDelegate next)
{
    public const string HeaderName = "X-Correlation-Id";

    public async Task InvokeAsync(HttpContext context)
    {
        var correlationId = context.Request.Headers.TryGetValue(HeaderName, out var incoming)
            && !string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(incoming)
                ? incoming.ToString()
                : Guid.NewGuid().ToString("N");

        context.Items[HeaderName] = correlationId;
        context.Response.Headers[HeaderName] = correlationId;

        using (context.RequestServices.GetRequiredService<ILogger<CorrelationIdMiddleware>>()
                   .BeginScope(new Dictionary<string, object> { ["CorrelationId"] = correlationId }))
        {
            await next(context);
        }
    }
}

// Program.cs
app.UseMiddleware<CorrelationIdMiddleware>();

    1003. How do I return consistent ProblemDetails for unhandled exceptions using IExceptionHandler?

    Implement IExceptionHandler, map known exception types to status codes, and register it with AddExceptionHandler plus UseExceptionHandler. The framework then writes a ProblemDetails payload for you.

    public sealed class GlobalExceptionHandler(ILogger<GlobalExceptionHandler> logger) : IExceptionHandler
{
    public async ValueTask<bool> TryHandleAsync(HttpContext context, Exception exception, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        logger.LogError(exception, "Unhandled exception for {Path}", context.Request.Path);

        var (status, title) = exception switch
        {
            KeyNotFoundException => (StatusCodes.Status404NotFound, "Resource not found"),
            ArgumentException => (StatusCodes.Status400BadRequest, "Invalid request"),
            _ => (StatusCodes.Status500InternalServerError, "Unexpected error")
        };

        var problem = new ProblemDetails
        {
            Status = status,
            Title = title,
            Detail = context.TraceIdentifier
        };
        problem.Extensions["correlationId"] = context.Items["X-Correlation-Id"];

        context.Response.StatusCode = status;
        await context.Response.WriteAsJsonAsync(problem, ct);
        return true;
    }
}

// Program.cs
builder.Services.AddExceptionHandler<GlobalExceptionHandler>();
builder.Services.AddProblemDetails();
app.UseExceptionHandler();

    1004. How do I set up JWT bearer authentication for protected endpoints?

    Register the JWT bearer scheme with token validation parameters that mirror the issuer, then add authentication and authorization to the pipeline. Keep the signing key in configuration or a secret store, never in source.

    builder.Services.AddAuthentication(JwtBearerDefaults.AuthenticationScheme)
    .AddJwtBearer(options =>
    {
        options.TokenValidationParameters = new TokenValidationParameters
        {
            ValidateIssuer = true,
            ValidIssuer = builder.Configuration["Jwt:Issuer"],
            ValidateAudience = true,
            ValidAudience = builder.Configuration["Jwt:Audience"],
            ValidateLifetime = true,
            ValidateIssuerSigningKey = true,
            IssuerSigningKey = new SymmetricSecurityKey(
                Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes(builder.Configuration["Jwt:Key"]!)),
            ClockSkew = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30)
        };
    });

builder.Services.AddAuthorization();

var app = builder.Build();
app.UseAuthentication();
app.UseAuthorization();

app.MapGet("/me", (ClaimsPrincipal user) => user.Identity?.Name)
   .RequireAuthorization();

    1005. How do I implement policy-based authorization with a custom requirement and handler?

    Define a requirement class, write a handler that evaluates it against the user and resource, then register the policy with its requirement. Policies keep authorization rules out of controllers and make them unit-testable.

    public sealed record MinimumAgeRequirement(int Age) : IAuthorizationRequirement;

public sealed class MinimumAgeHandler : AuthorizationHandler<MinimumAgeRequirement>
{
    protected override Task HandleRequirementAsync(
        AuthorizationHandlerContext context, MinimumAgeRequirement requirement)
    {
        var claim = context.User.FindFirst("birthdate")?.Value;
        if (DateTime.TryParse(claim, out var birthdate) &&
            DateTime.Today.AddYears(-requirement.Age) >= birthdate)
        {
            context.Succeed(requirement);
        }

        return Task.CompletedTask;
    }
}

// Program.cs
builder.Services.AddSingleton<IAuthorizationHandler, MinimumAgeHandler>();
builder.Services.AddAuthorization(options =>
    options.AddPolicy("Adult", policy =>
        policy.RequireAuthenticatedUser().AddRequirements(new MinimumAgeRequirement(18))));

app.MapGet("/adults-only", () => "ok").RequireAuthorization("Adult");

    1006. How do I add OpenAPI metadata to both controllers and minimal API endpoints?

    Register OpenAPI generation, add annotations to endpoints, and use response attributes on controllers. Descriptive metadata improves generated clients and the interactive document.

    builder.Services.AddOpenApi();

var app = builder.Build();
app.MapOpenApi();

app.MapPost("/orders", async (CreateOrderRequest request, IOrderService service, CancellationToken ct) =>
    {
        var id = await service.CreateAsync(request, ct);
        return TypedResults.Created($"/orders/{id}", id);
    })
    .WithName("CreateOrder")
    .WithTags("Orders")
    .WithSummary("Creates a new order")
    .Produces<int>(StatusCodes.Status201Created)
    .ProducesValidationProblem();

[ApiController]
[Route("api/products")]
[Produces("application/json")]
public sealed class ProductsController(IProductService service) : ControllerBase
{
    [HttpGet("{id:int}")]
    [ProducesResponseType<ProductDto>(StatusCodes.Status200OK)]
    [ProducesResponseType<ProblemDetails>(StatusCodes.Status404NotFound)]
    public async Task<ActionResult<ProductDto>> Get(int id, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        var dto = await service.GetAsync(id, ct);
        return dto is null ? NotFound() : Ok(dto);
    }
}

    1007. How do I implement keyset pagination instead of offset paging?
